--- Page 5 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a theoretical discussion on the nature of beauty and the artist's duty to study nature. We now move to the formal title page of the work, printed in Nuremberg in 1528, the year of Dürer's death.</meta>

# Herein are contained four

-> Books of Human Proportion / devised and written <-
-> by Albrecht Dürer of Nuremberg / <-
-> for the benefit of all those who <-
-> have a love for this <term>art</term>. <note>original: "kunst." For Dürer and his contemporaries, "art" referred to a specialized body of knowledge and technical skill that could be taught and learned through geometry and observation.</note> <-

-> 1528. <note>original Latin: "M. D. XXVIII."</note> <-

---

<image-desc>The famous monogram of Albrecht Dürer, consisting of a large, stylized capital letter 'A' with a smaller capital 'D' positioned within the space between its legs. The monogram is rendered in bold black ink with prominent serifs.</image-desc>

<note>This monogram served as Dürer's personal trademark and a legal seal of authenticity for his woodcuts and engravings, which were widely pirated across Europe.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of Albrecht Dürer's definitive treatise on human anatomy and mathematical proportion, published posthumously in 1528. It marks a pivotal moment in Western art history where the intuitive practices of the workshop were codified into a scientific system.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Nuremberg, human proportion, Renaissance art theory, artistic anatomy, 1528</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication of Albrecht Dürer's manual on measurement. In the previous page, Dürer discussed the importance of training the "eye-measure" so that a practiced artist can work with "free certainty" without needing to measure every single stroke, provided they have a foundation in proportional truth.</meta>

<sig>A ij</sig>

# To the Honorable and well-esteemed Lord Wilibald Pirckheymer /
# Councilor to His Imperial Majesty etc. my favorable dear Lord /
# and greatly helpful friend, I offer
## Albrecht Dürer my willing service.

Although I, favorable Lord and friend, do not doubt that some will venture to criticize this undertaking of mine, because I—as an **unlearned** <note>original: "vngelerter"; in the 16th century, this specifically meant one who had not received a formal university education in Latin.</note> man of small understanding and gifted with little art—dare to write and teach that which I myself have never formally learned or been instructed in by anyone else; nevertheless, because you have repeatedly urged me, and in part even compelled me, to bring these books of mine to light, I have preferred to place myself in the danger of gossip than to refuse any request of yours. 

Although I hope that no one who is gifted with virtue and understanding will interpret it poorly that I bring to light—so generously and for the common benefit of all artists—that which I have acquired with high diligence, constant effort and labor, and not without a great loss of my own time; rather, everyone will praise my goodwill and inclined heart, and understand it in the best possible way. Since I have no doubt that I will do a favor to all art lovers and those who have a desire to learn, I must let envy (which leaves nothing unpunished) take its usual course, and answer that it is much easier to criticize a thing than to invent it oneself. 

And it would indeed be different if the books of the ancients, who wrote about the arts of painting, were still before our eyes; then this undertaking of mine might be misinterpreted as if I presumed to find something better. But since such books have been entirely lost through the length of time, it cannot with any fairness be held against me if I—just as the ancients did—release my opinions and inventions in writing, so that other intelligent people may be given cause to do the same, and our descendants may have something they can increase and improve, so that the art of painting may in time reach and return to its perfection once again. 

Yet no one is forced to follow this teaching of mine as if it were entirely perfect in all respects; for human nature has not yet so declined that another could not also invent something better. Therefore, anyone may use this instruction of mine as long as it pleases them, or until they invent something better; if not, they may well consider that this teaching was written not for them, but for others who desire to accept it. For it must be a very **brittle understanding** <note>original: "spröder verstand"; a mind that is stiff, unyielding, or lacks the creative spark to innovate.</note> that does not trust itself to invent something further, but always lies upon the old path, only follows others, and does not venture to think further. 

Therefore, it becomes every intelligent person to follow another in such a way that they do not despair of eventually inventing something better; for if that happens, there can be no doubt that this art may in time regain its perfection, just as it was in antiquity. For it is obvious that the **German painters** are not a little skilled with their hands and the use of colors, although they have until now lacked the **art of measurement** <term>measurement (Messung): geometry and the mathematical principles of proportion</term>, as well as **perspective** <note>original: "Perspectiua"</note> and other such things. Therefore, it is well to be hoped that if they also acquire these, and thus master both the practice and the art together, they will in time yield the prize to no other nation. But without right **proportion** no image can ever be perfect, even if it is made as diligently as is ever possible; although it is not necessary to make all—and especially very small—images according to the measurement, for such would require too much effort. But if one [knows] the measurement...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer dedicates his treatise to his friend Wilibald Pirckheymer, explaining that he is publishing his geometrical methods to help German artists achieve the same "perfection" once held by the ancients.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Wilibald Pirckheymer, art of measurement, perspective, proportion, German art, Renaissance theory, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s dedication to Wilibald Pirckheimer. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how the lost writings of ancient artists might have provided the "correct instruction" that Dürer now seeks to provide through his own work on measurement and proportion.</meta>

...is correct instruction, and brings it into habit, any figure can subsequently be made much more easily even without physical measurements. So that this instruction of mine may be better understood, I have previously released a book on measurement—namely concerning lines, planes, solids, etc.—without which this teaching of mine cannot be fundamentally understood. Therefore, it is necessary for anyone who wishes to undertake this art to first be well-instructed in measurement, and to gain an understanding of how all things should be laid out in a ground plan and drawn upward in elevation, just as the skillful <term>Stonemasons</term> <note>original: "Steinmetzen"; Dürer often links the high art of painting to the practical geometry used by master masons.</note> use in their daily practice; for without this, he will not be able to perfectly grasp my instruction.

No one should allow themselves to be turned away if they do not understand everything immediately; for what is very easy cannot be very artful. But that which is artful requires diligence, trouble, and labor until it can be acquired and learned. It is surely a vain labor when much effort and diligence is spent on a thing that is fundamentally wrong. However, if a work has its correct proportions, it can be criticized by no one, even if it is very simply made. With this instruction of mine, I intend to write only of the outer lines of the form and figures, and where they should be drawn from point to point, but not at all of internal matters. <note>Dürer refers here to the "outer lines" or contours of the human form, focusing on geometry rather than internal anatomy or the "inner spirit" of the subject.</note>

As to how old this art may be, who first invented it, and in what esteem and dignity it once stood among the Greeks and Romans—likewise how a good painter or <term>craftsman</term> <note>original: "werckman"; a general term for a skilled laborer or artisan.</note> should be prepared—I have no need to write of that now. Whoever desires to have knowledge of such things should read **Pliny** and **Vitruvius**, <note>original: "Plinium vnd Vitruuium"; Dürer points readers toward Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History* and Vitruvius’s *De architectura* as the primary authorities on classical art history.</note> and he will receive sufficient instruction on that account. 

However, so that these books of mine might find a protector against gossip, and so that I might demonstrate the inclined will I bear toward your **Lordship**—for the sake of the many kinds of dear friendship and goodness you have shown me for a long time and in many ways—with such spirit as I can offer through my work, I have dedicated them to your **Excellency**. I pray that you will understand this undertaking of mine in the best light and, as you have always done, remain my favorable Lord and patron. In this I will place my total trust, and I shall be diligent to deserve it as best I can.

-><image-desc>A decorative calligraphic monogram or printer's mark consisting of three interlocking circles with a central flourish that resembles a stylized knot or a double-looped compass rose.</image-desc><-

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, Dürer emphasizes that a foundation in geometry and measurement is essential for any artist or stonemason. He dedicates the work to his patron, Wilibald Pirckheimer, seeking protection from critics and expressing gratitude for their long-standing friendship.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, measurement, stonemasons, proportion, Pliny, Vitruvius, patronage, Wilibald Pirckheimer, artistic practice, ground plans, elevations</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the first book of Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion." It follows the dedication to Wilibald Pirckheimer, where Dürer emphasized that artistic skill must be grounded in the "correct instruction" of geometry and measurement. Here, he introduces his practical system for measuring the human body using a proportional ruler.</meta>

<sig>A iij</sig>

# Beginning of the First Book.

<margin>
Divider
Crown
</margin>

**F**irstly, when I undertake to make a human figure, I employ this method: I take a <term>straight-edge</term> <note>original: "richtscheÿent"; a wooden ruler or measuring stick used for drawing straight lines.</note> longer than the figure is; upon it I draw a straight line as long as the figure is meant to be, so that one end touches the highest point of the **crown** and the other the **soles**. And whether a figure be tall or short, I draw a separate line for each individual figure. Therefore, whenever I subsequently name a part by its number, I always take it from the total length of the figure between the crown and the soles. Thus, I diligently divide this length—which in its number is now "one" unit—into anywhere from two to fifty or a hundred parts, as many as I require. I mark these with points on the straight-edge next to the long line, and I align them all at the top with the height of the crown, and mark them all with their numbers, such as 2, 3, 4, etc. By this method, the smallest numbers produce the longer parts, and the largest numbers produce the shorter parts. <note>Dürer is using a fractional system: the number "2" represents 1/2 of the body's total height, while "10" represents 1/10. Thus, a higher number indicates a smaller fraction of the body.</note> For example, of the whole length, the half-part is 2, the third-part is 3, the fourth-part is 4, and so on, as mentioned above. And if I need to, I also divide each pre-determined part—whether it be long or short—into as many parts as I wish, even or odd; for whoever wishes to measure very precisely must make small parts if he cannot reach everything with a specific [larger] part. Therefore, I later combine two or three kinds of numbers, large or small, even or odd, however I can best achieve the result. These numbers may also be reversed, as I will show hereafter, and may be guided and used according to each person’s pleasure. But for better understanding, I will draw the **divider** <note>original: "teÿler"; here referring to a proportional scale or a set of lines used to divide the total height into fractions.</note> I have just described; for this divider must first be made correctly and with certainty. Otherwise, much time and effort would be wasted if I always had to search for the length of every named part within the full length between the crown and the soles.

<image-desc>A vertical diagram on the right side of the page representing a proportional scale (Teiler). It consists of a primary long vertical line spanning the height of the text block, labeled "Scheitel" (crown of the head) at the top and "Soln" (sole of the foot) at the bottom. To the right of this main line are ten shorter vertical lines of diminishing lengths, all starting at the top baseline. They are labeled with Arabic numerals 1 through 10 at their respective lower endpoints, demonstrating proportional divisions of the total height.</image-desc>

->How I measure the figures, however, follows hereafter.<-

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic mark or emblem positioned below the text, featuring two interlaced circles with a complex, swirling line or flourish woven through them.</image-desc>

<margin>Soles</margin>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer introduces his fundamental method for measuring human proportions using a "divider" or proportional scale based on the total height of the figure from crown to sole. He explains that all body parts are calculated as fractions (represented by whole numbers) of this total height to ensure accuracy regardless of the figure's size.</summary>

<keywords>human proportion, measurement, straight-edge, divider, fractions, crown of the head, soles of the feet, Albrecht Dürer, geometry</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Following the introduction of his fractional measuring stick (the "divider"), Dürer now applies this system to a specific body type. He moves from the theoretical construction of the ruler to the practical application of measuring a "peasant-like" figure, establishing the vertical coordinates for various anatomical landmarks.</meta>

Firstly, I take for my subject a thick, peasant-like man <note>original: "bewrischen man"; Dürer categorized his models by social type and physical build, often using the "peasant" as a shorthand for a sturdier, shorter proportion (here, seven heads tall) compared to more "noble" or slender figures.</note> who shall be seven of his own heads long. I draw a straight **horizontal line** <note>original: "zwerchlini"</note> for this purpose, and upon it I place three upright straight lines at the correct distance from one another, each as long as the figure is meant to be. I use the first for the side view of the man, the second for the front view, and the third for the back view. Once these lines stand parallel, I must first determine the length of the man’s **limbs** <note>original: "glidmaß"</note> between the **crown** <note>original: "scheitel"</note> and the **soles** <note>original: "soln"</note>. I then mark these lengths across the three upright lines with horizontal lines at right angles. These lengths of the limbs, as they are described with their numbers and ciphers, I set outside the figure with straight vertical lines so that they may be recognized immediately, and so that each length of the limbs can be found quickly. The most important of these horizontal lines will be the following, though sometimes there may be more or fewer. The first height is the **crown**; the next line below it I call the **forehead line**; the next after that the **eyebrow**; then the **nose**; the **chin**; and further the height of the **shoulder muscles**; the height of the **pit of the throat**; the height of the **breast**; the **front of the ears**; the **back of the ears**; the **nipples**; **under the breasts**; in the **flanks**; in the **navel**; the **top of the hip**; the **end of the hip**; the **end of the belly**; upon the **genitals**; the **end of the genitals**; the **end of the buttocks**; the **indentation** in the middle of the upper leg; then the **outside above the knee**; the **inside above the knee**; the **middle of the knee**; the **outside below the knee**; the **inside below the knee**; the **end of the outer calf**; the **end of the inner calf**; the height of the **arch** on the foot; the **end of the outer ankle** on the **shinbone**; and finally, at the very bottom, the **end of the soles**.

I will place such words at the ends of the vertical lines that indicate the length of the limbs, so that all this may be easily recognized. I intend to use this method for all the figures throughout this entire little book.

->Now I first measure the length of the limbs.<-

<margin>From the crown to</margin>
- the height of the **pit of the throat**: a 10th part and an 11th part. <note>Dürer means 1/10 + 1/11 of the total body height.</note>
- the height of the **shoulder**: two 11th parts.
- the **end of the chin**: a 7th part.

The height of the **vertex** <note>original: "wirbels"; the highest point of the skull.</note> at the back of the head is midway between the crown and the forehead.

From the chin upward to where the hair begins above the forehead is a 10th part; I divide that part into three equal fields: the uppermost for the **forehead**, the second for the **eyes and nose**, and the third for the **mouth and chin**.

<margin>From the pit of the throat downward to</margin>
- the height of the **breast**: a 30th part.
- **under the armpits**: a 13th part.
- the **nipples**: a 10th part.
- **under the breast**: an 8th part.
- the **flanks**: two 11th parts.
- the **navel**: a 40th part.
- the **top of the hip**: a 30th part.

<margin>From the height of the flanks downward to</margin>
- the **end of the hip**: a 10th part.
- the **genitals**: an 8th part.
- the **end of the same**: a 6th part.
- the **end of the buttocks**: a 10th part and an 11th part.

From the end of the buttocks to the **indentation of the leg**: an 18th part.
From the soles upward to the bottom of the **ankle** of the shinbone: a 28th part.
But from the soles upward to the highest point of the **instep**: a 20th part.

Now that I have measured the body of the figure by its length down to the end of the hip, I will subsequently place the **knee joint** in its location. This will give the figure three different unequal lengths: namely, the torso from the height of the pit of the throat to the end of the hip is the first and longest; the second, from the end of the hip to the middle of the knee, is shorter; the third, from the middle of the knee to the end of the shinbone, is the shortest of all; for the rear limbs shall 

<meta>catchword: shall</meta>

<summary>Dürer explains his method for mapping the proportions of a "thick peasant" figure using vertical and horizontal lines, providing a detailed list of fractional measurements for various anatomical landmarks from the head to the feet.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, peasant man, measurement system, anatomy, fractions, Albrecht Dürer, limb length, artistic geometry</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Following the description of the vertical coordinates for a sturdy, "peasant-like" man, Dürer now explains the geometric theory behind leg proportions. He introduces a specific tool—the "comparator"—to ensure that the torso, thigh, and shin maintain a consistent mathematical relationship to one another.</meta>

A iiij

...longer and stronger than the front ones <note>The "front" limbs here refer to the arms, as Dürer compares human anatomy to the four-legged animals discussed in earlier theoretical traditions.</note>, as is observed in humans. I treat the body here as one long part—although it is assembled from many pieces, it is yet a single unit—strong enough to move the other limbs. These three lengths shall be held in proportion to one another: just as the length of the body relates to the upper leg, so shall the length of the upper leg relate to the length of the **shinbone** <note>original: "schinbein"</note>. However, I do not use this for all figures; I will do it as follows:

I draw a **triangle** <note>original: "triangel"</note> *a.b.c.* / *a.b.* shall be the horizontal side, but *b.c.* the vertical one, so that *b.* remains a **right angle**. Then I divide the vertical side *b.c.* with two points into three equal fields, and draw two straight lines from the angle *a.* to the two points *d.* and *e.* From this, I seek the proportion as follows:

I take a **straightedge** <note>original: "richtscheit"</note> and mark upon it with two points the length of the body, measured between the **pit of the throat** <note>original: "halßgrůblein"; the small hollow at the base of the neck.</note> and the end of the **hip**. Likewise, below that, the end of the outer **ankle bone** <note>original: "knorren"; referring here to the lateral malleolus at the bottom of the shin.</note> at the bottom of the shin; I label the top point of the throat pit with an *f.* and the end of the hip with a *g.*, while the end of the shin is marked with an *h.* Afterward, I take this straightedge and place point *g.* on line *a.d.* of the aforementioned triangle, and I do not let this point leave that line; however, I slide the straightedge back and forth on the line until point *f.* at the top touches the triangle's line *a.c.* and the bottom point *h.* touches the horizontal line *a.b.* 

Once I have done this, the line *a.e.* intersects the straightedge between *g.* and *h.* and, together with the length of the body, creates three proportional parts; I mark this point with an *i.* Thus it follows: as the length *f.g.* relates to the length *g.i.*, so does the length *g.i.* relate to the length *i.h.* <note>In modern terms, Dürer is using the triangle to create a geometric progression where the ratio between the torso and thigh is identical to the ratio between the thigh and shin.</note> Thus, I mark at point *f.* the height of the throat pit, at point *g.* the end of the hip, at point *i.* the middle of the **knee** <note>original: "kny"</note>, and at point *h.* the end of the shinbone. It is also to be noted that when I place my straightedge in the triangle, I tilt the top at point *f.* toward the vertical side *c.b.* 

This triangle may well be called a **comparator** <note>original: "vergleicher"; a tool for creating equal ratios.</note>. This method may also be used in reverse if one wishes to invert something. Many other useful things are brought into the work through this method by those who can use it correctly. Now I set this height *i.* into the figure that I will draw hereafter, and place the knee at that same spot; then I measure further.

From the middle of the knee upward to the kneecap: a **21st part**.

<image-desc>A bracketed list of measurements indicating the vertical divisions of the leg from the knee downward.</image-desc>
From the middle of the knee downward:
{ a **40th part**, where I end the knee.
{ two **19th parts**, where I end the outer **calf** <note>original: "waden"</note>.
{ an **8th part**, where I end the inner calf.

And before I go further, I will draw this triangle, the **comparator**, here opposite.

<summary>Dürer describes a geometric method using a right-angled triangle (the "comparator") to determine the proportional lengths of the torso, thigh, and shin. He provides specific fractional measurements for the knee and calf muscles relative to the total body height.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, comparator, anatomy, knee measurements, calf, straightedge, ratio, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric exploration of human proportions. While the previous page established the "comparator" tool for the legs and torso, this section applies the same mathematical logic to the measurements of the arm.</meta>

->**The Comparator**<-

Afterward, I determine the length of the arm as follows: from the **shoulder joint** <note>original: "achsselglid"</note>, which is level with the **pit of the throat** <note>original: "halsgrübleins"; the small hollow at the base of the neck (suprasternal notch).</note>, to the **elbow** <note>original: "elnbogen"</note> is two 11th parts.
But from the shoulder joint to the end of the **shoulder muscle** <note>original: "achsselfleisch"; literally "shoulder flesh," referring to the deltoid muscle.</note> is one 10th part.
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers is one 4th part.
From the tips of the fingers backward to the end of the hand <note>Referring to the wrist joint.</note> is one 10th part.
However, whoever wishes to may make them one 9th part long.
The lengths of the **limbs** <note>original: "glidmaß"; used here to refer to the individual segments or proportions of the body.</note> are marked above.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer defines the mathematical proportions of the human arm, using fractions to calculate distances between the shoulder, elbow, and fingertips. This page demonstrates his method of using a geometric "comparator" to ensure anatomical consistency in art.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, arm proportions, Albrecht Dürer, geometry, Renaissance art theory, measurement, The Comparator, shoulder, elbow</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical breakdown of human proportions. Following the geometric projection of the tilted head on the previous page, he now provides specific fractional measurements for the "thickness" (depth in profile) and "breadth" (width from the front) of a man’s body parts, calculated as fractions of the total body height.</meta>

Now I will, following the first vertical line of the **side-view** <term>side-view (nebensichtig): a profile perspective</term> man, first indicate the thicknesses on all horizontal lines that are drawn through the length of the limbs.

First, at the top of the head: on the line closest to the crown, I make the head thick through the back of the head <note>original: "hindern wirbel", referring to the occipital bone</note> one 9th part.

Across the forehead, one 14th part and one 15th part.
Across the eyebrows, one 7th part.
Across the nose, one 8th part.
Across the chin and neck, also one 8th part.
And the neck under the head and chin, I make one 12th part thick.
At the pit of the neck, one 9th part.
At the height of the breast, two 13th parts.
At the **armpits** <term>armpits (ochssen): an archaic German term for the axilla</term>, one 6th part.
Across the **nipples** <term>nipples (tůttlein): literally "little teats" or breasts</term>, one 6th part.
Under the breasts, one 12th part and one 13th part.
At the flanks, one 12th part and one 13th part.
At the hip joint, one 6th part.
At the end of the hip, across the belly and buttocks, one 11th part and one 12th part.
At the genitals of the man, also one 11th part and one 12th part.
And the leg under the buttocks, I make one 7th part thick.
But below that, in the middle of the upper leg, one 14th part and one 15th part.
Above the knee, one 10th part.
In the middle of the knee, one 12th part.
Under the knee, also one 12th part.
In the middle of the calf, where it is thickest, one 20th part and one 21st part.
At the end of the inner calf, one 13th part.
At the bottom, above the instep of the foot where the leg is thinnest, one 18th part.
And I make the foot one 6th part long.
Next, I make the thickness of the **side-view** arm: at the shoulder, two 21st parts.
But under the armpit, one 13th part.
In the elbow, one 18th part.
In front of the elbow, in the thickness of the arm, also one 18th part.
And at the hand, one 32nd part.
And I make the hollow of the hand one 30th part thick.

And in the drawing, I will place the arms next

<column-break/>

to the figures separately, so that such things do not interfere with others.
Thereafter, I will, following the other vertical line of the **front-view** <term>front-view (fürsichtig): a perspective facing the viewer</term> man, place the breadths of the same parts on all horizontal lines according to the limb lengths.

The man, I make him in the head, on the line of the back of the head, one 10th part broad.
Across the forehead, one 8th part.
Across the eyebrows, one 9th part.
Across the ears, one 8th part.
Across the nose, one 10th part.
And the neck is broad under the chin, one 12th part.
Between the shoulder joints at the height of the pit of the neck, one 5th part.
Across the breast, three 10th parts.
Between the armpits, one 5th part.
Between the nipples, two 15th parts.
At the flanks, one 5th part.
At the hip joint, one 9th part and two 19th parts.
At the end of the hip, one 4th part.
And on this line, the legs stand one 6th part apart from each other at the hips.
At the genitals, also one 4th part.
But the leg at the end of the buttocks, two 17th parts.
At the **hollow** <term>hollow (einpeissung): the inward curvature or indentation of the inner thigh or leg</term> of the leg, one 10th part.

This hollow is to be observed in the front-view image on the inside of the legs.

Next, I make the leg broad above the knee, one 12th part.
In the middle of the knee, one 14th part.
Under the knee, two 26th parts.
In the middle of the calf, one 22nd part and one 24th part.
At the end of the inner calf, one 14th part.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer provides a precise catalog of measurements for the "thickness" (depth) and "breadth" (width) of the human body, using fractions of the total body height to define every part from the head to the feet.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anthropometry, Albrecht Dürer, side-view measurements, front-view measurements, anatomical fractions, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of fractional measurements for Dürer’s first standard human figure (designated as "A") and provides his philosophical advice on drawing from life rather than from pure imagination.</meta>

At the bottom of the **shin** <term>shin (schinbeyn): the tibia</term>, one 27th part.
Through the **ankles** <term>ankles (knorren): literally "knots" or "protuberances," referring here to the malleoli</term>, one 22nd part.
And the front of the foot, one 15th part.

Afterward, I determine the widths of the **front-facing** <term>front-facing (fürsichtig): viewed from the front</term> arm.

Under the armpit, one 18th part.
Behind the elbow, one 21st part.
In front of the elbow, one 16th part.
Behind the joint of the hand <note>original: "glenck der hand," referring to the wrist</note>, one 25th part.
And the open hand, one 15th part.

Next, by the third line, I make the **rear-facing** <term>rear-facing (hinderwertig): viewed from the back</term> man between the armpits <note>original: "achsen," here referring to the width across the shoulders/axilla</note> one 4th part wide. And I split the buttocks for him from the bottom upward one 8th part.
And I make his heels at the back one 24th part wide.

When I have now all lengths, thicknesses, and widths

<column-break/>

orderly described and marked by the three upright lines, then I draw the figure with lines according to my best judgment therein. Or, if I am able, I place a well-proportioned person before me and draw the lines after him; that is always better than if it were made from one’s own mind <note>original: "eignem sinn," implying drawing from imagination or memory without a model</note>.

It is also especially to be noted, when one draws the lines of the man’s form, how nature has made the man so masterfully, as if he were made of two pieces: the body set upon the thighs. On both sides, from the hip area, a **fleshy ridge** <term>fleshy ridge (wulst): a fold or muscular contour</term> goes down around the belly, and likewise backward over the buttocks. I will indicate this in the following draft with a special line, as it stands before your eyes hereafter in the side-view, front-facing, and rear-facing image.

This man I designate with an **A.**

<summary>This page lists the anatomical landmarks and corresponding mathematical measurements for the rear view of Model A, a standardized human figure. It provides the terminology for specific body parts—from the crown of the head to the soles of the feet—aligned with Dürer's fractional scaling system.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s systematic categorization of human proportions. Having previously established the measurements for more slender "noble" figures, he now shifts his focus to a sturdier, "peasant" female body type, using a scale of seven head-lengths rather than eight.</meta>

In the following, I wish to describe a strong, stout, and rustic woman <note>original: "betrisch weyb"; Dürer uses this term to categorize a shorter, wider body type common among the laboring classes</note>, seven heads in height, consistent with the previous man. I will do this through the same system as used for the man. However, instead of drawing three vertical lines, I will place points in those positions to mark the length of the limbs. I will no longer draw horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch liniẽ"</note> through these points—neither for the thickness nor the width of the profile <note>side view</note>, front, or back views. Instead, I will place the numbers and digits at the same level as the horizontal lines next to the points. These will indicate how thick or wide the side or front image should be at that specific place. I will use this method for all subsequent figures so that the drawing does not become too cluttered.

->Now I first measure the limbs according to their length.<-

From the crown of the head to the height of the shoulder joints: one 10th part and two 23rd parts. <note>Dürer’s "parts" are fractions of the total body height.</note>
But from the crown to the hollow of the throat <note>the suprasternal notch</note>: two 11th parts.
From the crown to the bottom of the chin: one 7th part.
From the chin upward to the height of the rear vertebra <note>likely the prominent C7 bone at the base of the neck</note>: one 8th part.
But from the chin upward to the end of the forehead: one 10th part.
I divide this tenth part into three equal sections: In the top one, I place the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; in the third, the mouth and chin. And under the chin, I make the flesh hang down further. <note>Dürer is describing the "double chin" or fuller neck area typical of this stouter body type.</note>

From the height of the hollow of the throat to:
{
The flanks <note>the waist area</note>: one 5th part.
Under the breasts: one 7th part.
Onto the nipples: one 9th part.
Under the front of the armpits <note>original: "vorder ochsen"</note>: one 15th part.
To the height of the breast: one 28th part.
}

The armpit is always lower in the back than in the front.
From the flanks to:
{
The navel: one 22nd part.
The end of the hip: one 9th part.
The end of the belly: one 8th part.
The end of the groin: two 11th parts.
The end of the buttocks: one 5th part.
}

From the sole of the foot upward to the height of the cleft <note>the crotch</note>: one 20th part.
But from the sole to the outer ankle bone <note>original: "knorren des schinbeins"</note>: one 28th part.
From the top of the ankle bone upward to the middle of the knee: two 9th parts.
Or, I set the knee using the leveler <note>original: "bergleicher"</note> as I did before, which makes the knee lower than previously; do whichever you prefer. From the middle of the knee upward: one 30th part; there I end the knee. Likewise, one 30th part below it; there I end the bottom of the knee. From the middle of the knee downward: one 8th part; there I end the calf. And I make the foot one 6th part long. After that, I make the arm—from the shoulder joint to the elbow: two 11th parts. From the elbow to the tips of the fingers: one 4th part.
From the tips of the fingers backward to the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>: one 10th part.

Now that I have indicated the length of the limbs, I will next measure the thickness at all the points of the previously established lengths on the profile <note>side-view</note> image.

<columns>2</columns>
I make the profile head, at the point of the rear vertebra, 9 parts thick.

Always pay close attention to the points so that you do not go astray.

But at the point of the forehead: one 14th part and one 15th part thick.

<column-break/>
Across the eyebrows: also one 14th part and one 15th part.

Across the head and nose: one 8th part.

Across the chin and neck: also one 8th part.

And the neck: one 12th part.

But at the hollow of the throat: one 10th part.

At the height of the breast: one 7th part.

<vocab>
<term>betrisch weyb</term>: Literally "peasant woman," used by Dürer to describe a robust, stocky female physique.
<term>Heupter sibner</term>: Seven heads tall; a proportional scale for shorter, sturdier figures.
<term>zwerchliniẽ</term>: Transverse or horizontal lines used for marking measurements.
<term>nebensichtiges bild</term>: A profile or side-view drawing.
<term>halßgrublein</term>: The "throat-pit" or suprasternal notch at the base of the neck.
<term>tütlein</term>: An archaic term for nipples or small breasts.
<term>achßelglider</term>: Shoulder joints.
<term>Albrecht Dürer</term>: The German Renaissance master (1471–1528) who authored this treatise on human proportions.
</vocab>

<summary>This page details the specific measurements for a "peasant woman" standing seven head-lengths tall, outlining the vertical proportions and the thickness of the head and neck in profile. Dürer introduces a more streamlined notation system using points and numbers instead of overlapping lines to keep his anatomical diagrams clear.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomy, female figure, peasant woman, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance measurement, anthropometry, artistic theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s detailed measurements for the sturdy "peasant-type" woman, who stands seven heads tall. At the armpits <note>original: "ochssen"; here referring to the depth of the chest at the axilla</note>, two 13th parts.
Over the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note>, one 12th part and one 13th part.
Under the breasts, one 7th part.
In the soft of the waist <note>original: "weychen"; the flank between the ribs and the hip</note>, one 7th part.
At the navel, one 10th part and one 11th part.
At the end of the hip, one 8th part and one 10th part.
And the leg under the buttocks, one 7th part.
Above the knee, two 10th parts.
In the middle of the knee, one 12th part.
Under the knee, also one 12th part.
In the middle of the calf, one 20th part and one 22nd part.
At the end of the calf, one 12th part.
The leg at the bottom, above the foot, one 18th part.
Next, I determine the thickness of the side of the arm:
In the shoulder, one 11th part.
Under the armpit, one 12th part.
At the elbow, one 20th part.
Before the elbow <note>at the forearm</note>, one 7th part.
At the hand <note>the wrist</note>, one 34th part.
And the hollow <note>cupped</note> hand, one 27th part.
Next, I determine the widths of the parts of the front-facing woman at all the points where the vertical lines stand.
The front-facing head, I make at the point of the height of the rear vertebra <note>the base of the skull/top of the neck</note> wide by one 18th part and one 19th part.
Across the forehead, one 15th part and one 16th part.
Across the eyebrows, one 9th part.
Across the ears, one 8th part.
Across the nose, one 10th part.
And the neck under the chin, one 12th part.
Across the shoulder flesh <note>the trapezius</note> at the hollow of the throat, one 5th part.
And below that, the shoulder joints stand apart from each other by two 11th parts.
And the shoulders, I make across the breast wide by four 15th parts.

<column-break/>

Between the armpits, one 6th part.
Between the nipples <note>original: "wertlein"</note>, one 8th part.
Under the breasts, one 10th part and one 11th part.
In the waist, one 5th part.
Across the navel, one 4th part.
At the end of the hip, one 7th part and one 8th part.
On this line, the hip joints stand apart from each other by two 11th parts.
And the leg at the end of the pubic area <note>original: "scham"</note>, I make wide by one 15th part and one 16th part.
Above the knee, two 21st parts.
In the middle of the knee, two 25th parts.
Under the knee, also two 25th parts.
In the middle of the calf, one 22nd part and one 24th part.
At the end of the calf, one 14th part.
The leg above the instep, one 26th part.
Through both ankle bones <note>original: "knorn"</note>, one 25th part.
But the foot, I make wide at the front by one 16th part.
And at the back, the heel, one 28th part.
I also make the woman from the back, between the armpits, wide by one 5th part.
And the buttocks, split upwards from the bottom, one 6th part.
The front-facing arm, I make from the armpit downwards wide by one 16th part.
At the elbow, one 19th part.
Before the elbow, in the width of the arm, one 15th part.
At the front, by the hand, one 27th part.
And the open hand, one 16th part.
Next, I draw within these marks <note>original: "gemerck"</note> the contour lines of the woman from the side, front, and back.
And one should pay very close attention to where the contour lines must be drawn narrower or wider than these marks, as I have sketched in the following pages.
And the sign for this woman shall be **A. j.**

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." Having previously established the measurements for a man, he now provides the specific mathematical ratios for a "proportionate woman," adhering to a height of eight heads.</meta>

Next, I will describe a proportionate woman to follow the previously described man; she shall also be eight head-lengths tall, and for this, I utilize the frontal arrangement <note>original: "vorder ordnung." This refers to Dürer's standardized system of measurement for the human figure seen from the front.</note>.

### I first measure the length of the limbs
From the top of the crown to the hollow of the throat is a 6th part <note>Dürer's "parts" are fractions of the total body height.</note>
Again, from the top of the crown to the end of the chin is an 8th part
From the end of the chin upward to the top of the forehead is a 10th part
I also divide this tenth part into three equal parts, as mentioned before, for the forehead, nose, and chin, to divide them equally
From the hollow of the throat to the flanks <note>original: "weychen." The soft part of the torso between the ribs and the hips.</note> I set a 5th part
To under the armpits a 16th part
To the breasts <note>original: "tutlein," a 16th-century diminutive for breasts.</note> an 11th part
To under the chest a 9th part
From the flanks to the end of the buttocks an 11th part and a 12th part
To the end of the pubic area <note>original: "scham."</note> a 13th part and a 14th part
To the end of the hip a 10th part
To the navel a 40th part
From the sole upward to the bottom of the ankle bone <note>original: "knorren des schinbeins." Literally the "knob" of the shinbone.</note> a 30th part
To the height of the crotch <note>original: "ritz." This term refers to the anatomical cleft or fold.</note> a 21st part
To the middle of the knee an 8th part and a 9th part
Or I set the knee in its place using the leveler <note>original: "bergleicher." A specialized proportional tool or standard used by Dürer.</note>.
From the middle of the knee upward a 26th part, where I end the knee
From the middle of the knee downward to the end of the calf I set an 8th part

### Next, I determine the length of the arm;
From the shoulder joint to the elbow is a 5th part
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers is a 4th part
From the tips of the fingers backward to the wrist joint is a 10th part

### Next, I determine the thickness of the woman in profile;
In the head, across the forehead, a 9th part.
Across the eyebrows a 16th part and a 17th part
Across the nose two 19th parts
Across the neck and chin an 11th part
And I make the neck under the chin a 17th part thick
At the hollow of the throat a 14th part
Across the chest a 9th part
Across the breasts an 8th part
Under the breasts a 9th part
At the flanks a 9th part
Across the navel an 8th part

<column-break/>

At the end of the hip a 6th part
And the leg under the buttocks two 17th parts
Above the knee a 14th part
In the middle of the knee a 16th part
In the middle of the calf a 14th part
At the end of the calf an 18th part
And the leg above the instep <note>original: "rist."</note> a 26th part
Through the ankle bones a 17th part
And I make this foot a 7th part long

### Next, I determine the thickness of the arm;
In the shoulder a 14th part
Under the armpit an 18th part
Behind the elbow a 26th part
Before the elbow a 24th part
At the wrist joint a 40th part
And the hollow hand a 36th part

### Next, I determine the widths of the front-facing woman thus;
The front-facing woman is two 19th parts wide at the point of the forehead
Across the eyebrows a 10th part
Across the nose a 12th part
And the neck under the chin a 17th part
Across the shoulder-flesh by the hollow of the throat a 7th part
Below that, the shoulder joints stand a 7th part wide from each other
But the width across the chest and shoulders is a 9th part and a 10th part
Between the armpits a 7th part
Between the breasts a 10th part
At the flanks a 7th part
Across the navel two 11th parts
At the end of the hip a 9th part and a 10th part
And on this line I place the legs in the hips a 7th part wide from each other, and I make the leg from the pubic area downward a 10th part wide
Above the knee a 15th part
In the middle of the knee an 18th part
In the middle of the calf a 16th part
At the end of the calf a 19th part
And the leg at the instep a 34th part
Through the ankle bones a 29th part
Under the ankle bone a 31st part
And I make the foot a 20th part wide at the front

### Next, I determine the front-facing arm
In the muscle <note>original: "meus." Referring to the bicep/upper arm muscle, often called the "mouse" in early anatomical German.</note> a 24th part wide
Behind the elbow a 28th part
Before the elbow a 22nd part

Here I alter the two previously made images as follows, and yet I let several parts remain as they were before, which I also name in the description; and I describe the figure in its entirety. From the sole upward to where the man is split <note>original: "gespalten", referring to the crotch or inseam</note>, let it be a 2nd part <note>Dürer uses "parts" as fractions of the total height; a "2nd part" means 1/2 of the total height.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). Having previously discussed the geometric manipulation of 3D forms, Dürer now transitions into the specific anatomical measurements used to construct or "modify" the female figure. He uses a fractional system where the body's total height is divided into units (parts).</meta>

Next, I will modify the woman and measure from the crown of the head to:
- the height of the **pit of the throat** <note>original: "halßgrübleins" — the suprasternal notch at the base of the neck</note>: two 11th parts.
- the height of the **shoulder muscle**: one 7th part.
- the end of the **chin**: one 8th part.

From the end of the chin to the height of the **rear vertebra** <note>original: "hindern wirbels" — likely the C7 vertebra or the nape</note>: one 9th part.
From the end of the chin to the end of the **forehead**: one 10th part.
I divide these ten parts as I have often described before, and I construct the face within them.

However, I also measure from the height of the pit of the throat to:
- under the **armpits** <note>original: "ochsen" — an archaic term for the armpits</note>: one 17th part.
- onto the **breasts**: one 11th part.
- under the **breast**: one 9th part.
- into the **flanks**: two 11th parts.
- into the **navel**: one 40th part.

From the flanks to:
- the end of the **hip**: one 10th part.
- the end of the **belly**: two 17th parts.
- the **woman's cleft**: one 7th part.
- the end of the **pubic area**: two 13th parts.
- the end of the **buttocks**: one 11th part and one 12th part.

Further, I measure upwards from the **sole of the foot** to the height of the **ankle joint** <note>original: "ritz" — the crease or joint where the foot meets the leg</note>: one 23rd part.
But from the sole upwards to the **bone of the shin** <note>original: "knozren des schinbeins" — the ankle bone or malleolus</note>: one 35th part.
From there to the middle of the **knee**: one 4th part.
Or, I set the knee in its place using the **divider** <note>original: "vergleicher" — a tool used by artists to transfer or compare proportions</note>.

However, I measure from the middle of the knee to:
- above the knee: one 25th part.
- under the knee: one 36th part.
- the end of the **outer calf**: one 11th part.
- the end of the **inner calf**: one 9th part.

I make the **foot** one 12th part and one 13th part long.
After that, I make the length of the **arm**:
From the **shoulder joint** to the **elbow**: two 11th parts.
From the elbow to the **end of the fingers**: one 4th part.
From the end of the fingers into the **wrist**: one 11th part.

Next, I modify the **thickness** <note>Dürer refers here to the depth of the body as seen from the side profile</note> of the woman according to the side:
I make the thickness through the **forehead** one 18th part and one 19th part.

Across the **eyebrows**: one 9th part.
Across the **head and nose**: one 10th part.
Across the **chin and neck**: one 11th part.
And the **neck** under the chin: one 18th part.
At the height of the **shoulder muscle**: one 16th part.
Across the **pit of the throat**: one 14th part.
Across the **chest**: one 18th part and one 19th part.
Across the **breasts**: one 16th part and one 17th part.
Under the **breasts**: one 18th part and one 19th part.
In the **flank**: one 9th part.
Across the **navel**: one 8th part.
At the end of the **hip**: one 6th part.
At the end of the **belly**: two 13th parts.
The **leg** under the buttocks: one 9th part.
Above the **knee**: one 13th part.

<column-break/>

In the middle of the **knee**: one 16th part.
Under the **knee**: one 17th part.
In the middle of the **calf**: one 15th part.
At the end of the **inner calf**: one 17th part.
But the **shin** above the ankle joint: one 26th part.
Through the **ankle joint** across the **ankle bones**: one 24th part.

I modify the thickness of the **arm from the side** thus: 
I make the thickness in the **shoulder** one 16th part.
Under the **armpit**: one 18th part.
In the **elbow**: one 26th part.
Before the **elbow**: one 24th part.
At the **wrist**: one 40th part.
And the **hollow of the hand**: one 36th part.

The front of the head across the forehead: one 19th part and one 20th part.
Across the ears: one 9th part.
Across the nose: one 12th part.
The neck under the chin: one 18th part.

At the end of the inner calf: one 18th part.

And the foot at the front, wide: one 18th part.
Thereafter, I modify the widths of the arm:
Under the armpit: one 22nd part.
Behind the elbow: one 29th part.
Before the elbow: one 22nd part.
At the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>: one 36th part.
And the open hand: one 20th part.
Then, from behind the woman, I make the width between the armpits: one 6th part.
And the buttocks, split from below <note>the gluteal cleft</note>: one 9th part.
And the heels at the back, wide: one 34th part.
Next, I draw the contour lines into this diagram, as it stands **drafted** <note>original: "auffgerissen" — a technical term for a scale drawing or architectural-style plan</note> below.

<insert>B.</insert>

# The Proportions of the Female Body

->[Diagram: Profile View, The Arm, and Frontal View]<-

This plate provides the visual measurements for the female form, utilizing Dürer’s system of <term>Proportions</term> <note>The mathematical relationship between the parts of the body and the whole</note>. The numbers represent either fractions of the total body height or specific integer units from Dürer's "measure-box" system.

<column-break/>

### The Profile View
On the left, the figure is shown in profile to illustrate the depth of the body.
*   The head and chest are measured at **one 18th and one 19th part** <note>original: "1/18 et 1/19"</note> of the total height.
*   The segments of the leg are marked with the integers **13, 16, 17, and 15**.
*   The base of the foot is measured as **one 12th and one 13th part** <note>original: "1/12 et 1/13"</note>.

<column-break/>

### The Measurement of the Arm
In the center, a detached left arm is shown from the side. This detail allows for a precise <term>Measurement</term> <note>original: Vermessung</note> of the limb's segments.
*   Vertical bars indicate lengths of **16, 18, 36, 24, 40, and 56** units.

<column-break/>

### The Frontal View
On the right, the figure is presented in a full frontal pose to show the widths of the body.
*   Across the forehead: **one 19th and one 20th part** <note>original: "1/20 et 1/19"</note>.
*   Across the chest: **two 9th parts** <note>original: "2/9"</note>.
*   At the pubic area: **one 20th and one 21st part** <note>original: "1/20 et 1/21"</note>.
*   The neck and waist widths are marked with the integer **7**.
*   The width of the arm is **22**.
*   The width across the base of the feet is **18**.

<column-break/>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the female proportions described previously to the measurements of a male figure. Dürer is introducing a "nine-head" canon—a taller, more slender proportion than the standard eight-head model. He continues to use his system of dividing the total body height into fractional parts.</meta>

Below I will describe a man who is nine of his own head-lengths tall; I will maintain the same order as with the previous figures, and also in the division of the length, it is much like the previous figures, and I measure him thus:

From the height of the crown to the end of the chin is a 9th part. <note>Dürer is defining the "Head" as 1/9th of the total body height.</note>
From the end of the chin upwards to the end of the forehead is a 10th part.
I also place the posterior whorl <note>original: "hindern wirbel"; the vertex of the head</note> on the man’s head at this same height.
And within this 10th part, I divide the face exactly as previously mentioned.
But from the crown to the height of the shoulder-flesh <note>The trapezius muscles.</note> I set a 15th part and a 16th part.
Again, from the crown to the height of the pit of the throat <note>original: "halsgrüblein"</note> is a 6th part.

From the height of the pit of the throat to:
- the height of the chest is a 28th part.
- under the armpits <note>original: "ochsen"; an archaic term for the axilla.</note> is a 14th part.
- onto the nipples <note>original: "tutlein"; Dürer's term for the pectoral points.</note> is a 12th part.
- under the breast is two 19th parts.
- in the waist <note>original: "weychē"; the soft part of the flanks.</note> is a 6th part.

From the waist to:
- the navel is a 26th part.
- the hip joint <note>original: "hufft art"</note> is a 22nd part.
- the end of the hip is a 9th part.
- the end of the belly is an 8th part.
- onto the genitals <note>original: "scham"</note> is a 7th part.
- to the end of the buttocks <note>original: "hindern"</note> is a 6th part.

From the end of the buttocks downwards to the inner indentation of the leg <note>The gluteal fold or the inner thigh crease.</note> is an 11th part.
From the soles upwards to the height of the instep <note>original: "ritz"; the crease of the foot.</note> is a 23rd part.
But from the soles upwards to the ankle bone <note>original: "knorren des schinbeins"</note> is a 35th part.
From the end of the ankle to the middle of the knee is a 4th part.
From the middle of the knee to the outside of the knee is a 20th part.
From the middle of the knee to the inside above the knee is a 30th part.

But from the middle of the knee to:
- the outside under the knee is an 18th part.
- the inside under the knee is a 10th part.
- the end of the outer calf is a 10th part.
- the end of the inner calf is a 9th part.

<column-break/>

And I make the foot two 13th parts long.
Next, I make the length of the arm from the shoulder joint to the elbow two 11th parts.
From the elbow to the end of the fingers is a 4th part.
From the end of the fingers backwards to the wrist joint is a 10th part.

Next, I determine the thickness <note>Width or depth measurements.</note> for the man seen in profile:
In the head, across the forehead, is a 12th part.
Across the eyebrows is a 9th part.
Across the nose and head is a 10th part.
Across the chin and neck is two 23rd parts.
And the neck under the chin is an 18th part.
At the height of the shoulder-flesh is a 17th part.
Across the pit of the throat is a 12th part.
At the height of the chest is an 8th part.
At the armpits is a 15th part and a 16th part.
Across the nipples is also an 8th part.
Under the breast is a 16th part and a 17th part.
In the waist is an 18th part and a 19th part.
At the navel is an 18th part and a 20th part.
At the hip joint is an 18th part and a 19th part.
At the end of the hip is a 15th part and a 16th part.
At the end of the belly across the buttocks is an 8th part.
At the genitals is a 16th part and a 17th part.
But the leg under the buttocks is a 10th part.
Down by the indentation is an 11th part.
On the outside above the knee is a 15th part.
On the inside above the knee is two 31st parts.
In the middle of the knee is an 18th part.
On the outside under the knee is a 19th part.
On the inside under the knee is an 18th part.
In the middle of the calf is a 15th part.
At the end of the outer calf is a 16th part.
At the end of the inner calf is an 18th part.
And the shinbone at its thinnest point at the bottom is a 28th part.
At the instep is a 24th part.

Next, I determine the thickness of the arm in profile <note>original: "sentlichē arms"; likely referring to the side view of the limb.</note> at the shoulder is a 15th part.
Under the armpit is a 20th part.
In the elbow is a 26th part.
In front of the elbow is a 25th part.
At the wrist joint is a 48th part.

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the "Type D" male figure, which Dürer has established as being nine "heads" tall. And the hollow of the hand is one 38th part.

Next, I establish the widths of the man seen from the front <note>original: "fürfichtigen" — literally "forward-looking," referring to the frontal view.</note>:
In the head, across the width of the forehead, one 11th part.
Further down, one 10th part.
Across the eyebrows, one 11th part.
Across the ears, one 18th part and one 19th part.
Across the nose, one 12th part.
And the neck under the chin, one 18th part.
At the height of the shoulder-flesh <note>The trapezius muscles.</note>, one 16th part.
Across the pit of the throat, one 6th part.
Below this, the shoulder joints <note>original: "achsselglider"</note> stand apart from one another by two 13th parts.
But across the chest and shoulders, I make the width two 9th parts.
Between the armpits <note>original: "ochssen"; an archaic term for the axilla.</note>, one 7th part.
Between the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note>, one 9th part.
At the waist <note>original: "weichen"; the soft area of the flanks.</note>, one 7th part.
Across the navel, one 12th part and two 25th parts.
At the hip joint, one 12th part and one 13th part.
At the end of the hip, one 10th part and one 12th part.
On this line, the hip joints stand apart from one another by one 15th part and one 16th part.
And I make the leg, below the buttocks, one 12th part wide.
Down at the indentation <note>original: "einpeyssung"; likely referring to the curve of the inner thigh.</note>, one 14th part.
Outside above the knee, one 18th part.
Inside above the knee, one 19th part.
At the middle of the knee, one 21st part.
Outside under the knee, also one 21st part.
Inside under the knee, one 20th part.
At the middle of the calf, one 17th part.
At the end of the outer calf, one 19th part.
At the end of the inner calf, one 21st part.

<column-break/>

And the leg, where it is thinnest at the bottom, one 42nd part.
Through the ankles <note>original: "knorren"; the bony protrusions of the ankle joint.</note>, one 33rd part.
And I make the foot one 19th part wide.

Next, I establish the widths of the arm seen from the front:
Under the armpit, one 26th part.
Behind the elbow, one 31st part.
In front of the elbow, one 22nd part.
At the wrist joint, one 38th part.
And the open hand, one 19th part.

Next, I make the man, from the back, one 6th part wide between the armpits.
And the heels <note>original: "farschen"</note>, one 35th part.
And the buttocks, split from the bottom upwards, one 11th part.

Once I have marked all these things with points and indicated them with numbers, then I draw the figure of the man within them with lines, as it is then sketched out <note>original: "auffgerissen"</note> hereafter. This man’s mark is a D.

If I wish, however, to make the man’s head higher than it was before, I take an 8th part of the length between the crown and the sole, and place this length upwards from the chin, and raise the head with it; and I leave all other things as they were before, except for the whorl on the back of the head, which I place as high as the head was before. And on the head seen from the side <note>original: "nebensichtigen"; the profile view.</note>, I draw the forehead further forward above the eyebrows; in this way, this man becomes approximately eight of his own heads long, as it has been sketched out.

### Proportions of the Profile and Arm

### The Frontal View and The Arm

The detached study of the arm provides the following segment lengths:
The upper arm, 15 and 20 parts.

The fingers, 48 parts.
The total length of the hanging arm is two 11th parts of the total body height.

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical manual on human proportions, moving from the geometric "squaring" of the torso seen on the previous page to a detailed list of measurements for a female figure calculated at a total height of nine heads.</meta>

Next, I wish to show a female figure of balanced proportions according to the measurements just described, also nine heads in length. Following the previous order, I shall first measure the length.

From the height of the **crown** <note>the very top of the head</note> to the end of the **chin**: one 9th part.
From the end of the **chin** upward to the end of the **forehead**: one 10th part.
This length of the face I divide into three equal parts as mentioned before; but from the **crown** to the height of the **shoulder muscles** <note>original: *schulterfleisch*; refers to the trapezius and upper shoulder area</note>: one 15th part and one 16th part.
Again, from the **crown** to the height of the **hollow of the throat**: one 6th part.
From the height of the **hollow of the throat** to the top of the **breast**: one 30th part.
To below the **armpits** <note>original: *ochsen*; an archaic term for the axilla</note>: one 16th part.
To the **nipples**: one 11th part.
To below the **breasts**: one 9th part.
To the **waist** <note>original: *weychen*; the soft part of the torso between the ribs and hips</note>: two 11th parts.
From the **waist** to the **navel**: one 26th part.
To the end of the **hip**: one 10th part.
To the end of the **belly**: two 17th parts.
To the **crotch** of the woman: one 7th part.
To the end of the **pudenda** <note>original: *scham*</note>: one 12th part and one 13th part.
To the end of the **buttocks**: one 11th part and one 12th part.
From the end of the **buttocks** downward in the **upper leg**: one 12th part.
From the **sole** upward to the height of the **instep** on the foot: one 26th part.
But from the **sole** upward to the bottom of the **ankle bone**: one 42nd part.
From there upward to the middle of the **knee**: one 4th part.
From the middle of the **knee** to above the knee: one 26th part.
From the middle of the **knee** to below the knee: one 30th part.
To the end of the **outer calf**: one 11th part.
To the end of the **inner calf**: one 9th part.
And the **foot** I make one 7th part long.
Next, I make the length of the **arm** from the height of the **shoulder** to the **elbow**: two 11th parts.

<column-break/>

From the **elbow** to the end of the **fingers**: one 4th part.
From the end of the **fingers** to the **joint of the hand** <note>the wrist</note>: one 11th part.

Next, I determine the **thickness** of the female figure in **profile**.
In the **head** across the **forehead**: one 12th part.
Across the **eyebrows**: one 10th part.
Across the **head and nose**: one 11th part.
Across the **neck and chin**: one 13th part.
And the **neck** below the chin: one 21st part.
At the height of the **shoulder muscles**: one 19th part.
Across the **hollow of the throat**: one 14th part.
At the height of the **breasts**: one 10th part.
At the **armpits**: two 19th parts.
Across the **nipples**: one 18th part and one 19th part.
Below the **breasts**: two 19th parts.
At the **waist**: two 21st parts.
Across the **navel**: one 9th part.
At the end of the **hip**: one 7th part.
At the end of the **belly** across the **buttocks**: one 14th part and one 16th part.
And the **leg** below the buttocks: one 10th part.
Down in the **thigh**: two 21st parts.
Above the **knee**: one 15th part.
In the middle of the **knee**: one 18th part.
Below the **knee**: one 19th part.
In the middle of the **calf**: one 16th part.
At the end of the **inner calf**: one 19th part.
Down in the **shin**: one 28th part.
In the **instep** through the **ankle**: one 26th part.

Next, I determine the **thickness** of the **arm** in **profile**.
At the **shoulder**: one 18th part.
Below the **armpit**: one 20th part.
At the **elbow**: one 39th part.
Before the **elbow**: one 26th part.
At the **joint of the hand**: one 49th part.
And the **hollow of the hand** <note>the palm</note>: one 40th part.

Next, I determine the **widths** of the **front-facing** female figure.
At the end of the **forehead**: one 11th part.
In the middle across the **forehead**: one 10th part.
Across the **eyebrows**: one 11th part.
Across the **ears**: one 10th part.
Across the **nose**: one 13th part.
And the width of the **neck and the chin**: one 21st part.

At the height of the **shoulder muscles** <note>original: *schulterfleysch*</note>: one 19th part.
Across the **hollow of the throat**: two 13th parts.
Under this line, the **shoulder joints** stand apart from each other by one 7th part.
The width across the **breast**: one 5th part.
Between the **armpits** <note>original: *ochssen*; Dürer uses this archaic term for the axilla</note>: one 8th part.
Between the **nipples** <note>original: *tutlein*</note>: one 11th part.
Under the **breasts**: one 15th part and one 16th part.
In the **waist** <note>original: *weichen*; the soft part of the torso between the ribs and hips</note>: one 8th part.
Across the **navel**: one 11th part and one 12th part.
At the end of the **hip**: one 10th part and two 21st parts.
On this line, the legs stand apart at the **hips**: one 15th part and one 16th part.
At the end of the **belly**: one 10th part and two 21st parts.
And the leg under the **buttocks** <note>original: *hindern*</note>, I make two 21st parts wide.
Further down: two 25th parts.
Above the **knee**: one 17th part.
In the middle of the **knee**: one 19th part.
Below the **knee**: one 20th part.
In the middle of the **calf**: one 17th part.
At the end of the **inner calf**: one 20th part.

<column-break/>

At the bottom of the **shinbone**: one 39th part.
In the **instep** <note>original: *rist*</note> through the **ankles** <note>original: *knorren*; the bony protrusions of the ankle joint</note>: one 31st part.
And the **foot** is one 20th part wide at the front.
After that, I determine the widths of the **forward-facing arm**.
Under the **armpit**: one 26th part.
Behind the **elbow**: one 33rd part.
In front of the **elbow**: one 23rd part.
At the **wrist** <note>original: *gelenck der hand*</note>: one 40th part.
And the **open hand**: one 22nd part.
And at the back, make the woman one 7th part wide between the **armpits**.
And the **buttocks** are divided from below by one 10th part.
And at the back, make the **heels** one 37th part wide.
Afterward, I draw the figure of the woman with lines at all the points and numbers I have recorded, just as I have **plotted** <note>original: *aufgerissen*; Dürer's term for technical drafting or sketching</note> it hereafter.
The woman’s mark is **D. i.**
And if I wish to construct the woman’s **head**, I proceed in the same manner as I described and **plotted** for the man just previously.

Now I want to make a long, thin man / and his height shall be ten of his heads long, and I will use the previously described method <note>Dürer refers to his system of using fractions of the total body height to determine proportions.</note> / and first measure the length of the limbs.
From the height of the crown, I set to the end of the chin one 10th part.
From there upwards to the end of the forehead, one 11th part.
In this length, I make the face as mentioned before.
But from the height of the crown to the height of the shoulder flesh <note>The trapezius muscles where they meet the neck.</note>, two 17th parts.
To the pit of the throat, one 13th part / and one 14th part.
To the height of the shoulder, one 6th part.
From the height of the shoulder to the height of the breasts, one 25th part.
To under the armpits, one 17th part.
To the nipples <note>original: "tütlein," an archaic term for the teats or nipples.</note>, one 13th part.
To under the breast, two 21st parts.
To the flanks <term>flanks: the soft area of the side between the ribs and the hip bone</term>, two 13th parts.
From the flanks to the navel, one 30th part.
To the hip joint, one 21st part.
To the end of the hip, one 8th part.
To the genitals, one 14th part / and one 15th part.
To the end of the same, two 13th parts.
To the end of the buttocks, two 11th parts.
From the end of the buttocks downwards to the indentation /
inside the leg, one 11th part.
Between the end of the buttocks and the sole of the foot, I here place the knee in the middle.
From the sole upwards, one 35th part; there I end the ankle bone <note>original: "knorn," referring to the bony protrusion of the malleolus</note> at the bottom of the shinbone.
But from the sole upwards, one 26th part / I set the height of the instep on the foot.
From the middle of the knee upwards, one 30th part; there I end the top of the knee.
But from the middle of the knee downwards, one 40th part; there I end the bottom of the knee.
Again, from the middle of the knee downwards, on the outside one 10th part / and on the inside one 9th part; there I end the calves of the leg on both sides.
And I make the foot one 7th part long.
Afterwards, I make the length of the arm /
From the shoulder joint to the elbow joint, two 11th parts.
But before the elbow, I wish here to set two measurements / first, one 4th part to the end of the fingers /
From there backwards to the wrist joint, one 11th part.
Or from the elbow joint to

<column-break/>

the end of the hand, three 11th parts.
And from the end of the fingers backwards to the wrist joint, two 21st parts.
Afterwards, I determine the thickness of the man in profile <note>original: "seyntlichen mans," meaning the figure viewed from the side</note> at all the points with which the lengths of the limbs are marked.
I make the man, according to the side-view, in the head at the point of the forehead, one 13th part thick.
Across the eyebrows, one 11th part.
Across the nose and head, one 11th part.
Across the mouth and neck, one 13th part.
Across the chin and neck, one 14th part.
And the neck under the chin, one 22nd part.
At the height of the shoulder flesh, one 20th part.
Across the pit of the throat, one 13th part.
At the height of the shoulders, one 11th part.
At the height of the breasts, two 17th parts.
At the armpits, one 8th part.
Across the nipples also one 8th part.
Under the breasts, two 17th parts.
In the flanks, one 11th part.
Across the navel also one 11th part.
At the hip joint, one 10th part.
At the end of the hip, one 17th part / and one 18th part.
Upon the genitals, one 9th part.

And the buttocks split from below, one 11th part.
And the heels at the back, one 37th part wide.

Afterward, I draw the contour lines of this man according to these marked numbers, just as I have drafted it below.
However, if I wish to make the man's head taller, I place one 9th part with one end at the point of the chin and let the other end extend above the head, making the head that much taller than it was before. It follows then that the head must be drawn thicker and wider. Yet, I leave the entire face with all its features as it was before, as shown in the following drafts with lines for the side, front, and back views. But the man is then no longer ten heads long, but becomes instead nearly nine heads long.

This man’s mark is an **E.**

Following the man described previously, I will now show a proportional woman <note>Dürer uses the term "weyb," which in 16th-century German simply meant "woman," though today it carries a more archaic or blunt tone.</note> in the same order as mentioned before. First, I will establish the points for the length of the body, and measure within those the length of the limbs.

From the crown of the head to the end of the chin: a 1/10 part.
From the end of the chin upwards to the edge of the forehead: a 1/12 part.
In these twelve parts, I divide the face as described before.
And from the crown of the head to the end of the jaw <note>original: "tröllens," referring to the jawline or the area under the chin.</note> and the chin: an 1/18 part and a 1/19 part.
