<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing human proportions, moving from the general measurements of the female figure on the previous page to the mechanical posing of figures and a detailed coordinate system for the male head. It is part of Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion."</meta>

These previously described figures I bend at the joints through the spine <note>original: "ruckgrad"</note> and in all other limbs, as everyone should already know; but so that an understanding of it is shown here, I want to place the spine in the rear fourth of the body's thickness for both the aforementioned figures when seen from the side, and mark it with small triangles. Likewise, for the front-facing figures, indicate some of those same joints with small rings. And although I have measured all the figures here before to the same length from a divided scale for the sake of less effort, yet if one wanted to put them into a work and arrange the figures together, the thinner figures should always be made longer than the thick ones.

And when the man and the woman of one type are arranged together, the line of the woman from which she is measured shall be taken an 18th part shorter than the man's line is; otherwise, the woman would seem larger than the man, because her naked flesh should be drawn fleshier and softer <note>original: "mölleter"</note> than the man's, but the man of a harder constitution <note>original: "geprech"</note> and less fleshy.

This wide difference of the previously made measurements of the figures, from the strongest to the weakest, I have set down for this reason: so that everyone may take cause from it, through which ways he may change and distinguish his undertaking as he pleases, as I will further show in the following little books.

As I have shown four kinds of differences in the figures here before, I will subsequently describe and draw a man more clearly than before, but use the previous order. First, make the head seen from the side in a **square** <term>vierung<gloss>a square or quadrate used as a geometric frame for proportion</gloss></term>, a height of one-eighth of the man's length and just as wide.

But the measure for this head I will take in the drawing from a longer line than that in which the aforementioned figures are described, because of a greater necessity to show the small parts more clearly.

When I set this square, I mark the front upright side of the plan where the nose shall stand out with an **a.** and the rear upright side with **b.** But the upper transverse side with a **c.** and the lower with a **d.** Thereafter I divide the parts in the face; first according to the length with horizontal lines <term>zwerchlinien<gloss>horizontal or cross-lines</gloss></term> all parallel, and set for each its letter; these show the high and low parts of the face.

To the first, I draw a horizontal line **e.** above the side **d.** a 10th part high, as I mentioned before in the man of eight heads' length. This line separates the hair in front and the bareness of the forehead; but the rear crown of the head <note>original: "wirbel"</note> I set a third higher between **c.** and **e.** Thereafter I divide **e.** **d.** with two lines **f.** **g.** into three equal fields. The upper line **f.** shall touch the eyebrows and the top of the ears, but the line **g.** the bottom of the nose, ears, and the head has its indentation at the back in that same place on the neck, as was partly shown before. Thereafter I divide **e.** **f.** with two points into three equal fields, and draw through the lower point with a horizontal line **h.** This line touches above the eyebrows the masculine bulge of the forehead; from there the forehead rounds itself upward to the beginning of the hair. But if I divide **f.** **g.** with two points into three equal fields, I cut off the upper third with a horizontal line **j.** <note>original: "J."</note> so that between **f.** **j.** the whole eye is contained, and the two corners of the eyes end at the back and front in the middle between **f.** **j.** But if I divide **f.** **g.** with three points into four equal fields, I cut off the lowest fourth with a horizontal line **k.** This line touches the top of the nostrils <term>naspellen<gloss>an archaic term for the wings of the nose or nostrils</gloss></term>; also the lower earlobe <term>ozipffellen<gloss>referring to the lobe of the ear</gloss></term> is contained between **k.** **g.** Thereafter I divide **g.** **d.** with a line **l.** into two equal fields; this line touches the top of the chin, as the **d.** does the bottom. But if I divide **g.** **l.** with a line **m.** in two, this line goes through the middle of the mouth. The upper lip I make in the lower third between **g.** **m.** but the upper

<summary>This page details the mechanical posing of proportion figures using geometric markers and explains the aesthetic differences between male and female builds. It introduces a precise alphabetical grid system for mapping the features of the human head within a square frame.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomy, geometry, facial grid, male vs female proportions, artistic theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drawing the human head, specifically focusing on the geometric construction of the profile (side view). It follows the previous discussion of the general proportions of the male figure, now applying a grid system to map facial features like the nose, lips, and chin.</meta>

Two-thirds I leave for the philtrum <note>original: "rynlein" – the vertical groove between the base of the nose and the upper lip</note> under the nose. I place the lower lip in the upper half-part between **l** and **m**, but in the lower half-part, I place the hollow indentation under the lip above the chin. Thus, with these horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerchlinien"</note>, the most prominent parts of the high and low points of the head are indicated.

Furthermore, I wish to indicate the depth in the face with vertical parallel lines; and by "depth," understand what recedes from the front where the nose stands out.

First, I divide the two vertical sides **a.b.** with six vertical lines **n.o.p.q.r.s** into seven equal fields. The first line **n** touches the front of the eyeball and the corners of the mouth. But I end the back corners of the eyes in the middle between the vertical lines **n** and **o**, for these things should be understood as doubled on both sides in practice. After that, I draw a diagonal line <note>original: "ort lini"</note> from the corner formed by the horizontal side **c** and vertical **n** into the corner of the two lines **a** and **f**; there I place a **t**. After that, I draw another diagonal line, but from the aforementioned corner **c.n.** into the corner formed by the vertical **a** and the horizontal **g**; there I place a **v**.

But the line **n.t.** shall touch the forehead with its form, and above the line **n.v.** I draw the shape <note>original: "possen"</note> of the nose. And where the horizontal line **e** intersects the diagonal line **n.t.**, there I mark the beginning of the hair above the bare forehead. After that, I draw a vertical line midway between the two verticals **a** and **n** and between the horizontals **d** and **g**; this touches the front of the upper lip of the mouth and the front of the chin. After that, I divide **n.r.** between the horizontals **m** and **d** with two vertical lines into three equal fields; the front-most touches the front of the lower lip, but the rear touches the end of the hollow indentation between the lower lip and above the chin. I draw the eyebrows nearly to the line **o**. I also raise them in their middle a little above the horizontal **f**. And between the two verticals **q** and **r** and between the horizontals **f** and **g**, I enclose the entire ear; however, I draw the lower earlobe in the front half-part between **q** and **r** and between the two horizontals **f** and **g**. I place the rear whorl of the hair on the head between the verticals **s** and **b** and horizontals **c** and **e**, at the third-part toward the vertical **s** and also a third-part above the horizontal **e**. On the horizontal **g** between the verticals **s** and **b**, at the nearest quarter-part toward **s**, I set the head upon the neck. After that, I draw the contour lines of the side-view face within and near these intersecting lines, so that the top of the head touches the horizontal side **c** between the verticals **p** and **q**. Likewise, the rear vertical side **b** between the horizontals **h** and **f**, from there down to the neck, and the neck itself all the way down through the square <note>original: "vierung"</note>.

After that, I draw the head down the front to the forehead, and then the forehead, nose, mouth, and chin; the nose touches the vertical side **a** between the horizontals **f** and **g**. Thus, the entire head is enclosed in this square, touching all four sides. But from the chin, I draw the jawbone backward and upward to behind the ear. After that, I draw the eyes and brows, and likewise the ears in their place. I also draw the bareness of the face where the hair begins, and proceed from the front whorl of the forehead over the horizontal line **e**, often between the two verticals **p** and **q**, back down to the front of the mid-ear. After that, I draw the front of the neck under the horizontal **d** downward behind the vertical line **o**, a quarter-part toward the vertical **p**. If I wish, however, I make the neck a 14th part thick, or two 27th parts. Whoever wishes to use these things should pay close attention to the curved contour lines, how they are drawn in and out near the straight lines; for whoever does not take heed of this will quickly fall into deformity <note>original: "vngestalt"</note>, yet the cross-lines in the square never let things run entirely out of proportion.

Now that I have finished the side-view head <note>original: "nebensichtig haupt"</note>, I will proceed to make the front-view <note>original: "fursichtig"</note> in the other square, which will be of equal height with the first, but narrower.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details a geometric grid system for drawing the human head in profile, using horizontal and vertical coordinates to place features like the philtrum, lips, chin, and ears. He emphasizes that following these cross-lines prevents anatomical deformity and prepares the reader for the subsequent frontal view.</summary>
<keywords>proportions, Albrecht Dürer, human head, profile view, grid system, geometry, facial features, anatomy, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting the human head. Having established the side profile on the previous page, the author now explains how to project those measurements forward to create a perfectly proportional frontal view and the back of the head using a grid system.</meta>

First, I draw all the horizontal parallel lines <note>original: "zwerch parallell linien"</note> of the first square of the side-facing view <note>original: "nebensichtigen angesichts" — refers to the profile</note> forward, out from the square as far as I need them, in the same order as the letters stand from **c** to **d**. These lines in the forward-facing view <note>original: "fursichtigen angesicht" — the frontal view</note> divide all high and low points exactly the same as in the side view; after that, I draw vertical lines through these horizontal lines, which will indicate all the widths of the parts in the forward-facing view.

First, I draw two vertical lines **a** and **b** on both sides through the previously drawn horizontal lines, and I set them a 10th part away from each other; after that, I divide the space between the two sides **a** and **b** into five equal fields with four vertical parallel lines, **c, d, e, f**. In the middle field **d. e.**, I place the crown <note>original: "scheytel"</note> in the center between the horizontal lines **c** and **e**. After that, I place the nose in the middle, so that it touches with its nostrils <note>original: "naspellen"</note> **d** and **e** between the horizontal lines **k** and **g**. I also let the two corners of the mouth touch the vertical lines **d** and **e** on the horizontal line **m**, and the chin remains at the bottom, also between the vertical lines **d** and **e**. After that, I set the two eyes so that they touch the four vertical lines **c, d, e, f** with their four corners, between the horizontal lines **f** and **J**.

After that, I divide the vertical spaces **a. c.** and **f. b.** in the middle from each other with two vertical lines **g** and **h**. These two lines **g** and **h** touch the height of the cheeks on each side at the horizontal line **k**, and the eyebrow ends on both sides in the middle between the vertical lines **g. c.** and **f. h.** under the horizontal line **f**. Furthermore, I divide **a. g.** and **h. b.** in the middle from each other with two vertical lines **i** and **k**. These two vertical lines separate the bare forehead from the hair on both sides between the horizontal lines **e** and **f**, but the head exceeds these two lines on both sides so that half the ears are covered between the horizontal lines **f** and **g**. I also draw the two ears on both sides between **a. i.** and **k. b.** between the horizontal lines **f** and **g**. But above the horizontal line **f**, they exceed the two vertical sides **a** and **b** and approach the two vertical lines **i** and **k** at the bottom with their earlobes <note>original: "zipffellen" — literally 'little tips'</note>.

Now that the vertical lines are drawn through the horizontal lines—which indicate all widths, heights, and lows in the forward-facing view—I then draw the form lines of the face into this division. First, the head from the top, where it touches the horizontal line **c** in the middle between the two vertical lines **d** and **e**; from there, rounding out on both sides to the vertical lines **a** and **b** at the horizontal line **h**; from there down to the ears at the horizontal line **f**, midway between **a. i.** and **k. b.** After that, I draw the bare forehead and the jawbones through the two vertical lines **g** and **h** on the horizontal line **m** all the way down to the chin; and from the aforementioned ends, I draw the neck down from both sides onto the horizontal line **d**, where I make it a 15th part wide, or a little wider. After that, I draw the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin into it, along with the primary features that the face requires.

Once that is finished, I make the third square in there, the back of the head <note>original: "hinderwertige haubt"</note>, according to the outer lines of the forward-facing view, and I set the back crown <note>original: "wirbel" — the whorl or vertex of the head</note> in its place, and draw the rounding of the head onto the neck.

If I wish to lay out this above-described head in a ground plan, I do this through a triangle <note>original: "triangel"</note> which I will show hereafter, so that I may change one given thing into another, yet bring each one forth proportionally <note>original: "gleichförmig"</note>. I designate this triangle with **1, 2, 3**; it should always have a right angle so that it may be used on all sides and however one needs it. It can have equal or unequal sides, long or short. In it, I take whichever side I want for myself and mark it with points; but here I set approximately **4, 5, 6** etc. From these, I draw parallel lines toward one side of the triangle to the third side; thus they divide that same side just as the first is divided. If I then go back from the newly formed points with parallel lines, as before, toward the undivided side, it will also be divided proportionally, just like the first and the second.

<summary>This page explains how to construct a frontal view of a human head by projecting horizontal lines from a profile view onto a vertical grid. It also introduces a geometric method using a right-angled triangle to scale and transfer these proportions accurately.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, frontal view, technical drawing, grid system, facial features, geometry, scaling triangle, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page follows the instructions for drafting the human head in frontal and profile views. Having established the primary squares for these views, Dürer now introduces the "transfer" method—a geometric technique for projecting these two-dimensional measurements into a three-dimensional "ground plan" or foreshortened perspective.</meta>

E ij

From this figure, many different things can be made, and for my purpose, it may well be called a **transfer** <term>vbertrag: a method of projecting dimensions from one view to another to create a new perspective</term>; because through this way one may carry and pull each thing into another. But before I press the head down into the ground <note>referring to a top-down, foreshortened view or ground plan</note>, I will first sketch this transfer here below.

---

<note>A technical geometric diagram from Albrecht Dürer's manual on measurement, showing a right-angled triangle with internal grid lines and projections, used to demonstrate perspective transfer (vbertrag). The diagram consists of a right-angled triangle with a grid of horizontal and vertical lines, and several diagonal lines originating from the hypotenuse toward the right angle. It is labeled with numbers 1, 4, 5, 6, 2 along the top and 3 at the bottom vertex. The words "The transfer" (original: "Der vbertrag") are printed inside the triangle.</note>

---

And so I use the transfer for this. I set the side-view <note>original: "nebensichtig"</note> and the front-view <note>original: "fürwertig"</note> head before me, as described in their squares <term>vierung: the grid or "squaring" used to measure the proportions of the face</term>, and first draw all vertical lines—**a. n. o. p. q. r. s.**—straight downward from the square of the side-view head as far as I need them. Afterward, I also draw all vertical parallel lines—**a. i. g. c. d. e. f. h. k. b.**—from the other square of the forward-facing countenance straight downward as previously mentioned.

Next, I draw a straight horizontal <term>zwerch: horizontal or crosswise</term> parallel line beneath the two squares of the head, through all the vertical lines that were drawn down from above. Where this horizontal line intersects the two vertical sides **a. b.** of the forward-facing view, I place **1** at line **a** and **2** at line **b**. Then I measure downward from the number **1** on line **a** the width of the square of the forward-facing view; there I place **3** and connect **2** and **3** with a diagonal line <term>ortlini: a diagonal or "edge" line used for geometric projection</term>. Thus, this triangle for the "pressed-down" ground plan <note>nidergedruckten grund: a plan view seen from above</note> is finished, which I call the transfer.

Afterward, where this diagonal line **2. 3.** intersects the vertical lines drawn down from the forward-facing head, I proceed straight across horizontally with parallel lines through all the vertical lines drawn down from the side-view head. Where the horizontal line **2. 1.** and the horizontal line **3** intersect the two vertical sides **a. b.** of the side-view, I place **4. 5.** at the front and **6. 7.** at the back of the corners of that square. This creates an oblong square <note>a rectangle</note> in which the head is contained in the "pressed-down" ground plan.

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric "transfer" method, using a triangular diagram to project measurements from frontal and profile views into a top-down ground plan.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, geometry, Albrecht Dürer, transfer method, vbertrag, ground plan, human proportion, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s explanation of how to use a geometric "transfer" to project facial features from standard front and profile views into a "plan view," which is a top-down perspective looking down onto the top of the head.</meta>

Now that the squares **4. 5. 6. 7.** have been drawn through with the help of the **transfer** <term>vbertrag: a method of projecting dimensions from one view to another to create a new perspective</term> using the vertical lines of both the side-view and the front-facing countenance, then the small squares indicate where each section of the face, with its characteristic lines, should be placed. For each square contains its own part, such as the eyes, nose, mouth, chin, ears, neck, and all lines belonging to the form, just as I have drawn them hereafter with lines. I have drafted <term>aufgerissen: sketched or plotted out</term> everything precisely—first the head in profile <note>original: "nebensichtig"</note>, then the front view <note>original: "fürwertig"</note>, the back view <note>original: "hinderwertig"</note>, and finally in the **plan view** <term>nidergedruckten grund: literally a "pressed-down ground," referring to a top-down or bird's-eye perspective</term>.

It is useful to know that if this head were made of wax, and at whichever horizontal line <term>zwerchlini</term> it was cut through, one could know exactly what shape that specific plane <note>original: "planus"</note> would have; this is found through this method. If I wish, however, to make the head seven parts high—whereas it is eight parts high in the front, as I mentioned earlier regarding the figures—I still leave all other parts of the face as they were before, only I heighten and thicken the head on the side from the forehead upwards, and then backwards down to the neck, between the horizontal lines **g.** and **m.**; however, I make the neck two 27th parts thick. 

Afterward, I widen the front-facing head in order, so that it becomes approximately two 17th parts wider across the forehead, and I pull it back in on both sides above the ears toward the two vertical sides **a. b.**, and also heighten and expand the bare parts of the forehead, and pull the ears further from the head, as will be shown in the following draft. It is to be noted that if a figure is correctly divided into seven of its head-lengths, it is not to be despised.

<note>A complex technical diagram by Albrecht Dürer showing the geometrical projection and proportional analysis of a human head from profile, frontal, and plan views using a grid system (nidergedruckten grund). This illustration from Dürer's foundational 1528 treatise on human proportion is a masterpiece of Renaissance scientific art, demonstrating advanced descriptive geometry and anatomical study.</note>

In the same way that I have placed the head into a **plan view**, one may also place a whole figure—whether it stands straight or crooked—into a plan view.

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Dürer shifts from the geometric projection of the human head to the specific proportions and construction of the hand, following his established system of using "parts" of the body’s total height as a base measurement.</meta>

<note>Decorative woodcut initial 'F' with foliate patterns.</note> Furthermore, I will describe the outstretched hand of a strong man, as it was shown previously; and just as I previously made it one-tenth [of the body's total height] long, I shall let it remain so. I set this length of one-tenth before me as a vertical line, marking it at the top with **a.** and at the bottom with **b.**, and I enclose it between two parallel horizontal lines <term>zwerchlini: a horizontal or cross-line</term> **c.** and **d.** After that, I divide the line **a. b.** with 18 points into 19 equal fields <term>gleyche felder: equal units of measurement in Dürer's grid system</term>. 

Then, I draw another vertical line between the horizontals **c.** and **d.**, parallel to the vertical **a. b.**, far enough away so that the hand I intend to make remains unobstructed. Let this line be **e.** at the top and **f.** at the bottom; I place this in the middle of the width of the outstretched hand, so that it passes through the middle finger <note>original: "lange singer," literally the long finger</note>. Here I draw the hand upright so that the tip of the longest finger touches the horizontal line **c.**, but at the bottom, I let the line **d.** pass through the middle of the wrist joint.

Next, I draw a horizontal line **g.** from point **11.** <note>The text says 'i' in the OCR, but context suggests point 1 of the 19 units, or perhaps an error in the original; following the logic of the grid, these points correlate to specific phalanx lengths</note> which touches the end of the ring finger <term>golt finger: literally "gold finger," the traditional name for the finger wearing a wedding ring</term>. Then I place a point on the line **a. b.** midway between **1.** and **2.**, from which I draw a line **h.**; this touches the end of the index finger <note>original: "zeyger finger"</note>. Then I draw a horizontal line **j.** from point **4.**, which touches the top of the last part of the little finger. From point **7.**, I draw a horizontal line **k.**, which touches the end of the thumb.

Once I have indicated the ends of the fingers, I mark their hindmost joints <term>hinderste glyd: the proximal phalanges, where the fingers meet the palm</term> where they sit upon the hand. I draw a horizontal line **l.** from point **11.**; this passes through the two hindmost joints of the index and middle fingers, for they stand at the same height next to each other in the front of the hand. Thus, the middle finger becomes longer than the entire rest of the hand. Where the line **l.** intersects the vertical **e. f.**, I place an **m.** 

Afterward, I set a compass with one foot on point **f.** and the other on point **m.**; from there, I draw a curve backward out of the hand as far as I need. Within this curved stroke, I place the two hindmost joints of the ring finger and the little finger, for they sit deeper in the hand than the front two. Then I draw a horizontal line **n.** from point **14.**, which goes through the middle of the thumb's hindmost joint. Now that the length of the hand and all the fingers are indicated, I will further show the joints of the fingers. I draw a horizontal line **o.** from point **10.**, which goes through the middle of the second joint of the thumb. 

Next, I divide the index finger lengthwise between **h.** and **l.** with a horizontal line in the middle; this goes through the middle of its second joint. Then I divide the foremost half-part lengthwise with 6 points into 7 equal fields, and from point **3.** I draw a horizontal line which goes through the middle of the foremost joint <note>the distal phalanx</note> of the aforementioned finger. Next, I draw a horizontal line **p.** from point **6.**, which goes through the second joint of the middle finger. Then I divide the space between the horizontals **c.** and **p.** with 6 points into 7 equal fields, and from that same point **3.** I draw a horizontal line that goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the middle finger. The horizontal line **k.** goes through the middle of the second joint of the ring finger. Then I divide the foremost length with 8 points into nine equal fields, and from that same point **4.** I draw a horizontal line that goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the aforementioned finger.

I make the little finger as long as the two back parts of the middle finger are, or let it remain as it has occurred before. I divide this length with 10 points into 11 equal fields and draw a horizontal line from point **6.**; this goes through the middle of the second joint of the little finger. After that, I divide the 6 upper parts anew with ten points into eleven equal fields, and from the newly created point **5.** I again draw a horizontal line which goes through the middle of the foremost joint of the aforementioned finger. Let everyone pay close attention to the numbers taken from between **a. b.** or from the other smaller parts.

I make the nails on the fingers each half as long as the part of the joint on which they sit. Next to the middle finger on both sides, the split between the fingers goes to the halfway point of the hand's length; but between the ring and little finger, the split goes deeper...

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Dürer concludes his discussion on the depth of the thumb cleft before moving into a rigorous mathematical analysis of the hand's width and thickness relative to his nineteen-unit scale.</meta>

...into the hand; however, the cleft between the thumb goes even deeper into the hand.

Now that I have described the length of the parts in the hand, I will then indicate the thickness and width of the same parts. And although I previously described their thickness next to the side views of the figures, it did not fit well here with the hand; therefore, I will now describe the outstretched hand according to its width, and only then its thickness from the side.

I make the flat hand at the wrist joint on the **horizontal line d.** <term>zwerchlini: a horizontal or transverse line used in Dürer’s geometric grid</term> seven-19ths wide relative to the length **a. b.** But across the width **n.**, it is eleven-19ths; for the hand is widest at the **thumb joint** <term>knorren: literally a "knot" or protuberance, referring here to the knuckle or the bony ball of the thumb</term>. And the other width from the outside near the index finger on the horizontal **l.**, through the hindmost joints of the fingers to the end of the circular curve—outside at the end of the little finger—I make the hand as wide as the index finger is long.

I make the longest finger at the very back one-fifth wide of its own length, and at the front, one-quarter narrower than it is at the back. I also make the index finger at the back just as wide as the long finger, but at the front, I make it a little narrower than the long finger. The ring finger <note>original: "gold finger," literally the "gold finger," the traditional name for the finger that wears a wedding ring</note> and the smallest finger I make each one-fifth wide of its own length, and make each one-quarter narrower at the front than it is at the back. I make the thumb at its middle joint one-third wide of its own length.

The fingers stand at the back of the hand, each in the middle of its width; there I will draw small circles for them in the sketch.

It is to be noted that in the hand, no finger has a form like any other. Let everyone observe this in people, and he will find it to be so. I also observe that when the hand is outstretched, it does not stand entirely straight against itself but shifts outward near the little finger. After that, I determine the thickness of the hand from the side: at the wrist joint of the arm, five-19ths of the vertical length **a. b.** But through the knuckle of the thumb on the horizontal line **n.**, it is half as thick as the flat hand is wide. Likewise, further down in the palm <note>original: "pallen," referring to the thenar eminence or the "ball" of the hand</note>, it is also that thick; but the hand forms a hollow, because the two sides—the thumb and the little finger—stand inward; therefore, the hand is not as thick as it appears or as the outer lines indicate.

The thumb in its foremost joint is just as thick as it is long at the front part of the little finger; but further toward the front, it is one-quarter thinner, yet thick enough at the back toward the knuckle. The fingers are roughly as thick as they are wide according to their measurements, though it may vary a little in the joints.

Now that I have recorded all the lengths, widths, and thicknesses of the hand, I then draw the shape of the hand with lines, as I have sketched it hereafter. Whoever replicates this should take care that he misleads nothing <note>Dürer is warning the reader that even small errors in these precise ratios will distort the realism of the drawing</note>, for these lines are difficult to draw correctly.

<note>This technical diagram illustrates a human hand from both frontal and profile views. It features precise measurement scales labeled a-b, lettered coordinates such as c, n, l, and d, and proportional ratios like 11/19 and 5/19. This illustration is a foundational piece of Albrecht Dürer's work on human proportion, demonstrating how to construct the body using rigorous mathematical principles rather than just visual estimation.</note>

<summary>Dürer provides precise mathematical instructions for drawing the human hand, defining its widths and thicknesses using a system of nineteen parts. He emphasizes that the hand is not a flat plane but has a natural hollow and that each finger possesses unique proportions.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the proportions of the hand, Dürer now shifts his focus to the lower extremity, specifically detailing the geometric construction of a strong man’s foot to ensure proper scale relative to the rest of the body.</meta>

<note>A decorative initial letter 'F' is adorned with floral motifs, typical of the Renaissance style used in technical manuals.</note>

Henceforth, I will describe a strong man’s foot more clearly than before, just as I mentioned previously regarding the man who is made eight head-lengths tall, whose foot I described as one-sixth <note>original: "6. teyl"</note> of his total length long and one-seventeenth wide.

From this length and width, I draw an **elongated rectangle** <term>vierung: a quadrangle or rectangle, which Dürer uses as the bounding box for his anatomical drawings</term> and place it horizontally <note>original: "vber zwerch"</note> before me on a flat surface. Into this, I draw the foot so that it touches both sides, front and back, as will follow. When I draw the foot with its shaped lines, I mark the farther long side with an **a.** and the one closer to me with a **b.** The two short sides I mark as follows: the front, where I want to draw the toes, with a **c.** and the back with a **d.** In this elongated rectangle, I draw the foot so that it lies completely flat on a pressed-down ground.

Thereafter, I first divide this rectangle along its length between **c.** and **d.** with two lines, **e.** and **f.**, into three equal fields. The line **e.** stands at the front; whatever letters I mention first shall be placed at the front so that no error arises between the parts. It should be noted that although the letters are placed among one another, the aforementioned ones should always be placed first, as stated above. In the front field, between **c.** and **e.**, I will place the **toes**. In the middle field, between **e.** and **f.**, I will make the **bare foot** <note>the midfoot or arch area</note>. In the third field at the back, I will place the **heel** and the **bone above the foot** <note>the ankle and lower leg</note>.

Afterward, I divide the line **d.** with six points—**g. h. i. k. l. m.**—into seven equal fields. From point **g.**, I draw a parallel line to the frontmost line **c.** Where it touches that line, I place an **n.**; where it intersects the vertical line **f.**, I place an **o.** Next, I divide the space between **l.** and **m.** with a point **p.** in the middle. From this point **p.**, I draw a parallel horizontal line up to line **f.**; at that point, I place a **q.** Then I draw a slanted line <term>schleme lini: a diagonal or "slanted" line used to define the tapering or curves of the anatomy</term> **k. q.**

Thereafter, I place a point **r.** on line **c.** midway between **a.** and **b.** Then I divide the segment **r. b.** with two points, **S.** and **T.**, into three equal fields. I also divide **b. e.** with two points, **v.** and **x.**, into three equal fields. Then I again divide **e. f.** with two lines, **y.** and **z.**, into three equal fields. Furthermore, I divide **d.** with two points, **st.** and **ß.**, into three equal fields. Where line **g.** intersects line **e.**, I place a **tz.** Then I draw a horizontal line from point **h.** to line **f.**; where it touches, I place an **a. i.** Afterward, I divide **r. S.** with two points, **1.** and **2.**, into three equal fields. I also divide **r. c.** with two points, **1.** and **2.**, into three equal fields. Then I divide **ß. d.** with four points—**1. 2. 3. 4.**—into five equal fields. From point **3.**, I draw a line to the horizontal **h. a. i.** and place a **b. i.** there. It should also be noted that I have placed the letters for the short lines all on the horizontal **a.** From these, I draw several slanted lines: one **e. n.**, another **r. tz.** Then another from point **2.** (between **r.** and **S.**) to point **y.** Then one **t. l.** and one **b. o.**, followed by **b. a. i.**

Next, I draw a straight line from point **st.** to the slanted line **k. q.** and place an **e. i.** there. Then I set a compass <term>circkel: a pair of dividers or a compass used for precise geometric measurement</term> with one foot on point **tz.** and, with the other foot, scribe <note>original: "reyß," meaning to draw or score a line</note> from point **n.** out from the foot to the slanted line **r. tz.** Keeping the one foot of the compass stationary, I draw a circular stroke between the slanted lines **tz.** and **b. o.** Then, keeping one foot of the compass still at point **tz.**, I close it slightly and set the other foot at point **2.** on line **b.** (between **x.** and **e.**) and scribe from there forward to the slanted line **b. o.** Still keeping the compass foot at point **tz.**, I set the other foot at point **r.** and scribe from there to the slanted line **t. z.**

Now that I have indicated with slanted and circular lines where I will place the toes and where they are cut off at the front, I will henceforth place their hindmost joints. I place a point on line **e.** midway between the slanted lines **b. o.** and **b. a. i.** At this point, I will place the hindmost joint of one of the smallest toes. I then set a compass with one foot at point **3.** and the other at the point just made; from there, I scribe a curve to the horizontal line **g. n.**...

Here I place the hindmost joint of the big toe; afterward, I place two points upon this circular stroke, centered between the slanted lines <note>original: "schlemen"</note> **s. n. r. z.** and **b. o.** In that position, I set the two hindmost joints of the middle toes. I will sketch these according to their size, marking them with elongated rings. The hindmost joint of the little toe I place with a point on line **e.**, centered between the horizontal line **b.** and the slanted lines **b.** and **a. i.** The big toe has two joints, and I make the rear part slightly longer than the front.

I divide each of the three middle toes into three joints, so that the hindmost part is as long as the two front parts combined. However, each frontmost part of these middle toes is one-quarter longer than the second part behind it—not because of the internal bone, but because of the flesh they have at the tip. For the smallest toe, I create a strong "hind-ball" <note>the fleshy padding or ball of the toe</note> at its hindmost joint and let it end at the front at line **r.** I make its hindmost part longer than the front ones. I make every nail on all toes half as long as the segment upon which it stands. Afterward, I place one foot of a compass on point **n.** and the other foot slightly above the point of the hindmost joint of the little toe on line **e.** From there, I draw a curve through the slanted lines, and where this curve intersects the slanted lines, I draw short lines from those points as a marker. This is how far I split the toes into the flesh, although on the top of the foot I draw the veins and hollows much further back. Line **f.** touches the front of the instep, from where the bone pulls upward; but beneath the tread, the cut extends two-thirds past line **f.** toward **z.**

Furthermore, I divide **b. d.** with four points into three equal fields, and from point **z.** I draw a straight line that touches the tendons <note>original: "spann'adern"</note> at the back, above the heel.

Once I have drawn all the straight, slanted, and curved lines through which the foot is entirely proportioned <term>gepossiert: modeled or laid out in a preliminary geometric form</term>, I then draw the "shape line" <note>the final contour or outline</note> into it, showing how it should lie upon the pressed-down ground, beginning at the front.

First, I draw the big toe with its ball coming forward from point **e.**, outside the slanted line **e. n.** until almost the middle of the back part of the toe; then I move in a curve under the slanted line **e. n.** and draw the shape line in front of the foremost joint back to the slanted line **e. n.** From there, I draw the toe completely forward to the circular stroke midway between **n. r.** From there, I draw it backward as far as it is split, up to the line **r. ts.** Next, I draw the three middle toes between their slanted lines so that their fronts touch the circular strokes. Regarding this, it should be noted that the big toe should not stand pointing straight out, as is found among many coarse people <note>original: "groben menschen"; Dürer implies that straight toes are a sign of a lack of refinement or physical labor</note>. For this reason, I have drawn the slanted lines so that the toes are positioned outwards toward the little toe, one more than the other, for the sake of strength and aesthetic beauty. Therefore, I leave a space between the largest and the next toe, as the middle three toes should stand more outward. But the smallest toe I draw inward, toward the other toes of the slanted line **b. a. i.**; I end it on the outside at line **e.**, two-thirds from its hindmost joint between line **b.** I also draw the foremost toe the largest, but those following are always smaller than the one before. Herein I show how the knuckles <term>knözlein: small bones or joints</term> and other parts should have their shape, each one different from the rest. Afterward, I draw the outside of the foot from the ball of the little toe into the angle **b. y.**

Then I draw the inner line of the foot from point **e.**, curved specifically toward line **y.**, midway between the long side **a.** and the line **n. g.**, and continue drawing it backward, curving inward until line **f.**, midway between **f. o.** From there, I go further back and round the heel to line **d.** at point **h.** From there, I draw the heel...

...just as it has an unusual curved line extending to the slanted lines <note>original: "schlemen," referring to the diagonal grid lines used in Dürer’s construction system</note> **k. q.** However, along line **s. t.**, I draw a curve into the foot, but from point **q.**, I finish the drawing to the ball behind the little toe at point **y. b.** I mark the soles on the inside with special small points on line **s. t.** Through the section in the instep, across both ankles <note>original: "knorren," the bony protrusions of the tibia and fibula at the ankle joint</note> of the shinbone, I make the width a 26th part; but below the ankles, I make it a 31st part, and I extend the section forward along line **f.** two-thirds toward line **z.** I also draw the inner ankle flatter and the outer one more pointed; likewise, I place the inner one further forward and the outer one further back. In this way, the hollow next to the tendon <note>original: "spanaderen," referring to the extensor tendons or the prominent saphenous vein area</note> behind the inner ankle becomes longer and flatter than the outer one. Afterward, I draw a specific ball on the outer side, closest to the bone, forward from line **f.** to line **z.**; this gives the foot a pleasing appearance. Then I draw the two sections through the foot **e. f.** separately, showing the form they take on the plane so that all this may be more clearly understood; for just as I demonstrate these two sections, one may seek out the sections in all bodies, whether they be straight or slanted.

Next, I will project the foot from the ground plan <note>an elevation drawing based on the base measurements</note> at a height of one 24th part above the flattened plan. Through the transfer from the flattened ground plan, I trace the lines **c. e. y. z. f. d.** as they stood before, and whatever else is necessary, all upward. I intersect these projected lines with two horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> at a height of one 24th part; this creates an elongated rectangle in which I place the projected foot. Afterward, I divide the horizontal side **a. b.** with two other horizontal lines **e. f.** into three equal fields. Thus, the first projected lines indicate where all parts of the foot belong according to their length: such as the frontmost sections of the toes, then their joints and the gaps between them, as well as where the nails should stand, and where the foot with all its characteristics and balls—likewise the bone of the heel in its section—is projected. I begin in the corner **a. f.** and draw the instep down to the vertical line **e.** at point **3.** From there, I draw the ball behind the big toe down to the horizontal **f.** Afterward, I continue forward beneath that to point **1.**, where the big toe ends at the front and top; but the smallest toe touches at the top with its hindmost joint and ball the fourth part between **f.** and **b.** Then I draw the three middle toes in order, making the longest one the highest, and each one further forward lower than the last, and I make the smallest toe quite low at the front. <meta>This page transitions from the construction of the human foot to a detailed geometric analysis of the female head, maintaining the same proportional grid system Dürer applied to the male figure on previous pages.</meta>

<note>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'S' featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs. This finely detailed woodcut initial from Albrecht Dürer's landmark work on human proportions exhibits characteristic early 16th-century German book design.</note>

**S**ince the foot has been completed / I will henceforth describe a woman's head more precisely / by following the same method and process used previously to describe the man's head / and just as I previously took one 8th part of the body's length for the height of the woman's head in the other type of figures / I will also take one 8th part here / yet in the same scale as the man's head.

First, I set three squares <note>original: "vierung," a proportional grid or square frame</note> next to each other / and by transferring the measurements / I make each one an 8th part [of the body's length] high / the first one an 8th part wide / in which I draw the head from the side profile / but the other two squares / one for the front view <note>original: "fursichtigen," looking forward</note> and the other for the back view <note>original: "hinderwertigẽ," looking backward</note> / I make each a 10th part wide. 

In the beginning, I mark the two vertical sides of the square / in which I draw the head from the side / the front side, where I will place the nose / with an **a.** and the back with a **b.** Then I divide with other vertical lines between **a.** and **b.** all the depths found in the face / and I mark each with a specific letter / and the numbers I use to describe them / I take from the total length of the figure. Now I place the first line **c.** an 80th part behind **a.**; this line **c.** touches the top of the crown on the forehead / where the hair begins / and the eyebrows end at the front / for they should not touch each other above the nose / it also touches the front of the upper lips. After that, I set another line **d.** a 64th part backwards / understand this always to be from the front line **a.**; this line touches the front of the upper eyelids, the lower lips of the mouth / and the front of the chin. Then I draw a line **e.** a 56th part backwards / this touches the depths above the eyelids / the eyeball at the front / the <term>nostril wing</term> <note>original: "naspellein," referring to the outer flare of the nostril</note> at the back / the corner of the mouth / and the <term>hollow</term> <note>original: "holkelen," the indentation or groove</note> under the lower lip. Then I set a line **f.** a 40th part backwards / which touches the back corner of the eyes. Furthermore, I set a line **g.** a 32nd part backwards / which touches the back ends of the eyebrows / and at the bottom, the fold under and behind the chin. After that, I set a line **h.** a 22nd part backwards / which touches the front of the neck. Further, I set a line **i.** a 14th part backwards / which touches the inside of the ear. Then I draw a line **k.** an 11th part backwards / at this line the back of the ear ends / but the lower earlobe remains halfway toward line **i.** Then I set a line **l.** a 9th part backwards / which touches the back crown of the hair on the head / and the nape of the neck at the bottom of the head / from there I draw the neck downwards and backwards. Finally, I draw the last vertical parallel line **m.** two 17th parts backwards / which touches the middle of the back of the neck.

Once I have drawn all the vertical lines and indicated / what they should touch / then I divide the height and depth of the parts of the face with parallel horizontal lines. 

First, let the top horizontal line of the aforementioned square be an **n.**; it touches the highest part of the head / and the bottom line shall be an **x. y.** Then I draw a horizontal line **o.** an 80th part under line **n.** / which touches the back crown of the head. Then I draw a line **p.** downward 30 parts from **n.** / it touches the front hairline above the forehead. Then I draw a line **q.** a 16th part under **n.** / it touches the top of the eyelid / the top of the ears / and the eyebrows end upon it at the back and front / but in the middle, I draw them above it. Then I draw a line **t.** in the middle between **q.** and **y.**; it touches the bottom of the nose / and there the nape of the neck meets the shoulders / then I draw a line **r.** and cut off the top third between **q.** and **t.**; it touches the bottom of the eyelid. However, I place the two corners of the eyes in the middle / likewise the eyeball between **q.** and **r.** Then I cut off the bottom fourth between **q.** and **t.** with a line **s.**; it touches the back of the nostril above / and the ear below. Then I divide **t.** and **y.** with a line **f.** in the middle from each other / it touches the top of the chin. Then I divide **t.** and **r.** with a line **b.** in the middle from each other / it passes through the middle of the mouth. Then I divide **t. b.** into three equal fields / in the bottom one I place the upper lip / but in the top two, I place the hollows <note>the philtrum</note> at the front under the nose and above the mouth. Between **b.** and **f.** I make two parts / in the upper one I place the lower lip / and in the lower part, I place the hollow between the lower lip and the chin.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details the geometric construction of a woman's head using a proportional grid, defining specific vertical and horizontal coordinates for features like the eyes, lips, and hairline.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, Albrecht Dürer, human anatomy, technical drawing, facial features, Renaissance geometry, woman's head, grid system</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric construction of the female head, moving from the profile view to the frontal, rear, and top-down (plan) views, using the same coordinate grid system established on the previous page.</meta>

Next, I draw a slanted line from the point where the vertical **.t.** closes, until the horizontal **p.** in the angle **a. y.** Over this line, I draw the forehead between **p.** and **q.**; but I draw the nose below that, between **q.** and **t.**

Afterward, I divide the second square <note>original: "vierung," the proportional grid frame</note> of the front-facing head <note>original: "fursichtigen haubtes," the head viewed from the front</note>, first with horizontal lines by transferring them from the first square; then I also divide it with vertical lines. I mark the two vertical sides of this square with **a. b.** Then I divide **a. b.** with nine lines **d. e. f. g. h. i. k. l. m.** into ten equal fields. But I divide **a. d.** and **m. b.** with two lines **c. n.**, each in the middle from one another. Now that the grids <note>original: "quadraturen," the system of squaring or gridding the space</note> in both squares are drawn through each other, within which the parts of the face are contained, I then draw the outline lines into it, with smaller and softer features <note>original: "gebrechen," here referring to the physical details or marks of the face</note> and a sweeter style than a man's face. And first, I draw the head from the side, such that the head touches the top horizontal **n.** at the vertical **j.**, and likewise at the back, the vertical **b.** between the horizontals **p.** and **q.** Then I draw the front of the head: the forehead, nose, mouth, chin, the cheeks, ears, then the eyes, and the bareness of the forehead <note>the hairline</note> into all the previously shown markings. And I make the neck one 14th part thick.

Next, I draw the outline lines between the cross-lines of the front-facing square. First, the rounding of the head, which touches the top horizontal **n.** in the middle, and on both sides the verticals **a. b.** between **p.** and **q.** Then I draw the jawbones down from the two verticals under the horizontal **r.** to the end of the chin. I draw the ears on both sides, between the verticals **a. c.** and **n. b.** and between the horizontals **q. s.** But the inner lobes <note>original: "zipffellein," referring to the tragus or the small points of the ear</note> I draw within the verticals **c. n.** I mention this again from the start so that it may be better noted. Then I draw the neck down from under the ears to the horizontal **y.**, where I make it one 15th part wide. Next, I draw the bareness of the forehead on both sides over the middle between the verticals **a. c.** and **n. b.**, between the horizontals **p.** and **q.** And from the line **g.** on the horizontal **p.**, I draw the hair above the forehead on both sides, the bareness upward, until the middle between the horizontal lines **o. p.** Then I draw both eyes so that their corners touch the vertical lines **e. g.** and **i. l.**, and I draw the eyebrows at the verticals **f. k.** over the horizontal **q.** and let them end on both sides under this line, in the middle between the verticals **d. e.** and **l. m.** Afterward, I draw the nose, but I do not let its nostrils <note>original: "pelle," literally the skins or wings of the nose</note> touch the two verticals **g. i.**, but I draw the mouth so that it touches both of them with its corners.

Then I draw the back of the head according to the outer lines of the front-facing view and place its features therein.

Afterward, I draw the head by transferring it into the flattened ground-plan <note>original: "nydergedruckten grund," referring to a top-down perspective or plan view</note>, as was described before for the man's head. I have drawn this so precisely hereafter that it will be clear to anyone of little understanding, even if no writing were accompanying it.

And if I wish to heighten this woman's head or make it wider, I do as I have indicated before regarding the man's head; however, it is not by as much, for it is fitting that the woman's head remains smaller than the man's.

**In what follows**, I will describe and draw <note>original: "auffreissen," meaning to draft or lay out geometrically</note> a young child. For this, I will use the same order as with the previously described figures. First, for as long as I wish to make the child, I set three upright lines or points that represent them side by side. By the first, I will make the child from the side; by the second, from the front; and by the third, from the back. I draw over and under these three upright lines with two horizontal lines: let the top be **.a.** and the bottom be **.z.**

Furthermore, I divide the length of the parts in this child with horizontal lines. Since **.a.** and **.z.** represent the crown of the head and the soles <note>the vertical limits of the figure</note>, I measure down a 4th part from the crown. There I place a horizontal line **.b.**, which touches the end of the little neck where it meets the body, both at the back and the front where the pit of the throat <note>original: "halßgrüblein," the suprasternal notch</note> is located.

From this height, I create a square <note>original: "vierung," the proportional grid frame</note> for the side-view head <note>original: "nebensichtigenn haupt"</note>, in which I enclose the head. I mark the front vertical side, where the nose projects outward, also with an **.a.**, and the back with a **.b.** And before I proceed further with the child, I will first fully describe the head.

To begin, I divide the head according to its length—the high and the low—with horizontal lines, as often mentioned before. From the horizontal **.a.**, I measure down a 24th part and place a horizontal line; this touches the top of the forehead at the front. But from **.a.** down a 16th part, I place a horizontal **.c.**, which goes through

...the hinder whorl <note>the crown or "wirbel" where the hair rotates</note> on the head; from this line downward, I set another **.d.** at a distance of one-sixth. This line touches the bottom of the chin, and the back of the head rests within it upon the neck. It also touches the height of the body in the middle above the shoulder where the shoulder muscle <note>the trapezius</note> is drawn upward; for one notices that the neck is drawn in its length with double-curved lines <note>original: "zwifachen schlangen linien," literally "double snake lines," referring to the natural S-curves of the anatomy</note>.

Afterward, I divide the space between **.d.** and **.b.** with two points into three equal fields, and I cut off the lowest third with a line **.e.**; upon this, the little throat <note>original: "tröllein," referring to the front of the neck or Adam's apple area</note> touches under the chin, and thus the little child gains a very short little neck. But if I divide **.a.** and **.b.** with a line **.f.** in the middle from one another, it touches the eyebrows at the front and back; however, in their middle, I raise them above it. It also touches the tops of the ears, and the head touches the upright line **.b.** there. Further, I divide **.f.** and **.d.** with a line **.g.** in the middle from one another; this touches the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the ears. After that, I divide **.f.** and **.g.** with two lines **.h.** and **.i.** into three equal fields; the upper line **.h.** goes through the center of the eyeball and both corners of the eyes; and I enclose the entire eye between the horizontal lines **.f.** and **.i.** with all its parts; and the line **.i.** touches the top of the nostrils, and I make the lower earlobe between **.i.** and **.g.** Afterward, I divide **.g.** and **.d.** with four horizontal lines **.k. l. m. n.** into five equal fields; the line **.k.** touches the top of the upper lip, but the line **.l.** I draw through the middle of the mouth. Then I divide **.l.** and **.m.** in the middle from one another; in the upper part I make the lower lip, but in the lower part I make the hollow <note>the mental-labial sulcus</note> under the lower lip above the chin on the line **.m.** I also draw, near the line **.l.**, a particular indentation of the head above the nape of the neck <note>the "gnick"</note>—where that is located will be shown hereafter—the line **.n.** touches the front dimple in the chin, the rounding of the cheeks below, and the side of the little neck above.

Now that the horizontal lines are drawn, I then draw the upright lines into them, which indicate all the depths of the parts of the head, and I mark them also with their letters. I divide the upright line **.a. b.** with an upright **.c.** in the middle from one another; this touches the front of the ear and stands in the middle of the body. However, I set a line **.d.** midway between **.a.** and **.c.**; this touches the back of the eyebrows and the front of the little neck and the bareness of the forehead.

Afterward, I divide **.a. d.** with four lines **.e. f. g. h.** into five equal fields; the line **.e.** touches the bridge above the nose in front of the eye, and the upper lip. But the line **.f.** touches the front ends of the eyebrows, the little nose at the back, the cheeks in front, the corner of the mouth, and the hollow under the lower lip above the chin. The line **.g.** touches the hair on the forehead above and the depths of the eyelid above, as well as the eyeball in front, and the indentation under the chin. The line **.h.** touches the hinder corners of the eyes, but the front corners step forward beyond the line **.g.** by half the distance toward **.f.** But I divide **.a. e.** with a line **.i.** in the middle from one another; this touches the forehead in front, though the little nose protrudes a bit. Afterward, I divide **.e. f.** with an upright **.l. d.** <note>the double letter likely refers to a specific coordinate point</note> in the middle from one another; this touches the lower lip in front and the chin in front. Afterward, I set a line **.k.** three twenty-parts backward from **.a.**; this touches the back of the ear, but the lower earlobe I make halfway toward the line **.c.** However, I set a line **.l.** one-fifth part backward from **.a.**; this touches the aforementioned indentation at the bottom of the head above the nape. The neck ends at the back midway between **.k.** and **.l.** Afterward, I divide **.l. b.** with three points into four equal fields, and at the third point, I place the whorl at the back of the head.

Since I have now drawn the square <term>square</term> <note>original: "fierung," the proportional grid frame</note> of the profile head <note>original: "nebensichtigen haubtes"</note> with the upright and horizontal lines, through which many squares are made, I would draw the specific placement of the parts of the face therein. But before I do that, I will first indicate the length of the parts through the body.

### Next, I make the length of the arm.

From the shoulder joint to the elbow, 2/11ths parts.

From the elbow to the tips of the fingers, 1/4th part.

From the tips of the fingers to the wrist joint, 1/9th part.

### Next, I make the thickness of the arm in profile.

In the shoulder, 1/10th part.

Under the armpit, 1/12th part.

In the elbow, 1/16th part.

In front of the elbow, 1/15th part.

Further forward, 1/18th part.

Behind the hand, 1/23rd part.

And the hand I make 1/21st part thick.

Since I have indicated the length of the child's parts, I will further describe the thicknesses of the child in profile <note>original: "nebensichtigen"</note> at all the points of the indicated lengths.

This child is 2/15ths parts thick at the point of the shoulder joint.

At the height of the breast, 1/6th part.

The midline between them passes right through the crown, nose, mouth, and chin. Then I divide [segments] a. c. and c. b. with two [lines] d. e. in the middle of each; these two touch the outer corners of the eyes and the width of the little neck.

<term>Anatomy</term>: The study of the structure of the body.
<term>Proportion</term>: The comparative relation between things or magnitudes as to size, quantity, or number.
<term>Albrecht Dürer</term>: (1471–1528) A central figure of the Northern Renaissance, famous for his prints, paintings, and theoretical treatises.
<term>Divider</term> <note>original: "Teiler"</note>: Dürer’s specific unit of measurement used to divide the body into mathematical fractions.
<term>Measurement</term> <note>original: "Mensur"</note>: A system of measuring or a set of standard dimensions.
<term>Anthropometry</term>: The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.
<term>Woodcut</term>: A relief printing technique where an image is carved into the surface of a block of wood.
<term>Renaissance</term>: The period of European history marking the transition from the Middle Ages to modernity, characterized by a revival of classical learning.
<term>Humanism</term>: A Renaissance intellectual movement that turned away from medieval scholasticism and revived interest in ancient Greek and Roman thought, emphasizing human potential.

<note>A highly detailed Renaissance woodcut by Albrecht Dürer shows the front and back views of a male body. The figures are annotated with a complex system of proportional measurements and reference grid lines, representing a landmark in the history of anatomical art and scientific observation. This plate from Dürer's 1528 treatise "Four Books on Human Proportion" is an iconic work of the Northern Renaissance. It exemplifies the era's fusion of artistic mastery with early modern scientific inquiry and is a foundational document in the history of anatomical studies.</note>

<summary>This page presents Albrecht Dürer’s mathematical breakdown of the male physique through front and back views, using his "Divider" system to establish ideal proportions. It marks a pivotal moment where Renaissance art meets the precision of proto-scientific anthropometry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the female figure's proportions in Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). | | measuring rod | number | parts | tiny bits <note>original: "trümlein." In Dürer's system, these are the smallest units of measurement, following the "measuring rod" (the largest), "number," and "parts."</note> |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| In the instep | 0 | 2 | 9 | 8 |
| At the end of the outer ankle through the foot | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |

So, now that these lengths, thicknesses, and breadths have all been described, I then draw the form of the man with his lines correctly placed therein: by the first upright line according to the side view, but by the second upright line from the front view.

Afterward, I outline the rear-facing man by the third upright line, using the outer lines of the forward-facing man as a guide, and I form him from behind:

| | measuring rod | number | part | tiny piece |

| And the heel is broad — | 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |

<note>A decorative emblem is shown here, featuring an intricately interlaced knot design in the Renaissance style. Such flourishes were common in high-quality early modern printing and are often associated with the workshop of Albrecht Dürer.</note>

Thus, as I have described above, I have sketched this man hereafter.

<note>A woodcut diagram depicts the front and back views of a male human figure with detailed anatomical measurement lines and numerical proportion indicators. This is a seminal work by Albrecht Dürer from his 'Four Books on Human Proportion' (1528), representing a pinnacle of Renaissance scientific illustration by combining artistic mastery with mathematical rigor to define ideal human proportions.</note>

<note>In this system, Dürer uses the letter 'T' to stand for 'Teile' (Parts). He divided the total height of the figure into a set number of units, which were then subdivided into smaller 'minutes' to allow for extreme precision in depicting the body's ratios.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the measurements of the male figure to the proportions of the female figure, following the same hierarchical system of "orders" established in the previous section of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528).</meta>

Following the man described previously, I will now describe a woman, using the first order <note>Dürer refers to his "First Book," which establishes a canon of proportions where the body is divided into fractional units rather than fixed lengths like inches or centimeters.</note> from the highest point of the head, the crown, to:

| | measuring rod | number | part | particle |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the back of the crown | 0 | 0 | 6 | 0 |
| To the forehead | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin | 0 | 7 | 7 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder muscle | 0 | 8 | 3 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder | 1 | 0 | 4 | 0 |
| Into the hollow of the throat | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |

**From the hollow of the throat to:**

| | measuring rod | number | part | particle |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the breast | 0 | 1 | 7 | 0 |

<note>Dürer’s technical vocabulary for anatomy and measurement:
<term>Meßstab (measuring rod)</term>: The largest unit of Dürer's proportional system; the total height of a figure is six rods.
<term>Zahl (number)</term>: One-tenth of a rod.
<term>Teil (part)</term>: One-tenth of a "number."
<term>Trümlein (particle)</term>: The smallest subdivision, one-tenth of a "part."
<term>Schulterfleysch</term>: The trapezius muscle connecting the neck and shoulder.
<term>Halsgrüblein</term>: The suprasternal notch or "hollow" at the base of the throat.
<term>Tütlein</term>: An archaic term for nipples.
<term>Weychen</term>: The soft area of the waist or flanks.
<term>Spaltung</term>: Literally "the split," referring here to the vulva.
<term>Einpynssung</term>: The tapering or indentation of the thigh as it approaches the knee.
<term>Rist</term>: The instep or bridge of the foot.
<term>Knozren</term>: The bony protrusions of the ankle (malleoli).
</note>

<summary>This page provides comprehensive data tables for the proportions of the female figure, detailing vertical measurements and lateral thicknesses from the crown of the head to the sole of the foot. It continues Dürer's rigorous mathematical approach to human anatomy, establishing a female canon to complement his male studies.</summary>
<keywords>female proportions, measurement units, anatomy, Renaissance art theory, Albrecht Dürer, anthropometry, mathematical canon</keywords>

<term>Proportion</term>, <term>Anatomy</term>, <term>Measurement</term>, <term>Albrecht Dürer</term>, <term>Theory of Proportions</term>, <term>Anthropometry</term> <note>The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.</note>

The precision and systematic approach make it a high-value object for historical and artistic study.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general proportions of the male figure to the specific measurements of a female figure in Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." Having established the male ideal on previous pages, Dürer now applies his mathematical system—consisting of the measuring rod, numbers, parts, and fractions—to the female body for artistic study.</meta>

->measuring rod, number, part, fraction<-
<note>Dürer uses a specific fractional measurement system: the "measuring rod" is the largest unit (the total height of the figure), which is then divided into "numbers," then "parts," and finally "fractions" or "trümlein" (literally 'little crumbs') for extreme precision.</note>

Next, I wish to describe another woman following the man described previously, and I shall use the same order and measurements as before.

# PART %

From the highest line of the crown to:

| The forehead ——— | 0 | 1 | 9 | 0 |
| The end of the chin ——— | 0 | 7 | 6 | 0 |
| The height of the shoulder muscle — | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |

| The pit of the throat ——— | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 |

| Under the back armpits ——— | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |

| Under the breasts ——— | 0 | 7 | 3 | 0 |

> Now that these lengths, thicknesses, and breadths have all been drafted <note>original: "auffgerissen," a technical term for plotting or drawing a plan.</note>, I then draw the form of the woman with her lines into it. By the first upright line, I draw the side view; by the second, the front view. Afterward, I draw the woman from the back at the third upright line, following the outer lines of the front-facing woman, and I make her buttocks split upward from the end — — — — — 0 | 5 | 7 | 0

And just as described above, I have hereafter drafted this woman, so that one may follow it accordingly.

Having established vertical lengths on the previous page, Dürer now moves to "thickness" (depth from a side profile) and "breadth" (width from a frontal and rear profile). The measurement system uses a "Meßstab" (measuring rod) divided into "Zal" (numbers), "Teyl" (parts), and "Trümlen" (fragments/drums), providing a highly precise geometric blueprint for artists.</meta>

<term>Theory of Proportions</term> (German: *Proportionslehre*): The systematic study of the mathematical relationships between different parts of the human body, aimed at finding "ideal" beauty.

<term>Albrecht Dürer</term>: The preeminent German Renaissance artist (1471–1528) who spent his final years documenting a scientific approach to drawing the human form.

<term>Anthropometry</term>: The scientific study of the measurements and proportions of the human body, a field Dürer pioneered through his geometric woodcuts.

<summary>This page lists the mathematical ratios for a male figure with a raised arm, using Dürer's specialized units of "Parts" (Teile) to define the proportions of the head, torso, and limbs. It serves as the data key for the accompanying anatomical woodcut diagram.</summary>

<keywords>human proportions, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance anatomy, geometry, mathematical ratios, anthropometry, anatomical measurements</keywords>

### Technical Terms
<term>Anatomy</term>, <term>Proportion</term>, <term>Theory of Proportions</term>, <term>Human Figure</term>, <term>Renaissance</term>, <term>Albrecht Dürer</term>, <term>Geometry</term>

<note>This image is a significant historical document representing the Renaissance quest for ideal beauty through geometry. By dividing the body into "Parts" (Teile), the artist attempts to find a universal mathematical law that governs the human form. The use of a posterior view allows the artist to map the musculature of the back and the alignment of the spine against a central vertical axis.</note>

<summary>This page presents a posterior anatomical study of a male figure, using a system of "Parts" and geometric arcs to define ideal human proportions. It reflects the Renaissance transition toward using empirical mathematics and geometry to standardize the representation of the human body.</summary>

When you stretch out this man’s arms and raise them as high as he is long, and then place one foot of the compass on his navel, you may then circle him with the other foot in the manner of a circle. <note>This is a reference to the "Vitruvian Man," the concept that a well-proportioned human body fits within the geometric shapes of the square and the circle.</note>
And as described above, I have clearly drafted this man hereafter, and marked him with the numbers and his signs.

It represents the period's rigorous mathematical approach to capturing the human form, making it a masterpiece of both scientific documentation and artistic theory.</note>

Likewise, if you stretch this woman out with her arms in the shape of a cross and raise her hands as high as she is long, and then place one foot of a compass on her navel and the other foot at the soles of her feet and draw a circle all around, the circle's path will touch the furthest parts of the hands and feet. <note>This describes a variation of the Vitruvian Man, specifically adapted for the female figure to show the geometric perfection of the body.</note>

The figure has one arm raised to a horizontal line, with a large geometric arc drawn from the fingertips to the ground level near the feet. This is a seminal illustration from Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528), representing a masterwork of Renaissance art and science. It is iconic for its systematic approach to the human form through geometry and measurement.</note>

<term>Proportion</term>: The harmonious relationship of different parts of the body to one another and to the whole.
<term>Anatomy</term>: The study of the internal and external structure of the body.
<term>Measurement</term>: The act of determining the exact dimensions of the body.
<term>Albrecht Dürer</term>: The German Renaissance master (1471–1528) who sought to find mathematical laws for beauty and nature.
<term>Part</term>: <note>original: "Teil"</note> A specific unit of measurement used by Dürer to divide the height of the human body.
<term>Anthropometry</term>: The study of the measurements and proportions of the human body.
<term>Geometry</term>: The branch of mathematics used here to map the human form using lines, circles, and arcs.

<note>This sophisticated anatomical diagram shows a human male figure from the back, used for the study of proportions. It includes a central vertical axis, horizontal height and width lines, and a large circular arc demonstrating the range of motion or reach of the right arm. Handwritten fractions and measurement symbols (the 'P' for Part <note>original: 'T' for 'Teil'</note>) are placed at the shoulder, mid-back, sacrum, and feet. This drawing is a high-quality example of Early Modern artist-scientist studies of human proportion, closely following the traditions established by Albrecht Dürer. Furthermore, I will describe another man in the same order as before. Therefore, I first measure the length of the limbs and other necessary things along the first vertical line, namely:

From the highest point of the **crown** down to:

| | measuring rod <note>original: "meßstab"</note> | part <note>original: "zahl"</note> | fraction <note>original: "teyl"</note> | drumlet <note>original: "trümlen"</note> |

| To the back of the head | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin | 0 | 7 | 6 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder flesh | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder joint | 1 | 0 | 6 | 0 |
| Into the hollow of the throat | 1 | 1 | 5 | 0 |

Thereafter, I divide three equal fields with two horizontal lines between the forehead and the end of the chin. In the uppermost, I place the forehead. In the second, the nose, eyes, and ears. In the third, the mouth and chin. I do this with all heads, which I will not always mention hereafter for the sake of brevity.

From the **hollow of the throat** down to:
