Once all lengths, thicknesses, and widths are drawn, then I trace the figure with **graceful lines**. Along the first vertical line, I draw the profile view; but along the second vertical line, I draw his frontal figure. Thereafter, I outline the figure of the back-facing man along the third vertical line, using the outer lines of the frontal man. And I make his buttocks split from below | 0 | 5 | 5 | 0 | and make his heels wide | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

When you stretch out this man’s arm and raise his height, you circumscribe him with a circle-line <note>original: "zirckelriß," a circle drawn with a compass</note> if you place one foot of the compass in the navel. And as described above, I have drawn this man here entirely according to my opinion, so that one may follow it more clearly.

## [Anatomical Proportion Study of a Man]

<note>The illustration displays a male nude in a rear view with his right arm extended. A circular arc connects the fingertips of the raised hand to the base where he stands, demonstrating the geometric "reach" of the human form. The body is marked with horizontal measurement lines and numerous proportional numbers, along with the abbreviation "T3" <note>original: "T3"; this refers to three "Parts" within Dürer's fractional measurement scheme.</note>.</note>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric study of human proportions, moving from the male figure described on the previous pages to the female figure. # PART [SECTION]

Following the man I have just described, I now wish to describe a woman in the same order. I must first [set down] the length of the limbs and the necessary parts of the body along the first upright line, thus:

### From the height of the top-most line to:
| | measuring rod | number | part | small fragment |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The back of the head <note>original: "hindern wirbel"</note> | 0 | 9 | 0 | 0 |
| The forehead | 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |
| The end of the chin | 0 | 7 | 5 | 0 |
| The height of the shoulder muscle | 0 | 9 | 0 | 0 |
| The pit of the neck <note>The jugular notch at the base of the throat.</note> | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 |

| The back of the armpit | 0 | 4 | 6 | 0 |
| The nipples | 0 | 5 | 6 | 0 |
| Under the breast | 0 | 7 | 2 | 0 |
| The flanks <note>The area of the waist or side of the torso.</note> | 1 | 1 | 8 | 0 |

Now that all these lengths, thicknesses, and widths are described and plotted, I then draw the figure of the woman, skillfully placing the lines within: using the first upright line for the side view, and the second for the front view. Afterward, I draw the rear-facing woman at the third upright line using the outer lines of the front-facing woman, and split the buttocks from the bottom up.

| | measuring rod | number | part | small fragment |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| And make the heels wide | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

If you stretch out this woman's arms and raise them as high as she is tall, you may circumscribe her with a circle drawn with a compass <note>original: "circkelryß"</note> if you place one foot of the compass on the navel and sweep around with the other.

And just as it is described here above, I have subsequently plotted this woman and marked her with all the figures and characters as they appear before your eyes, so that you may better guide yourself by them.

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric and anatomical analysis of human proportions. Following the posterior view of a male figure on the previous page, Dürer now transitions to a "side-view" or profile study of a woman, using the same modular measuring system. The text provides a rigorous set of tables for vertical lengths and horizontal thicknesses.</meta>

Now I will assign another method to the man previously described; from the previous arrangement, I first measure the length of the limbs and necessary parts as follows:

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Tiny Part |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| From the highest line of the head to the line of the back crown <note>original: "hindern wirbels"; refers to the vertex or highest point of the skull's curve.</note> is | 0 | 0 | 7 | 0 |
| To the forehead | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin | 0 | 7 | 4 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder flesh | 0 | 8 | 7 | 0 |
| Into the hollow of the throat <note>The suprasternal notch, the visible dip at the base of the neck.</note> | 1 | 0 | 2 | 0 |
| And from the forehead to the eyebrows is | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

Afterwards, I divide with a transverse line <note>original: "zwerchlini"; a horizontal reference line.</note> between the eyebrows and the end of the chin into two equal fields. In the upper field, I place the nose, eyes, and ears; in the lower field, the mouth and chin.

### Thereafter I measure further.

From the hollow of the throat to

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Tiny Part |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The height of the shoulder is | 0 | 0 | 8 | 0 |
| To the shoulder joints | 0 | 1 | 5 | 0 |
| Under the front armpits <note>original: "ochsen"; an archaic term for the armpit (Achsel/Uchse).</note> | 0 | 4 | 7 | 0 |
| Under the back armpits | 0 | 5 | 7 | 0 |
| To the breasts | 0 | 6 | 5 | 0 |
| Under the chest | 0 | 8 | 1 | 0 |
| To the flanks <note>original: "weychen"; the soft part of the side between the ribs and the hip.</note> | 1 | 2 | 5 | 0 |

From the flanks to

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Tiny Part |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| In the navel is | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| To the end of the hip | 0 | 6 | 5 | 0 |
| To the end of the belly | 0 | 7 | 5 | 0 |
| To the crotch <note>original: "spaltung"; literally the "split" or division of the legs.</note> | 0 | 8 | 7 | 0 |
| To the end of the pubic area | 0 | 9 | 6 | 0 |
| To the end of the buttocks | 1 | 0 | 8 | 0 |

From the end of the buttocks to

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Tiny Part |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The indentation of the leg is <note>original: "einpeissung des beins"; likely the gluteal fold or the narrowing of the thigh.</note> | 0 | 4 | 5 | 0 |
| Upon the knee | 0 | 8 | 7 | 0 |
| To the middle of the knee | 1 | 0 | 9 | 0 |

From the middle of the knee to

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Tiny Part |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Below the knee is | 0 | 1 | 5 | 0 |
| To the end of the outer calf | 0 | 5 | 8 | 0 |
| To the end of the inner calf | 0 | 6 | 7 | 0 |
| To the height of the instep | 1 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| To the end of the outer ankle of the shinbone | 1 | 3 | 9 | 0 |
| To the end of the sole | 1 | 5 | 6 | 0 |
| And the foot is this long | 0 | 9 | 0 | 0 |

### Thereafter I determine the length of the arm as follows.

From the shoulder joint into

<note>A full-page anatomical diagram shows a female figure standing with one arm extended horizontally to meet a large circular arc, representing the reach of the limb. The body is bisected by a vertical center line and divided horizontally by numerous measurement lines that align with the anatomical points defined in the text. <note>Technical Terms:
**Proportion**: The balanced relationship between parts of a whole.
**Anatomy**: The study of the structure of the body.
**Parts**: <note>original: "Teile"</note> Dürer’s primary unit of measurement for the human figure.
**Fractions**: <note>original: "Trümlein"</note> Literally "small pieces," these are the sub-units used for fine details.
**Compass arc**: <note>original: "Zirkelschlag"</note> A geometric curve drawn to demonstrate the range of movement or proportional symmetry.
**Theory of Proportion**: <note>original: "Proportionslehre"</note> The Renaissance science of using mathematics to achieve beauty and accuracy in art.</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed anatomical diagram of a female figure mapped with Albrecht Dürer's "Parts" and "Fractions" measurement system. It serves as a visual proof of his mathematical approach to capturing ideal human proportions during the Northern Renaissance.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomical diagram, Renaissance science, measurement units, geometry in art</keywords>

<gloss>3/5 Parts <note>original: "T 3/5"</note></gloss>
<gloss>6 6 / 3 Parts <note>original: "6 6 / T 3"</note></gloss>
<gloss>3 2 / 8 Parts <note>original: "3 2 / T 8"</note></gloss>
<gloss>n \quad n</gloss>
<gloss>1 9 / 6 1 / 3 Parts <note>original: "1 9 / 6 1 / T 3"</note></gloss>

### Key Technical Vocabulary

<term>Proportion Theory</term>: <note>original: "Proportionslehre"</note> The study of the mathematical relationships between the individual parts of the human body to create a harmonious whole.
<term>Anatomy</term>: <note>original: "Anatomie"</note> The scientific study of the structure of the body, which Dürer sought to standardize through mathematical observation.
<term>Albrecht Dürer</term>: The German Renaissance master whose 1528 work *Four Books on Human Proportion* established this system.
<term>Scale / Measurement</term>: <note>original: "Mensur"</note> The specific system of measurement or "mensuration" used to determine the exact dimensions of the figure.
<term>Geometry</term>: <note>original: "Geometrie"</note> The application of points, lines, and angles to map the human form in space.
<term>Compass arc</term>: <note>original: "Zirkelschlag"</note> A curved line drawn with a compass to illustrate the swing or range of motion of a limb, such as the arm reaching upward.
<term>Canon</term>: <note>original: "Kanon"</note> A set of rules or standards defining the "ideal" proportions for the human figure.

---

<note>An anatomical diagram shows a human male figure viewed from behind, demonstrating body proportions and the range of arm motion using a geometric arc. Numerical measurements are inscribed along the central axis and limbs. This is a significant historical example of Renaissance scientific illustration, specifically from the tradition of proportional studies established by Albrecht Dürer. The precision of the linework and the integration of mathematical notation with the human form make it a masterpiece of early modern technical drawing.</note>

<summary>This page presents a posterior anatomical study of a male figure, utilizing Dürer’s proportional units and geometric arcs to map the body's dimensions and potential movement. It serves as a technical companion to earlier studies, emphasizing the mathematical "canon" of the human body.</summary>

<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, anatomical drawing, Renaissance geometry, posterior view, measurement units, canon of proportions, range of motion</keywords>

| | **†** | **z** | **I** | **%** |
| And make for him, from the bottom upwards, the buttocks divided by | 0 | 5 | 8 | 0 |
| And make for him the heels <note>original: "ferschē"</note> wide | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |

And in the same way as I present this man described above, I have sketched him in the following pages so that one may guide oneself by the measuring rod. One should also know that every body is wider across its surface in front than it is thick in profile; but the legs, when viewed from the side, are thicker than they are wide from the front. The figures that were measured with parts and numbers in the first booklet may also be measured approximately with the measuring rod. It makes a great difference if a figure's head is taken away and another is set in its place <note>Dürer suggests that changing the proportions of the head can fundamentally alter the perceived character or "type" of the entire figure.</note>.

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical geometric instructions for drawing the human head, following the anatomical measurements established in the earlier sections of his "Four Books on Human Proportion."</meta>

Next, I will more precisely describe two types of men's heads, as I have already indicated in the figures at the beginning of this booklet, and I will partly use the order described in the first book.

First, I make a square <term>vierung<note>A geometric frame or "squaring" used to establish proportional boundaries.</note></term> of a seventh part <term>sibenteils<note>Dürer’s "seventh part" refers to a system where the height of the head is one-seventh of the total body height.</note></term> in height of the figure's length.

However, whoever wishes may make the square an eighth part in height of the figure's length, provided that in the eighth part all things are divided equally, just as in the seventh part, in both small and large scales. Now, I take for myself the square as it stands above, as wide as it is high. And let the two upright <term>auffrechtē<note>vertical</note></term> sides be the front, **a**, and the back, **b**. But the two transverse <term>zwerch<note>horizontal</note></term> sides shall be the top, **c**, and the bottom, **d**. Thereafter, I divide the height of the seventh part into eight **E** <note>Dürer uses "E" to represent a primary division unit</note> and one part into ten ┴ <note>This symbol represents "trümlein" or the smallest fractional units of his scale.</note>. Next, I measure as follows hereafter, just as was indicated before, and I first divide the vertical lines. The first line, **e**, I set from line **a** backward —
<margin>| 0 | 0 | 9 | 0 |</margin>

This line touches the eyeball in front and the nostrils <term>naßpellen<note>literally "nose-skins," referring to the wings or flares of the nostrils</note></term> behind.

Thereafter, I set a line, **f**, behind line **a** —
<margin>| 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |</margin>

This line touches the eyebrows <term>augpraen<note>archaic German for eyebrows</note></term> at the back, but the eye remains with its back corner in the middle between **e** and **f**.

Now, I set the third line, **g**, from **a** backward —
<margin>| 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |</margin>

This line touches the front of the ear <term>or<note>archaic spelling for Ohr (ear)</note></term>.

Now, I set the fourth line, **h**, from **g** backward —
<margin>| 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |</margin>

The ear ends at this line.

Thereafter, I set a line, **i**, forward from **b** —
<margin>| 0 | 1 | 3 | 0 |</margin>

This line touches the nape <term>gnick<note>the back of the neck</note></term> where the head stands upon the neck.

Next, I set a line, **k**, forward from **b** —
<margin>| 0 | 0 | 7 | 0 |</margin>

This line touches the crown <term>wirbel<note>the whorl or top-most point of the skull</note></term> at the top back of the head, and touches the neck below on the horizontal line **d**. Once the vertical lines are drawn, I then draw the horizontal lines thus: From the horizontal line **c** downward —
<margin>| 0 | 2 | 6 | 2 |</margin>

I set a line, **l**; this line touches the hairline <term>harwachs<note>the point where the hair begins to grow</note></term> on the forehead. Thereafter, I divide **c** and **l** with a line, **m**, in the middle between them; this line touches the crown of the hair at the back of the head by the vertical line **k**. Thereafter, I divide **l** and **d** with two lines, **n** and **o**, into three equal fields. This line, **n**, goes through the eyebrows and touches the top of the ear; but the line **o** touches the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the ear. Thereafter, I divide **o** and **d** with a line, **p**, in the middle between them; this line touches the top of the chin under the lips <term>lebßen<note>archaic term for lips</note></term>. Thereafter, with a line, **q**, in the middle between them; thus the upper lip remains between **o** and **q**, and the lower between **q** and **p**, and the line **q** goes through the middle of the mouth. Thereafter, I divide **n** and **o** with a line, **r**, into two equal parts; in the upper part, the entire eye is made with all its belonging parts. Thereafter, I divide **r** and **o** into seven equal fields, and cut off the bottom three fields with a line, **s**; this touches the top of the nostril, and the small earlobe is drawn between **s** and **o**. Thereafter, I draw two diagonal lines <term>ort lini<note>lines that establish the "ort" or specific coordinates/slopes of the facial profile</note></term>; the first from the point where the vertical line **e** and the horizontal **c** meet, to the point where the vertical **a** and the horizontal **o** meet. Over this diagonal line, the forehead is drawn between the horizontals **l** and **n**, but the nose would be drawn beneath it between the horizontals **n** and **o**. I draw the second diagonal line from the point where the vertical and horizontal **n** meet, to the point where the vertical **e** and the horizontal **d** meet. On this diagonal line, the upper and lower lips and the front of the chin are touched. Once this is done, I then draw the contour lines of the face diligently into it with all things, so that the head at the top...

<note>A vertical measurement chart labeled with 'measuring rod', 'number', 'part', and 'fraction' <note>original: "meßstab, zall, teyl, trümlein"</note>. It consists of a grid and numerical values aligned with specific descriptive lines in the main text body, used to define the proportions of the human head. An excellent example of 16th-century technical diagramming for artistic proportion, showing the geometric systematization of anatomical drawing.</note>

<summary>This page provides a detailed mathematical and geometric guide for constructing a male head in profile using a grid system based on "sevenths" or "eighths" of the body's length. Dürer specifies exactly where facial features like the eyes, nostrils, hairline, and chin should fall relative to vertical and horizontal coordinates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric instructions for constructing the proportions of the human head, focusing on the placement of features within a grid and how to adjust the head's size relative to the body.</meta>

<margin>
| measuring rod | number | part | fraction |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

...the transverse line **c** at the upright line **h**; and behind, between the transverse lines **n**, the upright line **b**. And make the neck thick from the nape <term>gnick<note>The back of the neck or nape.</note></term> to the throat <term>gorgel<note>The front of the neck or "gullet."</note></term> —

<margin>0 | 1 | 8 | 0</margin>
As I have drawn the head hereafter; but should this head be too large for your figure, then let all other things remain as they were made before, and draw the head alone smaller. Do it thus: between the transverse lines **g** and **h**, let the head touch at its highest point in the middle between the transverse lines **c** and **m**, and at the back at the upright line **k**, in the middle between the transverse lines **l** and **n**. Then draw the head down to the nape at the transverse line **o**, extending forward from the upright line **b** —

From there, draw the neck down to the transverse line **d** at the upright line **i**. Use whichever [method] pleases you, for this is not shown in vain.

## The other head, make it in its proper square thus.

<margin>0 | 1 | 1 | 0</margin>
Let the two upright sides of the square <term>vierung<note>A geometric frame used to plot proportions.</note></term> be the front **a** and the back **b**, and let the upper transverse line be **i** and the lower **k**. Thereafter, I first divide the upright lines into it, and set the first line **c** backward from **a** —

<margin>0 | 2 | 2 | 0</margin>
This line touches the eyeball in front and the wing of the nose <term>naßbelle<note>Literally "nose-skin," referring to the outer flare or ala of the nostril.</note></term> behind. Thereafter, I set the second line **d** backward from **a** —

<margin>0 | 4 | 0 | 0</margin>
This line touches the end of the eyebrows <term>augpraen<note>Archaic German for eyebrows.</note></term> and, down on the transverse line **k**, it touches the back of the chin; the back corner of the eyes ends in the middle between **c** and **d**. Thereafter, I set the third line **e** backward from **a** —

<margin>0 | 5 | 0 | 0</margin>
This line touches the front of the ear, and near it, the top of the head touches the transverse line **i**. Thereafter, set the fourth line **f** backward from **a** —

<margin>0 | 1 | 0 | 0</margin>
The ear ends at this line. Thereafter, I set another line **g** forward from **b** —

<margin>0 | 0 | 8 | 0</margin>
This line touches the crown of the hair <term>wirbel<note>The "whorl" or highest point of the scalp.</note></term> on the head, and at that same spot the nape of the head sits upon the neck. Thereafter, draw the transverse lines down from the transverse line **i** thus —

<margin>0 | 1 | 4 | 0</margin>
Set a line **l**; this line touches the back of the crown of the hair on the head at the upright line **g**. Next, set a line **m** down from line **i** —

<margin>0 | 4 | 6 | 0</margin>
This line touches the hairline <term>harwachs<note>Literally "hair-growth," the line where the hair begins on the forehead.</note></term> on the forehead. Thereafter, I divide the space between **m** and **k** with two lines, **n** and **o**, into three equal fields. The line **n** goes through the middle of the eyebrows and touches the top of the ear. But the line **o** touches the bottom of the nose and the ear, and the head at the nape stands upon the neck at this line. And the neck is thick from there to the throat —

<margin>0 | 1 | 0 | 0</margin>
Thereafter, draw a line **p** down from line **n** —

<margin>0 | 0 | 5 | 0</margin>
Thus the entire eye will be contained between **n** and **p**, and the two corners of the eyes are in the middle between **n** and **p**. Thereafter, set a line **r** above line **o** —

<margin>0 | 1 | 0 | 0</margin>
This line touches the top of the wing of the nose, and the lobe of the ear has its particular curve between **r** and **o**. Thereafter, set a line **s** upward from **k** —

This touches the top of the chin under the lips <term>lebsen<note>An archaic term for the lips.</note></term>. Thereafter, divide the space between **o** and **s** with a line **t** in the middle from each other; this line goes through the middle of the mouth. Thereafter, draw the two diagonal lines <term>ort liniẽ<note>Boundary or corner lines used to define the profile's slant.</note></term>: the first from the point where the upright **c** and the transverse **i** meet, to the point where the upright **a** and the transverse **o** meet. But draw the other from the point where the upright **a** and the transverse **n** meet, to the point where the upright **c** and the transverse **k** meet. Above the upper diagonal line, the forehead is drawn between the transverse lines **m** and **n**, but below it, the nose is drawn between the transverse lines **n** and **o**. Thereafter, the lips, mouth, and chin are drawn by the lower diagonal line. Now that the straight lines are all drawn, draw within them the shape of the face with diligent lines, as I have drawn it hereafter.

...each limb for itself, measured and divided according to its own length and size. But below, the ankles are half as wide. And yet, in this manner, you may set the measurements however you wish. This method can be used for all parts throughout the entire body, so that one may examine every thing very precisely.

<meta>This page begins the third book of Albrecht Dürer’s treatise. While the first two books established the "ideal" or standard proportions of the human body, Dürer now introduces the principles of variation and "transformation" (verkerung) to create a diverse range of figures.</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' features a man's face integrated into intricate foliage and scrollwork patterns. This Renaissance letterform demonstrates Dürer's skill in blending human features with ornamental design.</note>

# The Third Book.

In this third book, I wish to show how one may change and transform the previously described proportions according to each person's will, increasing or decreasing all things so that an image becomes unrecognizable and remains not at all within its previous form and measure. I take this up in the following for figures of men, women, and children, and likewise in everything else.

But to understand this correctly, one must first of all know which things allow such a transformation to occur. This can be done through the joining together of <term>widerwertige<note>Literally "contraries" or "opposites." Dürer uses this term to describe the dialectical relationship between different physical characteristics.</note></term> in all specific proportions or <term>accidents<note>In the Aristotelian sense used in the Renaissance, "accidents" refer to non-essential qualities that can change without altering the essence of the subject, such as its size, age, or thickness.</note></term>. For in identical things there is no difference except that one is not the other; therefore, identical things cannot be distinguished from one another. What may be called "opposites" useful for measurement are these following things:

->Large } { Small<-
->Long } or { Short<-
->Wide } { Narrow<-
->Thick } { Thin<-

Furthermore, there are some attached or accidental things which also transform the figure, such as:

->Young Old<-
->Fat or Lean<-
->Lovely Ugly<-
->Hard Soft<-

Next, one uses other things that serve to change such matters, such as:

->Crooked } { Straight<-
->Even } { Uneven<-
->High } or { Low<-
->Round } { Angled<-
->Pointed } { Blunt<-

Next, these things also transform:
->{ Upright<-
->{ Transverse<-
->{ Or Diagonal<-

Also, [this] transforms greatly:
->{ Giving<-
->{ Taking<-
->{ Much<-
->{ Little<-

Furthermore, one sometimes uses:
->{ Right<-
->{ Left<-
->{ Front<-
->{ Back<-
->{ Top<-
->{ Bottom<-

From such aforementioned things, one may distinguish a thing from that which was first made.

<summary>Dürer introduces the third book by explaining how to vary "ideal" proportions through the use of opposites and accidental qualities to create unique human figures. He provides a systematic list of physical and spatial contraries—such as large and small, fat and lean—that an artist can manipulate.</summary>
<keywords>transformation, variation, proportion, opposites, accidents, aesthetic theory, figure drawing</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s exploration of how to modify the "ideal" human proportions established in his first book. He specifically addresses how the concepts of scale (Large and Small) and proportion (Long and Short) affect the visual appearance of a figure.</meta>

...to divide distinctly and to make the form different from before. We wish to put this into practice a little, and first we take up the two words **Large** or **Small**, which are to be used in all things that one wishes to make large or small. In such a thing, in itself, there is no other change of form except that one says "this is a large thing" or "the other is a small thing." Yet both may be of one kind and one proportion; therefore, the written proportions may be made large or small, and each contains within itself its own parts of measure and number. We take as an example of this a model of a small <term>verjungten<note>Literally "diminished" or "tapered," referring here to a scale model.</note></term> house, according to which a large palace is built and erected; or how a small sphere compares to a large one. Thus, the words "large" or "small" remain, and the form does not change, as was said above.

But if the words "large" or "small" are to bring about a distinct change in the form, then this must happen in certain parts and not in the whole all at once. If one makes some parts larger and others smaller, then the appearance of that same thing becomes different. As when you make a figure large above the belt and smaller below the belt; and likewise, as mentioned above, to use this through the entire <term>glidmaß<note>The proportions or "measure of the limbs."</note></term> in every single part, from the smallest to the largest. In such a way, the variations are infinitely many. Therefore, it is to be noted that no thing is recognized as large or small next to another unless the opposite part is placed against the large or the small. This may be used by anyone as far as he has need of it.

Further, we wish to take up the words **Long** or **Short**. From these, we wish to make the first described "thick man" of the first book long and short. Now note: if this man is lengthened, he becomes thinner and narrower in his measure; but if he is made shorter, he becomes thicker and wider according to his kind and measure. In such lengthening or shortening, one lets the thickness and width remain unchanged in all things, just as they were indicated in the first described man. But if those two men—the lengthened and the shortened—are drawn back into their own measure to the length of the first described man, then their thickness and width will all be different from the first described man from whom they were drawn. And all parts of the length become equal again to the first described man, yet they look unlike one another, because one has become thinner and narrower, and the other thicker and wider. Through the aforementioned way, the length of the limbs is comparatively lengthened or shortened between all the <term>zwerch linien<note>Transverse or horizontal lines used in Dürer's diagrams to mark the divisions of the body.</note></term> which then indicate the length of things in the body. If one stretches them far from one another, the image lengthens; if one pushes them close together, it makes the image shorter. Therefore, as spoken above, such a thing neither gives nor takes anything from the thickness or width in the entire proportions of the limbs; yet the words "long" or "short" make a distinction in the figures. And if one then measures them anew with the <term>zirckel<note>A compass or pair of dividers used for transferring measurements.</note></term>, their description will be different from that of the first man. Now, it would be tedious if one always had to take the trouble, when lengthening or shortening a figure, to seek out every single part with the compass. Therefore, I will hereafter set down a rule for how one shall find this lengthening and shortening equally and easily.

First, I must have a <term>punctirte lini<note>A "pointed" or "dotted" line; a line marked with specific points of measurement.</note></term>, according to which I then wish to proportionally mark two others, a long one and a short one. This I do thus: I take for myself a line for the side **a. b.**, as long as the first described man of the first book was. This line is marked with points by the transverse lines with which the figure is described through the entire length of the limbs. This line **a. b.**

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric principles of varying human proportions by manipulating scale (large/small) and aspect ratio (long/short), noting that changing one dimension without the others creates entirely new physical types.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, scale, human figure, geometry, measurement, lengthening, shortening, transverse lines, dividers, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on altering proportions. Having introduced the concepts of scale (Large and Small) and proportion (Long and Short) on the previous page, he now provides a specific geometric method for lengthening or shortening a figure while maintaining its internal logic.</meta>

<sig>D iiij</sig>

I place [a line] upright before me / and set a point **c** far to the side of it. From this point **c**, I draw straight lines through all the points of the upright line **a. b.** and further outward. Now that **a. b. c.** has become a **triangle** / and the radiating lines look almost like the bristles of a brush / I then set two other upright parallel lines next to the upright line **a. b.**, one on each side. The shorter one I place toward point **c**, and I mark its top and bottom as **d.** and **e.** The longer one, however, I set further out; let its top be **f.** and its bottom **g.** And as much as I want the man to be taller / the further out I place the line / and as much as the man should be shorter / the closer I push the line toward point **c**. Thus, what we desire is now divided by the radiating lines drawn from point **c** through the three upright lines **d. e.**, **a. b.**, and **f. g.** / and every line is divided in exact proportion to the others.

The rule as it stands above, I have properly drafted <note>original: *auffgerissen*, meaning a technical drawing or schematic</note> / to be used not only in this case / but for further purposes / and I give this figure its own name / and call it a <term>transformer<note>German: *verkerer*. A geometric device or diagram used to "convert" or transform proportions from one scale to another.</note></term> / because a thing is transformed by it. Along with this transformer, the two <term>transfer grids<note>German: *röstlein*, literally "little grates." These are proportional grids used to map out and transfer points of a drawing.</note></term> may be used, to a greater or lesser extent.

Through this <term>transformer</term>, any thing that has infinite parts may be drawn uniformly into a larger or smaller size / for many wonderful things can be made from this.

Through this <term>transformer</term>, one may take a figure that was previously seven <term>heads tall<note>German: *haubt lang*. A standard unit of artistic measurement where the height of the head determines the overall height of the body.</note></term> / and make it eight, nine, or up to ten <term>heads tall</term>. In this way, between the head and the instep of the foot, the figure is always lengthened by one, two, or three head-lengths. However, if the figure is lengthened by that much / one must then also lengthen the head slightly as the situation requires / so that the head does not look too small <note>original: *muncket*</note> / though the figure will then no longer be as many <term>heads tall</term> as it was before.

Special care must be taken if one wishes to make the specifically described hands and feet from the first book longer or shorter through the <term>transformer</term> / and one must proceed with great diligence.

<note>An intricate example of 16th-century typographic decoration, likely from Albrecht Dürer's treatise on human proportion, showing characteristic Renaissance geometric interlacing.</note>

<summary>Dürer describes a geometric "transformer" (a triangular diagram) used to scale figures up or down. He explains how this tool can adjust a person's height from seven heads tall to ten, while warning the artist to adjust the head size so the proportions remain visually pleasing.</summary>

This figure shall be called the <term>transformer<note>German: *verkerer*. A term used by Dürer to describe a geometric device that "converts" or transforms proportions from one scale to another while maintaining their relative ratios.</note></term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "transformer" device introduced on the previous page, moving from the conceptual diagram to the practical construction of a physical tool for scaling figures.</meta>

D iij

<note>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'S' is adorned with foliate and scrollwork patterns, typical of the high-quality Renaissance printing found in Dürer's treatises.</note>

**S**O, now that these two newly made dotted lines—the long **f. g.** and the short **d. e.**—have each been drawn through with transverse lines <note>Horizontal or cross-wise lines.</note> through all the points they have received; I then draw the figure of the man, long and short, according to the placement and forward-facing view as indicated in the first-described booklet, in all its thickness and breadth. And just as this rule indicates the length of the elongated and the shortened man, I have drawn them both as they should be. However, I have not been able to conveniently print the elongated man here due to the small size of the paper; therefore, I have placed him back into his first-described length and redrawn him according to his newly gained proportions. This is done through the method that follows, which one may also use at any time for other things. Thus, the measurements on line **f. g.** become the thinner and narrower man, and on line **d. e.** a thicker, broader man. You may also use this <term>transformer<note>German: *verkerer*. A geometric device used to scale proportions.</note></term> in the same way for the qualities of thick and thin, or wide and narrow, as you do for long and short. It is also good and quite fitting that all figures throughout the booklets be drawn in a single length, so that one may better judge them against each other. It should also be known that through the aforementioned transformer, one may change every specific part in a previously made figure and leave others as they are; yet, whatever is taken from one part is added to another—this refers to the length of the limbs. But the same is also to be done on all the transverse lines of the figures by which the length is divided, and also in between, to make them thicker, thinner, wider, or narrower. For as often as you have a previously described line and place it in the transformer, and set two other lines—one before and one after or behind it—it shows you what it gives you. In this transformer, you may set whatever you need, and it will change it for you proportionally.

Now I wish to show how one should proceed so that one may work from this, and I will make an instrument out of the transformer. With this, you will find how to make every previously described part longer or shorter, thicker or thinner, wider or narrower, entirely proportional to one another through all parts of the figure's total divided length, thickness, and breadth.

First, draw a transverse line starting at **a.** and ending at **b.** Make this line fixed and steady. But from point **a.**, draw another line ending at **c.** This line **a. c.** must be movable; however, the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.** must never deviate from each other at point **a.**, but must remain fixed together.

And note, when I speak here of points or lines, I do not mean only those points and lines provided by <term>Mathematics<note>original: "Mathematica". Dürer is distinguishing between abstract Euclidean geometry and the practical, physical lines used by artists.</note></term>—even though those same invisible, indivisible points and lines are contained or intended herein. Rather, I speak here of such points and lines that are dotted and drawn with a needle or sharp pen against a <term>straightedge<note>German: *richtscheit*.</note></term>, made visible and understandable to the eye.

And when you wish to use this instrument, prepare the tool as follows: Draw the aforementioned line **a. b.** crosswise upon a suitable flat board, and drive a sharp needle into point **a.** firmly and securely. Tie a clean, twisted silk thread to it, stretch it out to the end of **c.**, and use it in place of the movable line **a. c.**, for the thread is easily moved back and forth.

When this is thus prepared, then draw a straight upright line **d. e.** from the middle of the transverse line **a. b.** at right angles upward.

Upon this line **d. e.**, the chosen part—whether it be length, thickness, or breadth—which you then wish to <gloss>change</gloss> must be placed so that one end touches the transverse line **a. b.** below. Thereafter...

<summary>Dürer describes the construction of a physical "transformer" tool made of a board, a needle, and a silk thread, used to proportionally scale different parts of the human figure.</summary>
<keywords>transformer, verkerer, proportions, measurement, geometric instruments, straightedge, Albrecht Dürer, drafting techniques</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for geometric transformation, moving from the general concept of the "transformer" to the specific construction and use of a physical scaling tool called the "selector."</meta>

Take the thread that is there in place of line **a. c.** and stretch it out, so that it touches the part at the top which you have placed on line **d. e.** Now, lengthen your intended part as much as you wish and place this same lengthened line with one end on the transverse line <note>The horizontal baseline.</note> **a. b.** at equal angles to line **d. e.** toward the end **b.**; move it upright until it touches the stretched thread **a. c.** at the top. Let this upright line be **f. g.** If, however, you wish to have the part shorter than the one you placed on line **d. e.**, then set your intended shorter line upright at equal angles on the transverse line **a. b.** behind the upright **d. e.** toward point **a.**; move it also upright on line **a. b.** until it touches the stretched thread **a. c.** at the top. Let this upright line be **h. i.** 

Thus, in this instrument, there are three upright parallel lines next to each other: on line **d. e.** is placed the first described length, but on line **f. g.** the lengthened part is taken, and from line **h. i.** the shortened part. Therefore, as a part is placed on line **d. e.**, the stretched thread **a. c.**—where it touches the height of line **d. e.**—measures comparable longer and shorter things on both upright lines **f. g.** and **h. i.**, and effectively transforms nearly anything one desires. Much can be done with this instrument, for whether one moves the thread **a. c.** high or low, it creates comparable parts against each other, whether in long, short, or medium-sized things; and this remains true even if the parts are uncountable in the numerical value one wishes to measure, such as when one cannot say "this is a 1/2 part, a 1/3 part, or a 1/5, a 1/6" and so on, as was said before.

I have drawn this plan of mine described above hereafter so that everyone may properly follow it and see it before their eyes. For this reason, I also call this instrument the <term>selector<note>German: *weler*. Likely derived from *wählen* (to choose), this tool allows the artist to "select" or dial in the desired proportion for scaling figures.</note></term> so that it may be known, because from it one can select <note>original: "zu weler ist"</note> whatever one wants, whether it be much or little. From such a selector, the thinner man and the thicker man were later made and drawn, as you see according to the figure of the selector. In this transformation, it should be noted that for every thing made in this way, the more it enters into the longer, thicker, wider, and larger parts, the further all things move apart in their measurements; and the smaller the parts are taken, the closer the things run together. Therefore, the thinner image changed by the <term>selector</term> will be much smoother <note>original: "linder," meaning more delicate or subtle in its proportional transitions.</note> than the enlarged image. This process described above can also be performed using a quadrant, as the compass arcs <note>original: *zirkelriß*</note> in the <term>selector</term> indicate.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates the 'Selector' (a proportional scaling tool), showing the triangulation between various points marked 'a' through 'i'. It features vertical segments of varying heights that correspond to arcs and a central diagonal line, demonstrating how a single measurement can be scaled up or down proportionally.</note>

->Let this instrument be called the selector.<-

<summary>This page describes the construction and use of the "selector" (weler), a geometric tool for proportionally scaling figures and objects, explaining how a thread and parallel lines can be used to increase or decrease dimensions accurately.</summary>
<keywords>selector, weler, geometric instrument, proportional scaling, technical drawing, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance geometry, measurement tools</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for modifying figures after they have been processed through the geometric scaling tool. The previous page established how to use the "selector" to lengthen or shorten a figure; this page focuses on the aesthetic adjustments (particularly to the head and feet) required to keep the proportions looking natural after such a transformation.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>

To this lengthened man, one quarter of his own length is to be added. And if one wishes to use him, one must make his head a little shorter, because the elongation gives him a deformed appearance. Therefore, one should shift the chin upward and re-divide the face, or one should remove the top of the head and re-divide the face again using the **selector** <note>original: "Weier" — a proportional scaling tool or "gate" used by Dürer to calculate changes in height and width.</note>. I am doing it here so that it touches the highest point of the height, and I make the length from the highest part of the crown of the head to the end of the chin two 15th-parts <note>2/15ths of the total body height.</note>, and from the end of the chin upward to the end of the forehead one 10th-part <note>1/10th of the body height.</note>.

Afterward, I divide the face lengthways again using the selector; however, it retains the thinness and narrowness as it was formed by the selector, just as I have drawn such faces according to my opinion next to the figures. I also make his foot two 13th-parts <note>2/13ths of the body height.</note> long, as it had become too short through the selector; I also make the instep lower as is proper. Especially for the sake of beauty and elegance, the thighs should be drawn a little thinner and narrower; I have indicated in the following drawings exactly where I mean for this to happen.

And this man is one 5th-part thinner than the first man described in the first booklet from which he was transformed. I have drawn how the head and everything else I mean should be shaped, so that you may see for yourself the difference I propose between the first-described man and the other man derived from him.

<note>A calligraphic knot ornament, known as *Schlingwerk*, is a decorative woodcut flourish typical of Albrecht Dürer’s printing style. These ornaments reflect the artist's interest in the intersection of mathematical precision and artistic decoration, often used to fill space or mark transitions between sections.</note>

It is further to be noted how this shorter man was made; understand it thus: In the **transformer** <note>original: "Verkerer" — the specific diagram used to invert or scale proportions.</note> where I placed and took the shorter line **d. e.**, I drew two straight lines from both ends of the lengthened line **f. g.**, crossing in the middle through the upright line **a. b.** Where their ends then intersect the two lines **e. f.** and **c. g.**, the straight line **d. e.** is drawn between them, as spoken of before.

This shortened man, if he is drawn back to his original length, becomes very thick, as the selector dictates. (However, if one wishes, one should reduce the head's thickness and breadth so that it remains human-looking if one intends to use such a figure.) And make the foot a 6th-part long again. All such things are drawn hereafter; it remains at your discretion to make these parts longer or shorter as you wish.

<summary>Dürer explains how to aesthetically adjust the head, feet, and thighs of a figure after it has been geometrically scaled, ensuring the transformed "lengthened" or "shortened" man remains visually pleasing.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Proportionslehre, human proportion, geometric scaling, Weier, selector, anatomy, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific anatomical measurements of the human head to the geometric principles used to alter those proportions. It builds upon the "squaring" method established previously, applying the mathematical concept of transforming a cube while maintaining its relative volume.</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'A' is embellished with intricate foliate ornamentation. This style is characteristic of early 16th-century German book arts, often seen in the works of Albrecht Dürer and his contemporaries, where even functional instructional texts were elevated with artistic flourishes.</note>

From the words "Long" and "Short," I wish to demonstrate again how one may take the man’s head—which was divided and described by crossed lines within its own particular cubic squaring <note>original: "vierung," referring to the grid system or framing used to define proportions</note> in the first book—and, assuming it is contained within a cube <note>original: "cubo," the Latin term for a three-dimensional square</note>, show how that same cube may be stretched (that is, heightened and compressed). Furthermore, one should know that if it is stretched, how much thinner and narrower it must become; or if it is pressed down, how much thicker and wider it must become, in order to maintain the same volumetric content <note>The author is describing a method of preserving the "weight" or volume of the head even when the proportions are distorted—a precursor to modern computer-assisted "stretching" of images.</note> in measure and weight. To do this, you must proceed as follows:

I take before me a proper, four-sided cube, equal on all sides, which is as high as the aforementioned man’s head; for the head must be contained within this cube. Now, I must first deal with the cube itself before anything is described or drawn within it. Let it be at each person's discretion how much they wish to heighten, stretch, or press down the cube; and so, begin.

Take one side of the proper cube; I mark the bottom **a.** and the top **b.** This line **a. b.** I heighten by an end-point **c.** as much as I want the cube to be higher than it was before. Thereafter, I take the side **a. b.** and the lengthened line **a. c.** and make from them a right-angled square <term>Winckelhacken|A tool similar to a carpenter's square or a set square used to ensure perfect 90-degree angles.</term>, so that **a. c.** is horizontal <note>original: "vber zwerch"</note> and **a. b.** is upright, with **a.** forming the corner. Then, the horizontal line **a. c.** is extended out past the corner **a.** to an end-point **d.**, so that **d. a. c.** becomes a stretched horizontal line. After this, a diagonal line <note>original: "ort lini"</note> **b. c.** is drawn.

Next, I take a proper set square—be it made of brass or wood—and place one side of it against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the set square touches point **b.** Then, I draw a line at a right angle straight down from point **b.** where the square lies. Where this right-angled line intersects the horizontal line **d. a. c.**, I place a point **e.** Once this is done, a proportional line has been found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**; this is the line **a. e.**

Now you must find another middle line between **a. b.** and **a. e.** To do this: Take the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** and join them together to make one long horizontal line **e. a. b.** Then, from point **a.**, draw a vertical line upwards at a right angle. Next, take a compass <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and place one foot in the center of this joined line **e. a. b.**, and place the other foot on point **e.** Draw a circle from there, rounding upward until it reaches point **b.** Where this circular arc intersects the vertical line drawn up from point **a.**, place an **f.** This is the width of one side of the heightened cube. Thus, **a. c.** is its height, and **a. f.** is its width.

However, if you wish to have the cube lower than its original side **a. b.**, it must become wider. This is done in the same manner as before, but in reverse. Just as the side of the cube **a. b.** was previously heightened by point **c.**, it must now be shortened by that same point **c.** To find this widening, proceed thus: Take the side of the first cube **a. b.** and stand it upright before you. As mentioned before, join the shorter line **a. c.** to it at a right angle horizontally. Then, extend the horizontal line **c. a.** out past point **a.** to an end-point **d.** Draw the diagonal line **b. c.** and take a set square again. Place one side against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the set square touches point **b.**, and draw a right-angled line from point **b.** through the horizontal line **d. a. c.** Where this line intersects, place a point **e.** Thus, the first middle line is found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**; this is the line **a. e.**

<summary>This page explains the geometric method for distorting human proportions by treating the head as a cube that can be stretched or compressed while maintaining a consistent volume. It provides step-by-step instructions for using a compass and set square to find the proportional width of a head when its height is changed.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, proportions, cube transformation, Albrecht Dürer, squaring, anatomical drawing, mathematical art, perspective, Renaissance techniques</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for transforming the proportions of a human head by altering the cube that contains it. Having established the "long" and "short" cubes on the previous page, the author now provides the specific Euclidean construction for determining the new width or height to ensure the head remains proportionally consistent.</meta>

P ij

Now you must find the other middle line <note>This refers to finding the geometric mean, a mathematical method used to find a value that mediates between two different proportions.</note> between **a.b.** and **a.e.** To do so, proceed as follows: Take the two lines **a.b.** and **a.e.** and join them together end-to-end in a straight line; this combined line is **e.a.b.** Afterward, draw a vertical line straight up from point **a** at right angles. Then, take a compass <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and place one foot in the center of this horizontal line **e.a.b.**, and with the other foot, draw a circular arc starting from point **e** over the top until it reaches point **b.** Where this circular arc intersects the vertical line that was drawn upward from point **a**, mark that point as **f.** This line **a.f.** is the length of one side of the expanded, compressed cube <note>original: "nider gedruckten cubus"</note>. Thus, its height is **a.c.**, as is clearly drawn hereafter in the figures.

<note>A faint geometric construction diagram shows a square with arcs and diagonal lines. This serves as a technical guide for the reader to visualize how to use a compass to find the "middle line" or geometric mean between two different lengths, which allows an artist to squash or stretch a shape while maintaining its mathematical "weight."</note>

It is also noteworthy that in every cube—whether heightened or compressed—the form of the face, which is drawn and divided by crosswise lines, becomes accordingly long or short.

<note>A decorative woodcut ornament consists of three intertwined circles forming a complex knot-like pattern, a common Renaissance motif known as "entrelac" or knotwork.</note>

It should also be noted that if you wish to draw the front-facing face <note>original: "fürſichtig angeſicht"</note> into its cube, it must be made narrower by a fifth part of its width, according to the measure given. Or, you may do it this way: Take the width of the elongated or compressed cube, and set eight of these widths end-to-end. Divide these eight parts into ten equal parts. Use one of those ten parts to set the width of the cube for the front-facing face, once the narrowing has been applied in the long and short cubes. Then, first divide the lines again using the <term>weier<note>The 'weier' (likely a variant of 'Weiger' or 'balance') refers to a proportional scale or a specific method of transferring measurements between different grids.</note></term> and draw the form of the face into it. What has been described here regarding the head is to be used for the entire body.

<meta>This page continues from the previous demonstration of standard proportions. Dürer here introduces "deformation" or systematic distortion, showing how a single set of measurements can be adapted into various physical types (e.g., top-heavy or bottom-heavy) using geometric transformations of the underlying grid.</meta>

<note>An ornate decorative initial 'E' features floral scrollwork and leaf-like flourishes, marking the start of this section of text.</note>

**E**ven further changes can be made within the <term>cube</term> <note>original: "cubo"; Dürer uses this term to refer to the three-dimensional geometric grid or bounding box containing the head</note> (through which the head is given a different form) according to the following principle: that the <term>cube</term> is expanded equally on all sides at the top <term>plane</term> <note>original: "plano"; a flat surface or face of the geometric grid</note> or flat surface, and by as much as it is leaned out at the top, it should be drawn in just as much and equally at the bottom on the lower <term>plane</term>.

Afterwards, one does the opposite of this. Now that the <term>cube</term> has been transformed in these two ways—the first time wide at the top and narrow at the bottom, and conversely narrow at the top and wide at the bottom—I then draw the straight lines of the head described previously back into the further changed [grid], along with the contour lines of the face. In this way, two types of faces are created: the first head becomes large at the top and small at the bottom, but the other head becomes small at the top and large at the bottom. One may use such <insert>methods</insert> for the whole body.

And just as the transformation is applied to the <term>cube</term> at the top and bottom, so should one use it in the <term>cube</term> at the front and back; for if the <term>cube</term> is expanded on its front <term>plane</term>, it is drawn in by that same amount on its back <term>plane</term>. The same applies if one does the opposite <note>original: "widersins"; meaning in reverse or contrary fashion</note>.

<note>Four coordinate-grid diagrams illustrate the distortion of human head proportions in both profile and frontal views. These grids show tapering and widening effects to demonstrate how anatomical features scale when the underlying geometry is transformed. Labels include numeric values 8 and 10 and alphabetical coordinates 'a' through 'n'. This reflects Dürer's pioneering work in what would later be called morphometrics.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to systematically distort human proportions by manipulating the geometric grid (cube) that contains the drawing, creating varied facial types by widening or narrowing the top, bottom, front, or back planes.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometric transformation, facial distortion, coordinate grids, Renaissance anatomy, anthropometry</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s explanation of how to systematically distort the human head. Having previously discussed tilting the front and back planes of the "cube" (the bounding box of the head), he now explains the visual results of these transformations and introduces diagonal "rhombus" distortions.</meta>

<sig>P iiij</sig>
<warning>Slight foxing and water staining on the right-hand margin.</warning>

...and then, if the straight lines along with the shape of the face are drawn back in proportionately through the <term>selector</term> <note>original: "weler"; a tool or mathematical method Dürer used to redistribute points from a square grid into a distorted one</note>, the face in the first <term>cube</term> <note>original: "cubo"; Dürer uses this to mean the three-dimensional geometric grid containing the head</note> will become large in the front and small in the back. Conversely, in the other <term>cube</term>, the face will become small in the front and the head large in the back, just as I have drawn out below.

One may also use such a transformation by letting the <term>plane</term> <note>original: "plano"</note> on the <term>cube</term> remain level at the top or bottom, rather than slanted, as is also shown in the drawing below; such methods may be used in many ways.

Another transformation to use with the <term>cube</term> is to make the two side-faces into the shape of a <term>rhombus</term> <note>original: "rauten weyß"; literally "diamond-wise"</note>. This happens when two diagonal angles are made narrow, while the other two diagonal angles are made wide. From this, you may first place one of the narrow angles at the front by the crown of the head and the other at the back by the neck; thus, a wide angle remains at the back of the head and the other at the front by the chin. One then does the opposite of this, as is also drawn below. The first head gains a pointed forehead, but the other has a high head at the back. These heads do not retain the same volume as the first head described in the first book.

Every depicted thing in a <term>cube</term> is transformed as often as the <term>cube</term> itself is transformed, provided that it is redistributed proportionately through the transformer or <term>selector</term>.

<insert>
**These** next distorted faces are transformed along with the entire <term>cubo</term> <note>Latin: "cube." Dürer uses this to refer to the three-dimensional grid or "bounding box" containing the head.</note>; yet all things are redistributed through the <term>transformer</term> <note>original: "verkerer"</note> or <term>scaler</term> <note>original: "weier." A technical term Dürer uses for a proportional transfer tool or method.</note> from the first described head, comparable yet altered. And therefore I will proceed with the first described face, though leaving its body as it was before. But the division of the differences in the face I will change through the shifting of the <term>horizontal</term> <note>original: "zwerch"</note>, vertical, and diagonal lines, thereby showing that one may also use this in the faces previously distorted with the <term>cube</term>, and how so many various forms of faces may be made. Such ways and means are infinitely many; therefore, different forms of people can be made always and as often as one wishes. Now we want to take up again the first described face of the first book, and treat it from the side with the <term>horizontal lines</term>, through which all things are distinguished according to their length. And primarily there are three of these that distinguish the largest parts. Therefore, it is to be noted that the top line *k.* first touches the top of the forehead; the second line *l.* goes through the <term>eyebrows</term> <note>original: "augpraen"</note> and touches the <term>ears</term> <note>original: "orn"</note>; and line *m.* indicates the end of the nose. If one now shifts these lines up or down, away from or toward each other, they create high or low fields in between; for what the horizontal lines take from one field, they give to another. Through such shifting, you may make shorter or longer whichever part of the face you wish. And in the same way that you may shift these three specific lines *k. l. m.* here and there, you may also shift the other horizontal lines *s. r. q. p. o.* between them, so that they remain straight lines; or you may shift them a little diagonally, slanting, or crooked, and draw them contrary to one another, whereby the fields between them become wide at one part and narrow at another. Thus, you may shift everything that can make something long, short, wide, narrow, thick, or thin, equal or unequal, according to your will; for every horizontal line can be shifted specifically in three ways: upward, downward, or diagonally. All horizontal lines may also be shifted upward or downward at once, or one half upward and the other half downward, or randomly as one wishes. The same is also to be done with the vertical lines. And whatever is changed and is to be drawn comparably again from the first face into a <term>distorted one</term> <note>original: "verkertß"</note>, that must happen through the <term>transformer</term> or <term>scaler</term>. Therefore, whoever seeks in these things will find rare and wonderful things. And according to the form of your intended face, so must you direct the straight lines that shall provide such change.
</insert>

For as said before, when the <term>horizontal lines</term> are shifted close together, whatever is made therein will become short; but when they are shifted far from each other, whatever is made between them will become long. But such occurs between the vertical...

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'D' is used to begin the main paragraph, featuring intricate floral and vine motifs characteristic of Renaissance printing.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to create infinite variations of the human face by shifting the horizontal, vertical, and diagonal lines of a coordinate grid, emphasizing that changing the space between lines directly alters the length and width of facial features.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "Verkehrung" (transformation) method. Having previously discussed the general concept of the "cube" or bounding box, Dürer now moves into the specific application of shifting horizontal grid lines to alter the vertical proportions of the face while maintaining the vertical structure.</meta>

...the lines of the side of the face, both thick and thin. Therefore, whoever takes these things into hand to use them will surely perceive what strange variations it is possible for him to create.

Now I wish to put part of what was mentioned above into practice; take the first described man’s head from the first little book <note>Dürer refers back to the standard proportions established in the first book of his treatise.</note>. Leave all the vertical lines as they are, and work first with the <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note>. Move the three lines **k. l. m.** between **i.** and **n.** so that they create four equal fields in which the largest division of the face is contained. After that, one redistributes the other lines **o. l. p. q. r. s.** proportionally using the <term>scaler</term> <note>original: "weler," a proportional transfer tool or ruler used to maintain relative distances when the overall scale changes.</note>. This seems to me to create a good form, because the features of the face are thereby evenly divided. Therefore, I have below drawn such a <term>transformed face</term> <note>original: "verkerts angesicht," referring to a head whose proportions have been mathematically distorted.</note> with its lines from the side, and you should take care to follow it.

Furthermore, it should be noted that heads sometimes have long or short faces; this is to be understood in this way: If the face in the head is very long, then the head—wherever it is covered with hair above the forehead—becomes very low. Therefore, I will set out such a long face by placing line **k.** upon line **i.**, and after that, the other lines **o. l. p. q. m. r. s.** shall be proportionally redistributed between **k.** and **n.** using the <term>scaler</term>. In this way, the hair growth at the top is entirely removed.

However, if it is intended to make the face short, then that same head gains a high, pointed top. Therefore, next to the previously made head with the long face, I will place such a one with a short face and draw it in its form as I describe hereafter. Take the forehead line **k.** and move it down between **i.** and **n.** by one third; and after that, redistribute the other horizontal lines **o. l. p. q. m. r. s.** proportionally using the <term>scaler</term>, just as it is placed before your eyes here.

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of how to manipulate the standard head proportions. While the previous page focused on shifting individual horizontal lines, this section introduces the concept of distorting the entire bounding box (the "square frame") into a trapezoidal shape to create diverse facial profiles.</meta>

HOWEVER, there is such a transformation where the <term>square frame</term> <note>original: "gefiert corpus," referring to the geometric bounding box or grid that contains the drawing.</note> in which the <term>head</term> <note>original: "haubt"</note> is described is made narrow in the front and wide in the back; and as much as is taken from the front, just as much must be added to the back.

One does the same in reverse. If one then draws all things anew into this distorted <term>square frame</term> proportionally as they should be, two <term>faces</term> <note>original: "angesicht"</note> will result from it, as I have <term>sketched</term> <note>original: "auffgerissen"</note> them both below in two printed <term>ground plans</term> <note>original: "grunden"</note>.

Now note further that all <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> in the head are to be shifted by means of the <term>distorter</term> <note>original: "felscher," a technical term Dürer uses for a proportional scale or device used to "falsify" or alter the standard measurements.</note> which will be described hereafter; so that the fields between them extend upwards and become shorter downwards. One also does this in reverse, or in the half-part of the head, either above or below, or between whichever horizontal lines one chooses.

This also brings great differences in the <term>shifting</term> <note>original: "verruckung"</note> of the lines in the opposite direction. If you now shift a line downwards which you previously shifted upwards—and this should also be observed for the <term>vertical lines</term> <note>original: "auffrechten linien"</note>, to shift them forward or backward—it creates significant variation. Therefore, I will further describe several faces, showing how my aforementioned opinion should be put into practice. By this, I expect to provide cause for much further thought on what might yet be created beyond what is indicated here; however, I will use certain shifts here as follows.

<note>Four diagrams illustrate the geometric distortion of a human head profile using a trapezoidal grid system. These woodcuts demonstrate Albrecht Dürer’s method of perspective or proportional shifting, a landmark in the mathematical study of art and anatomy from his 1528 work *Four Books on Human Proportion*.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to create facial variations by distorting the grid (the 'square frame') into trapezoids and shifting horizontal and vertical lines using a specialized scaling tool.</summary>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Having previously discussed how to distort the entire "square frame" or grid of a head, he now moves into "shifting" specific horizontal lines to create variety in facial features, such as making a forehead longer or a nose shorter, before introducing the concept of "broken lines" to further manipulate the profile.</meta>

First, it is our intention that we should completely shift the face we previously described by moving the <term>horizontal lines</term> <note>original: "zwerch linien." These are the grid lines Dürer uses to mark the features: the top of the head, the brow, the base of the nose, and the chin.</note> so that no part remains in its original place. We may wish to create a long or a short forehead, a long or a short nose, or a long or a short chin. 

If it happens that you push the horizontal line **l** <note>In Dürer’s system, line 'l' usually marks the brow line.</note> significantly upward toward line **k**, then a short forehead is created between them, and the nose is lengthened between **l** and **m**. However, if you push line **l** downward toward line **m**, you will have a long forehead between **k** and **l** and a short nose between **l** and **m**. But if you let line **l** stand still and push the horizontal line **m** <note>Line 'm' typically marks the base of the nose.</note> upward, a short nose is again created between **l** and **m**, but the mouth and chin will become long. If line **m** is pushed downward, a long nose is created between **l** and **m**, and the mouth and chin become short. 

Now, the two lines **k** and **m** can be pushed far apart from each other—so that line **k** is near line **i** and line **m** is near line **n**. In this case, the height of the head <note>The space at the very top.</note> and the length of the mouth and chin remain short, but the forehead and nose become very long. If, however, you push the two horizontal lines **k** and **m** close together, the height of the head becomes long, as do the mouth and chin, but the forehead and nose become very short. 

If you push **k** and **l** high upward, you will have a short top of the head and a short forehead. After that, if you divide the space between **l** and **n** equally again with the other horizontal lines using the <term>distorter</term> <note>original: "verkerer," a specialized proportional scale Dürer invented to mathematically alter dimensions.</note>, a long nose, mouth, and chin will result. You may also do the exact opposite of this, which will give you a high top of the head and a long forehead, with a short nose, mouth, and chin. 

But consider another shifting: push line **k** near line **i**, and line **m** near line **l**, and let line **l** stand still. From this results a low top of the head, a long forehead, a short nose, and a long mouth and chin. Do the opposite of this: push line **k** near line **l**, and line **m** near line **n**; this results in a high top of the head, a short forehead, a long nose, and a short mouth and chin. There is much that could be shown in such matters, which is omitted here for the sake of brevity. Also, in this "giving and taking" <note>An idiom for the proportional adjustments.</note>, one must take care to shift the lines so that nature is not forced too much, so that it remains human. 

Whoever wishes to put such things into practice should know that as you have dealt with the three lines **k**, **l**, and **m**, you may also do with the other horizontal lines **o**, **p**, **q**, **r**, and **s**. Now, as we have divided these things according to our will to make them long or short through the horizontal lines, the features are changed from the first intended <term>square frame</term> <note>original: "vierung." Dürer starts with a perfect square grid and then distorts it.</note>, yet they remain within the system of lines. One may also draw these same lines from the <term>corners</term> <note>original: "ort linien," referring to diagonal or angular lines connecting points on the grid.</note>—frontwards-upwards, backwards-downwards, and vice-versa—or leave some parts straight and make others curved. 

One may also shift the previously described corner lines of the first head at the nose however one wishes. One may also "break" the straight lines, either upwards or downwards; and in the vertical lines, one may break them forwards or backwards. The same is done with the corner lines. In some parts, you may turn the lines wherever you wish. You may also slide the fields between the horizontal lines—in which the lines are broken—forwards or backwards, upwards or downwards. Everyone will find what I am talking about through practice.

To help you understand what I call a <term>broken line</term> <note>original: "gebrochne lini." This refers to a line that is not a single straight stroke but is "bent" at a specific point to shift part of the drawing.</note>, mark my words: Let a given line be **a—b**. Place a point **c** wherever you wish on it. At that point, break the line apart and push one half-part upwards or downwards. Or, place two points on the aforementioned line, such as **c** and **d**. Break that segment **c—d** out and move it upwards or downwards. In the same way, the vertical lines can be broken forwards or backwards, and likewise with corner lines. Therefore, I will explain how the broken corner lines are used in changing the face. However, look below at the figure where I have sketched the broken line mentioned above, and after that, the distorted faces described just now.

-> a ——— c ——— b <-
-> a —— c — g — d —— b <-
