<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s systematic exploration of facial proportions. Building on the previous page's demonstration of shifting horizontal grid lines to alter the forehead and nose length, Dürer now introduces the concepts of "breaking" (bending) and "inclining" (shifting) these lines to create more complex and varied facial profiles.</meta>

<note>A decorative calligraphic initial "A" (transcribed as V) begins the section.</note> **AND** as previously indicated in the first chapter of the first book, the horizontal <note>original: "zwerch"</note> and vertical <note>original: "auffrechten"</note> lines are drawn as straight lines. These lines are altered once more when one "breaks" them <note>Dürer uses "break" (brechen) to mean bending a line at a specific point or joint to change its direction.</note> and also when one creates "inclined" lines <note>original: "ober ort"; Dürer's technical term for shifting or tilting a line or plane from its standard axis.</note> from them. Therefore, the two terms "broken" and "inclined" must always be kept in mind wherever they are needed. 

Now, I will first use the term "inclined" for all horizontal and vertical lines: I either move each horizontal line backward or forward, upward or downward. Similarly, I move the vertical lines either at the top or bottom, forward or backward. Through this, however, they all remain straight. For that reason, you must take each of the aforementioned lines and specifically curve them into a bow or a serpentine shape, upward or downward, forward or backward; "break" each line wherever it is necessary for your use. 

We shall begin with the forehead line, drawing it upward or downward at the back or front. Similarly, the line of the eyebrows <note>original: "augbraen"</note> should be "broken" at its location for the length of the eyebrows, and drawn either upward or downward at both ends, or "broken" in the middle above the eyes and the break drawn upward or downward. For you may curve the eyes upward or downward according to each case; this is also to be done with the corners of the eyes: if the length is "broken" out of the line, you may turn them with the front or back corners upward or downward, just as a needle <note>original: "zünglein," literally "little tongue"</note> is turned in a compass. You may also make the inner or outer corners of the eyes deeper or shallower.

Likewise, if the nose line is "broken," its back tips <note>original: "zipffelein"</note> may be drawn upward or downward. The same is to be done with the mouth. Similarly, with the jawbone <note>original: "kinbacken"</note>, it may be drawn upward or downward at the back. It is also very distinct to see from the side how the neck is placed forward on the head near the chin, and conversely, "inclined" far backward from the chin; thus the head becomes almost upright, while the former leans toward the earth. 

Furthermore, we have the ears to shift within their square frame <note>original: "vierung"</note>: upward, downward, forward, backward, or "inclined" to whichever side you wish. You may also make the square of the ear long, short, wide, or narrow, large or small. Some ears lie flat against the head, while others protrude far out. From such instructions, all straight things can be drawn inclined, curved, oblique <note>original: "schlem"</note>, and angular. From this, you will find how you can make all heights lower, thicknesses thinner, and flat things oblique. When the shape of the face is then drawn back in between such pre-determined altered lines, the appearance of the previous shape is entirely changed. 

In all of this, one must take very specific note of the words of distinction described at the beginning. From them, one finds that there are three types of faces to be seen from the side: namely, a flat, even face <note>original: "schletz"</note>; an outward-curving one like the heads of hares <note>A convex profile.</note>; and an inward-curving one <note>A concave profile.</note>. Such inward or outward bending may be drawn through curved, straight, or "broken" lines.

<margin>·</margin> In this, "giving and taking" <note>An artistic principle of proportional compensation or adjustment.</note> is used. These three types of faces can be changed in infinite ways; one may also make these three types of faces sharp and highly prominent, or flat. However, to demonstrate my writing, I have sketched my meaning of such three faces hereafter.

<note>A decorative calligraphic flourish or knot ornament marks the end of the text on this page.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric manipulation of facial features by "breaking" (bending) or "inclining" (shifting) the grid lines for the forehead, eyes, nose, and jaw. He categorizes facial profiles into three main types: flat, convex (hare-like), and concave.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry, profile types, broken lines, inclined lines, convex vs concave, artistic theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s exploration of geometric transformations. Having previously discussed "breaking" or bending the proportional lines of the face, he now moves into "inclining" or skewing the entire coordinate grid to create profiles that appear to lean forward or backward.</meta>

### An even face. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; An outward-curved face. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; And an inward-curved face.

<note>The image shows three woodcut diagrams of human facial profiles within regular grids, demonstrating even, outward-curved, and inward-curved variations. These illustrate how Dürer used geometric transformation to categorize different facial structures by bowing the vertical lines of the grid.</note>

---

There are still two other kinds of faces to be viewed from the side, which are to be shifted in their grid <note>original: "bierung"; Dürer's term for the proportional square or squaring of the face</note> in this way: The two horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> of the grid at the bottom and top remain straight horizontal lines in both shifts; however, both grids must be shifted into a rhombus shape <note>original: "rautens weiß"; skewed into a diamond-like parallelogram</note>. 

Understand it thus: The first grid is pulled forward with its upper front corner, and pulled backward with its lower back corner. One does the opposite for the second. Afterward, one redistributes all the previously described parts along the straight horizontal lines. But when one redistributes the vertical lines, they become entirely slanted lines <note>original: "ort lini"; diagonal or inclined lines</note> along both sides, which in the first case hang forward, and in the second hang backward. Then, one draws the outline of the face back into it; thus you will find in both grids what becomes of them, just as I have plotted <note>original: "auffgerissen"</note> these faces immediately after the three faces mentioned before.

### A forward-leaning face &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; A backward-leaning face.

<note>Two woodcut diagrams show facial profiles within skewed rhombic grids to illustrate "leaning forward" and "leaning backward" perspective distortions. This demonstrates how a standard face changes appearance when the underlying coordinate system is tilted or "inclined."</note>

**Vocabulary:** <term>Grid</term> (Bierung), <term>horizontal lines</term> (zwerch linien), <term>rhombus-style</term> (rautens weiß), <term>face</term> (angesicht), <term>plotted/drawn</term> (auffgerissen), <term>leaning forward</term> (für sich hangent), <term>leaning backward</term> (hindersich hangent)

It demonstrates Dürer's mathematical approach to creating variety in human features through coordinate geometry.</summary>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Following his discussion on shifting the entire grid into a rhombus shape (as seen on the previous page), he now explains how to manipulate individual vertical and horizontal lines to create specific facial variations, moving from abstract geometry to the anatomical details of the forehead, nose, and head shape.</meta>

<sig>Q iij</sig>

In these shifted faces, all vertical and horizontal lines can also be moved, just as was permitted in the others. Further instruction is needed on how one can work with the vertical lines to distinguish the shape of the face. Since the two sides of the square for the side profile are the front vertical line **a** and the back line **b**, and the six other lines between them—**c, d, e, f, g, h**—divide it into seven equal fields, these can be shifted according to one's will just as well as the horizontal lines. Each field between the vertical lines can be moved individually, backward or forward, as can several or all of them together.

And in the same way as was shown before regarding making things long or short with the horizontal lines, so it is with the vertical lines to make things thick, thin, broad, or narrow; here there is no lack of "giving and taking" <note>This is Dürer's phrase for adding to or subtracting from a measurement to change a proportion.</note>. And as I do with straight lines, so one also does with curved, crooked, and diagonal lines <note>original: "ort linien"; lines that slant or follow an angle rather than a strict vertical/horizontal axis</note>. If one wishes to use such things, one must pay attention. First, on the forehead: whatever is taken from its projection <note>its outward bulge</note> should be added to the height of the nose. This is also done in the reverse. This can happen through outward or inward curves, or straight-angled lines; thus it is done in all parts throughout the entire face when viewed from the side.

Therefore, opposing elements in the transformation must always be placed together. This happens based on what was said above: if you push one part forward, you then push the other part above or below it backward. This then makes a part thick or broad, thin or narrow.

Now, when the transformation is made in the head as a whole—whether through vertical, upper diagonal, or angled lines between the horizontal lines—one must also note that in each individual small part of the face, the terms **level**, **outward-curved**, or **inward-curved** are to be used. This applies to the specific parts just as it did to the three distinct faces <note>Referencing the "even," "outward," and "inward" face types established previously.</note>. From this, it follows that you can make a flat, outward-curved, or inward-curved forehead; the same is to be done with the nose, lips, mouth, and chin, and in all other things.

It should be noted that the line by which the roundness of the head is made, when seen from the side, can be drawn in many ways. For some have a flat head, others a pointed one, others an angular one, and so forth in many varieties, just as nature provides many kinds.

Now we wish to speak further of the diagonal line <note>original: "ort lini"</note> which is drawn because of the nose. It can be leaned backward as far as the vertical line **d**, or it can be drawn vertically in the middle at **c**, or it can be leaned forward as far as the vertical line **a**. But in such matters, I consider the balanced <note>original: "vergleychlichen"; proportional or harmonious</note> things to be the most beautiful. Although the deviant <note>original: "abgeschidnen"; literally "departed" from the norm</note> things bring wonder <note>or curiosity</note>, they are not all pleasing. Therefore, an example should be taken from the aforementioned indications: one might say that neither a pointed head nor a flat head is well-formed, but a round head is considered handsome because it is a middle ground between the others. By this, it is not proven that every middle ground between all things is the best; I only suggest this to be used in certain things. For instance, when one says: "That is a face that is too long or too short," or similarly with its parts: "That is a forehead that is too long, short, bulging, or hollowed."

The same is to be done with the nose, for some have large, hooked, long, and overhanging noses. On the other hand, some have entirely short or snub <note>original: "murret"; likely referring to a short, flattened, or "grumpy" looking nose</note> noses, upturned, thick, or bulbous...

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate the vertical grid lines of a profile to vary facial features like the forehead and nose, while arguing that a "round" head represents a beautiful middle ground between extremes like flat or pointed shapes.</summary>
<keywords>geometric transformation, facial proportions, head shapes, aesthetic theory, Albrecht Dürer, profile drawing, anatomical variation</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical instructions on how to manipulate the facial grid to create a vast variety of human features. Having previously discussed shifting the entire grid, he now focuses on how changing specific lines alters individual features like the eyes, mouth, and chin.</meta>

<sig>i</sig>

Sometimes the space between the eyes is pressed deep inward, or they are set high and flush with the forehead. Furthermore, some people have deep, tiny eyes, or high, large, bulging <note>original: "boltzete"; literally "bolt-like" or protruding</note> eyes; some open their eyes narrowly like a pig, and sometimes pull their lower lid further up than the upper lid is pulled down. There are also those who tear their eyes open as wide as circles, so that the entire eyeball is visible. Then there are some whose eyebrows are raised high above the eyes; for others, the brows lie right upon them or hang over them; and some eyebrows are thin while others are thick.

Furthermore, some have thick, high, club-like <note>original: "kolbete"; refers to a rounded, heavy shape</note> mouths, or thin lips that look as if they are bitten-in; and for some, the upper lip extends past the lower, and vice-versa, and often one lip is thicker than the other. One person has a long lip <note>the space between the nose and the mouth</note> starting from the nose, while another has a short one. Many a man has a thick, broad, large chin, while another has a small, pointed one; for some, the chin is set far back and slopes away toward the neck, while for others it projects far forward from the neck; and some are long while others are short, just as was mentioned above when describing how the horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> create these effects.

Further, some faces are "staff-like" <note>original: "ſtafflecht"; likely meaning tiered or angled like the steps of a staff or ladder</note>, by which is meant that they are furthest forward at the top, and then the further down they go, the more they recede. It can also be the opposite: furthest forward at the bottom, and the further up they go, the more they recede. For this, one should use broken lines <note>original: "gebrochnẽ linien"; lines that are not a single straight stroke but change direction at an angle</note>. Much could be written about these things and how one should use them in even the smallest parts, but a diligent researcher will soon find this for himself.

It is also granted here that one may use more or fewer of the previously described vertical, horizontal, and diagonal lines <note>original: "auffrechten zwerchen vnnd ort linien"</note>, either for a more precise investigation or for less effort. It is also permitted to curve the straight lines as needed, however one wishes. And so that I may be more clearly understood, I will hereafter sketch out <note>original: "auffreyſſen"</note> several of the heads described above, with their reversed, vertical, horizontal, diagonal, and broken lines—including some curved ones—and then draw the shape of the face back into them. This is for the benefit of those who might not understand the written instructions for sketching all the previously described faces, which have also been altered. In many ways, one can transfer features from one to another and mix them all together—from the last through all the others to the first, however one wishes—and handle them as strangely as one can imagine. For all things are constantly subject to change. And this should not be forgotten: whatever is taken away from the thickness of a face from the side must be added to the front in width, and the reverse is also true. Note further this instruction: I will shift all vertical and horizontal lines in the square <note>original: "vierung"</note> of the side profile quite arbitrarily and willfully across the diagonals, and then draw the facial features back into it. Whatever results from that will be shown here at the end in its own figure; everyone may do this differently as they please.

However, the methods described above serve more to show the differences between people than to define the form of beauty; yet one must know such things and the like, so that one may learn many things through much experience. For no one will truly know what produces a good form unless he first knows what produces a misshapen one.

<summary>Dürer explains how to systematically vary facial features like eyes, lips, and chins by manipulating his grid system, emphasizing that understanding "deformity" is essential for identifying beauty.</summary>
<keywords>physiognomy, facial features, eyes, mouth, chin, grid manipulation, broken lines, deformity vs. beauty, Albrecht Dürer, artistic theory</keywords>

<note>A woodcut diagram features four anatomical illustrations showing the construction of the human head using a proportional grid system. The top row shows basic profiles, while the bottom row demonstrates more complex perspective or proportional transformations with alphabet and numeric labels. These are key illustrations from Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528), representing a landmark in Renaissance art theory and technical drawing.</note>

**Furthermore**, I will show how one may alter and change the head [viewed] from the front. First, from all the altered lateral <note>side-view or profile</note> heads, one may draw the various horizontal lines <term>zwerch liniẽ: lines running across the width of the face</term> by transferring them into the square of the front-facing head.

Therefore, I will not speak much further about the horizontal lines in the front-facing head, except to let it be known that one can draw them diagonally <note>original: "vber ort"; meaning slanted or across the standard grid</note>—some or all as one wishes—for by such means, crooked faces are created, just as if one side is made lower than the other. Therefore, I will only undertake to deal with the vertical lines <term>auffrechten linien: lines running from top to bottom</term>.

Now note that between the two sides of the square of the face, **a** and **b**, there are other vertical lines, such as **i, g, c, d, e, f, h, k**, which grasp and distinguish the most important features in the face according to their width: such as the forehead, eyes, nose, ears, mouth, chin, and other things.

It is to be noted that each of these vertical lines in the aforementioned grid—either individually or all together—can be shifted. Wherever you move the two lines far apart from each other, the parts in between will become wide; but at the ends where they run close together, they will <note>The text breaks off here, likely continuing to explain that the features will become narrowed on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this excerpt, Dürer explains how to adapt his geometric grid system from profiles to front-facing portraits, specifically focusing on how shifting vertical grid lines allows an artist to widen or narrow specific facial features.</summary>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer's technical instructions on facial proportions. Having previously established a geometric grid, he now explains how to mathematically manipulate that grid to create an infinite variety of human faces—from the "beautiful" to the "misshapen"—while stressing the artist's duty to maintain the essential "nature" of the subject's gender and species.</meta>

The sections between the lines may be narrow, whether at the top or the bottom. The straight line may also be drawn in an arched fashion. Through such aforementioned methods, no line remains perfectly upright, but instead they slant outward or inward. From such shifting of the aforementioned lines—wherein the form of the face is drawn anew with lines—you will find that there will be wide or narrow foreheads; likewise, horizontally long or short eyes, set close together or far apart. One may make one eye larger than the other, with larger or smaller eyelids <note>original: "aug gliedern"</note>, and set one higher than the other; likewise with the eyebrows. There will also be wide or narrow noses, whether they be crooked or straight at the top or bottom, bulbous <term>kolbet: shaped like a club or knob</term> or pointed, angular or round, wide at the back by the nostrils and narrow at the front—and conversely, large and wide, or small and narrow nostrils. Likewise, there may be wide or narrow mouths, chins, and cheeks; thus, some features will be wide at the top and narrow at the bottom, and the reverse.

Through such unequal divisions, hunched and crooked faces and other oddities are created, such as with narrow or wide mouths, or crooked, thick, large, or small lips <note>original: "lehsen"</note>, or unequally wide or narrow jawbones, pointed or blunt, whole or cleft. Such things, and others of the kind that belong to such various distinctions, an industrious observer may search out fundamentally; through the aforementioned methods, he may find many wonders buried herein. And this is a particular truth: whoever understands and recognizes what makes a thing ugly and clumsy <note>original: "heßlich vñ vngeschickt"</note> can thereby conclude what he should avoid. The more that deformity is stripped away, the more that which is lovely and beautiful remains. And whoever learns to understand these described things will see in every single thing how the transformation <term>verkeerung: Dürer's term for the proportional shifting of a figure</term> is composed. This is of great use to anyone who wishes to create a recognizable human being, whether it be in a painting <term>gemel</term> or in carved reliefs <term>erhabnen bilden</term>. However, he who is in such a profession and does not understand these things, it is certainly not possible for him to make his work correctly, unless he happens upon it by mere chance.

Likewise, the head is different upon a long, thin neck than it is upon a thick, short <margin>•</margin> neck. Note also that some people have very large "defects" <note>In this context, Dürer uses "gebrech" (defects/wants) to mean prominent or exaggerated features rather than strictly medical illnesses</note> in their faces; that is, the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin are very large. Similarly, some have small "defects" of these aforementioned things.

Likewise, every finished head is to be altered and made unrecognizable with hair—whether shorn, curly or straight, thick or thin, long or short, in locks or combed, dry or wet. The same applies to the beard or being without a beard; all things are used just as with the hair.

From these described methods, I wish to sketch out a few faces. Every woman’s head is to be transformed just like the male one, yet in such a way that the womanly nature remains. With such a transformation, as said above, one may run through the entire body and search through all the limbs.

And yet, when I say what is "womanly" or "manly" nature, it is to be understood thus: that one should not change a man or a woman so much that the nature in the man is no longer recognizable as manly, nor in the woman as womanly—though distinctions may be made in size. An example of this is to be taken: all humans or other animals are similar within their own kind, yet one still recognizes the majority among them as female or male. Thus, woman and man must remain human in all things within their distinctions. Among the animals, one sees that no lion is ever so misshapen that one would see him as a donkey, nor is a fox ever mistaken for a wolf; therefore, no species of creature should be turned away from its nature according to the order [of creation].

<note>Glossary of technical terms: Human Proportion, Albrecht Dürer, Physiognomy, facial features, anatomy, art theory, transformation/distortion, painting, relief sculpture.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how mathematical variations of his grid system allow artists to depict the vast diversity of human faces while warning that an artist must understand deformity to achieve beauty. He emphasizes that despite these proportional shifts, the fundamental nature of the subject’s gender or species must always remain recognizable.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial proportions, artistic theory, beauty and deformity, gender in art, Renaissance anatomy, physiognomy, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s exploration of how to mathematically manipulate the human face. Having previously explained how to distort features to create variety, he now addresses the limits of these transformations, arguing that while an artist can alter a person's appearance to reflect their character—even making them look like an animal—one must never lose the essential nature of the subject’s species or gender.</meta>

But though we sometimes say a person looks lion-like <note>original: "lebisch" - an archaic term for lion-like</note>, or like a bear, wolf, fox, or dog—even though they do not have four feet like those animals—it does not follow that the actual limbs of the animal are present. Rather, it is the person’s outward appearance that, in our estimation, suggests the temperament <term>gemüt: the internal disposition or spirit</term> of such an animal within a human being. Such a comparison, which we form in our minds, does not affect the physical limbs themselves; therefore, one species does not actually mix into the other. 

For this reason, the comparison of the differences I am discussing can be applied in part to the canine species, for the variety among them is quite vast. Some are very large while others are quite small; they exist in more than a thousand-fold variety of proportions across all their limbs and in entirely different shapes. They may have smooth hair or be entirely shaggy and come in many different colors. Indeed, they differ more among themselves in their form than a wolf does from a fox; yet one always sees that they are neither wolves nor foxes. 

From this it is clear that changing the outward form does not transform one nature into another, for one can easily distinguish a dog from a wild beast. This is a specific distinction of form that one could discuss at length, but for the sake of brevity <note>original: "vmb verlengerung willen" - to avoid being too long-winded</note>, we shall leave it here. Therefore, one thing should not become another in its kind <term>geschlecht: genus, species, or gender</term> if it is meant to be distinct. This is said so that you may transform the figure of a man, yet in a way that he does not become a woman or something else entirely—and likewise with the woman.

<note>Four diagrams of human heads are inscribed in proportional grids, showing variations in facial features and structure through geometric transformation. These diagrams illustrate Dürer's method of using a coordinate-like system to mathematically shift the features of the face while maintaining the underlying structural integrity of the human head.</note>

<summary>Dürer discusses the limits of artistic transformation, explaining that while a human face may be altered to resemble an animal's temperament (physiognomy), it must remain fundamentally human and gender-appropriate. He illustrates this with grids showing how to mathematically vary facial proportions without breaking the "nature" of the subject.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric instructions on the transformation of the human figure. Previously, he discussed how to alter the character and appearance of a subject; here, he introduces a specific technical tool—a dual-scale system—to mathematically adjust the proportions of a drawing independently in height and width.</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial "N" is adorned with scrolling foliage, a classic example of the high-quality typography found in the 16th-century German printing houses where Dürer’s treatises were produced.</note>Next, I will show another way by which one can use the terms "thick," "thin," "broad," or "narrow" with two types of lines to measure anew any previously described figure. In this way, you may make it different than it was before: thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. A figure is changed by this method just as was previously described using the words "long" and "short," but through a different application. It must be understood in this way: should the previously made figure remain at its original described length, yet become thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower across its horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> and the spaces between them, then the line of the figure’s length is already measured. But for the thickness and breadth, you must have another line with which you wish to measure. To enlarge the figure, make this second line longer; however, if you wish to make the figure more slender <note>original: "geschmeydiger" - lit. more supple or graceful</note>, then make this second line shorter. Then, redistribute all necessary parts proportionally <note>original: "vergeleychlich"</note> back into your new line using the <term>verkérer</term> <note>Dürer’s term for a proportional divider or "transferrer," a tool used to scale measurements from one line to another</note>, and measure the figure with it. Thus, using the longer line will result in a thicker and broader measurement, while using the shorter line will result in one that is thinner and narrower. For this purpose, I will draw for you below a special figure of these two lines, which I shall call the <term>zwiling</term> <note>lit. "the twin," referring to a pair of proportional scales used in tandem to adjust height and width independently</note>, because two types of lines are used for one thing.

First, I place upright on one side the first divided line of the figure’s length, with "a" at the top and "b" at the bottom, which has already been divided into its parts. At the bottom end, "b," I join a horizontal line, making it shorter or longer according to my preference; let its end be "c." This creates a <term>triangel</term> <note>triangle</note> a-b-c. If this horizontal line is then divided again—either by the divider described in the first booklet or by the <term>verkérer</term>—according to the proportions of the upright line, then the <term>zwiling</term> is ready. You may then measure your figure with it, taking all your parts from the horizontal line whenever it concerns what is thick, thin, broad, or narrow.

This <term>zwiling</term> may also be used for the terms "long" and "short." For instance, if the upright line a-b is lengthened and then redistributed into itself using the <term>verkérer</term>, and the figure is then measured with it, the figure will become long and more slender in body according to its proportions. Therefore, whichever line is changed—the upright or the horizontal—and used to measure the previously described figure, the result will always be different. Thus you understand: if the figure you wish to change is to remain at its original length, but is to become larger or smaller in body than it was before, you must use the altered horizontal line from the <term>zwiling</term>.

But if you wish the figure to remain at its previously described thickness and breadth, and only make it longer or shorter, you may use the upright line as stated above. Thus you are informed that each of the two sides of the <term>zwiling</term> is a separate divider, just as was described in the first booklet; here, too, the terms "much" or "little" may be applied.

It is especially noteworthy that through these two lines—the long and the short—wonderful things can be measured, and even more marvelous things when one of them is moved or shifted from point to point during the measuring. Most marvelous of all is when both are shifted or moved. Certain builders <note>original: "bawleut" - likely referring to architects or masons familiar with geometry</note> will well understand what I am saying. From these methods, things considered wonderfully difficult are easily found by whoever searches diligently through these ways. Therefore, I have drawn this figure, the <term>zwiling</term>, below here for you to follow.

<meta>Following the introduction of the "twin" scale on the previous page, Dürer now applies this tool to modify the proportions of the "thick, strong man" described in his earlier work. He provides specific mathematical adjustments to the head, shoulders, and limbs to ensure the figure remains natural even when its dimensions are altered.</meta>

<sig>R</sig>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'U' featuring foliate and floral ornamentation.</note> From this above-described <term>twin</term> <note>original: "zwiling." A dual-scale tool used to adjust height and width independently.</note>, I will hereafter describe the first <term>manner</term> <note>original: "weyß." Refers to a specific stylistic or proportional variation.</note> of the first thick, strong man / taking it from the first little book for myself / and by means of the twin, transform it / making it thinner, narrower, thicker, and broader than it was described before.

It is to be noted that if, through the aforementioned transformation, a <term>human form</term> <note>original: "menschlich gestalt."</note> is too greatly distorted / and departs entirely from its proper nature / as when some parts become far too thin and narrow or too thick or broad / then one must assist those same parts through the words "give" and "take" / which were described earlier. <note>Dürer refers to a method of "giving" (adding) or "taking" (subtracting) units of measurement to correct visual imbalances caused by mathematical scaling.</note>

And therefore, I will hereafter change the manner of becoming thinner and thicker in certain parts / namely, in the thinner manner, one should make the head shorter / from the highest part of the **crown** to the end of the chin shall be two 15th-parts / and the height of the figure shall not be reduced at the top / rather, the face should be shifted upwards and remain unchanged in its division according to the length, remaining divided just as it was before / But the shoulders should be drawn down from the line of the chin on both sides to the **armpits** / And as the foot in the <term>squaring</term> <note>original: "vierung." The geometric grid or system used for proportional layout.</note> has become shorter, it shall be lengthened again / so that it has one 13th-part / and one 14th-part / After that, make the legs from the line of the **buttocks** down to the lower **ankles** a little thinner and narrower according to what is appropriate / But in the increased manner, I have let the manner remain as I described it in the first little book / but the **fleshy part** <note>original: "trollen." Likely referring to the jowl or the soft tissue under the chin.</note> under the chin should be larger / and with this manner of head, you may also proceed as before with the man's head / and I make the shoulders higher from the armpits up toward the neck / and I shift the line of the rear further down / for otherwise the rear would be too cramped / and also make the leg, viewed from the side / below the knee downward, a little narrower / and their foot shall be six [parts] long. But I make the legs seen from the front a little narrower around the ankles and the foot / Just as I have hereafter <term>sketched</term> <note>original: "auffgerissen."</note> and placed before your eyes each of the smaller and larger manners in two forms.

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying human proportions from Albrecht Dürer’s 1528 treatise. While the previous page focused on a "stout" figure, this section describes the mathematical process for creating a "thin and narrow" figure by adjusting a proportional scale known as the "twin."</meta>

Furthermore, I wish to describe the fourth man in the first book, changing him through the **twin** <term>zwisling: a proportional scale or divider tool used to modify dimensions</term> to make him thinner and narrower than he was before. I do it in this way: I make the **horizontal line** <term>zwerch lini: a transverse or horizontal measurement line</term> on the twin a sixth part shorter than the **vertical line** <term>auffrecht lini: the vertical axis representing the man's total height</term> of the man's length. I then use the **converter** <term>verkerer: a geometric device or mathematical method used to transform proportions</term> to create a new scale, as previously mentioned. However, the vertical line will remain divided along its length by its horizontal lines as before, as was described for the man; I only shift the horizontal line of the **armpits** <note>original: "ochsen," an archaic spelling of *Achseln*, meaning shoulders or armpits</note> further upward according to skill <note>i.e., proportional judgment</note> so that the shoulders do not become too thick. I also omit the lower breast line and place the **nipples** <note>original: "tütlein"</note> on the previous armpit line, so that they stand higher than before. I also shift the two horizontal lines above the foot further downward so that the **instep** <term>rist: the arched upper part of the human foot</term> does not become too high. Afterward, from the shorter horizontal line of the twin, I apply the thickness and width to all his horizontal lines and the spaces between them; thus, a longish, thin, narrow figure is produced.

Furthermore, if one wishes to make this figure's head a tenth part of the total length, they may do so, provided that the forehead line is shifted up to the highest point and the horizontal lines of the face are again divided equally. However, the figure will become long-necked if the head is shifted upward; and make the foot one-seventh part long.

To understand all this better, I have subsequently **drawn** <note>original: "auffgerißen," meaning to draft or plot a technical drawing</note> this aforementioned modified man along with his corresponding woman, also modified just as the man was, so that one may follow them.

In the same way that I shifted the horizontal lines in the man and omitted one, I also do the same with the woman.

<note>A fine example of early 16th-century calligraphic ornamentation used to balance the composition of a printed page.</note>

<summary>This page describes how to mathematically transform a standard human figure into a slender, elongated one using proportional tools called the 'twin' and 'converter.' Dürer provides specific instructions on shifting anatomical landmarks like the nipples, shoulders, and instep to maintain aesthetic balance in a thinner frame.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, anatomy, twin scale, horizontal lines, vertical lines, drafting techniques, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s geometric analysis of the human body from his "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). Following the detailed measurements of the female form on the previous page, Dürer here introduces a mechanical and geometric method for "distorting" or scaling these proportions—effectively an early form of anamorphic or perspective transformation—allowing an artist to lengthen or shorten a figure while maintaining its internal logic.</meta>

## Next, I will show another way in which a previously described figure may be altered in a distorted yet orderly fashion, so that any figure made beforehand is improved through this method, if one wishes to lengthen all parts upwards and shorten them downwards. Or, one can do the opposite: shorten the parts of the figure upwards and lengthen them downwards. And in all this, the thickness and width of the figure remain on all horizontal lines and in between, in all limb measurements as before. Or one might wish to make the figure thinner and narrower where it is lengthened at the top, according to the situation. Similarly, for the opposite figure where the upper parts are shortened and the lower lengthened, the horizontal lines and what is between them may be thickened and broadened. But how this is to be done, I will show as follows:

I place a straight horizontal line **a. b.** <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note>. Below it, I place a point **c.** If you wish to greatly expand the upper parts, place the point **c.** closer to the horizontal line **a. b.** But if you do not wish to expand the parts excessively, place the point **c.** well below the horizontal line **a. b.** Now note and remember: let line **a. b.** be the figure’s sole line <note>The baseline where the feet are placed</note>. Above this sole line **a. b.**, I draw another parallel horizontal line **f. g.** <term>barlini: a parallel line</term>, raised as high as the length of the figure I am describing, and between them, I place an upright dotted line in which the limb measurements <note>original: "glidmaß"</note> are divided by length; let this be **h.** at the top and **i.** at the bottom. Thereafter, I draw a horizontal line **k. l.** midway between **f. g.** and **a. b.**

Once that is done, I draw two lines upwards from point **c.** as long as I need them; let their ends be **d. e.** These diverge <note>original: "spalten sich"</note> from each other at the top and must be moveable; with these, one will have to measure the parts that are to become larger and smaller. But both lines must remain joined at point **c.** below. Now, if you take the part you intend—whether you want to make it larger or smaller at the top or bottom—and find how it should rightly be done, do it thus: Let the part that is to be measured stand on whichever horizontal line it may; always take the same widths or thicknesses as described in the first intended figure, and place them in the middle on the horizontal line **k. l.**, so that the upright line **h-i** passes through the middle of the placed part. If you then let the two moveable lines **d-e** touch this placed part on both sides, they give you the enlarged widths at the top on the horizontal line where they should stand. Thus, this method increases and decreases everything above and below the line **k. l.**, upwards and downwards. All this may be turned upside down and used in reverse; then everything will be opposite to the first.

I have drawn this figure twice hereafter, the second time reversed; and I give it a special name, and call it the **Indicator** <note>original: "Zeyger"</note>.

<note>An elegant example of early modern calligraphic decoration commonly found in scholarly treatises of the period, serving as a decorative flourish or printer's mark.</note>

A

<summary>Dürer explains a geometric method using a tool he calls the "Indicator" to scale and distort human proportions systematically. This allows artists to adjust the height and width of a figure while maintaining consistent internal measurements.</summary>
<keywords>geometric transformation, scaling, human proportion, Zeyger, Albrecht Dürer, limb measurements, horizontal lines, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. While the previous page introduced the "Pointer" (Zeyger) for shifting proportions, this page introduces the "Distorter" (Falscher), a geometric method for lengthening or shortening parts of the body to create different physical types or to correct for perspective.</meta>

# But to return to the distortion
(for which this above-described pointer is especially to be used and will be better understood), of which I say how one should make it: do thus. 

<note>An ornate historiated woodcut initial letter 'A' features architectural motifs and scrolling foliage, marking the start of this technical explanation.</note>

Take for yourself the fifth described man and his corresponding woman from the second book, and as their vertical lines of their length are divided by the transverse lines. Let one of those vertical dotted lines be **a.** at the top and **b.** at the bottom; set it upright before you. From point **b.**, extend a transverse line at right angles; let its end be **c.** After that, draw a diagonal line from **a.** to **c.** Then, from the dotted line of the figure, from all the points that indicate the length of the divisions, draw straight lines into point **c.** Thus this tool is finished, from which you must take the distortion of the figure that is lengthened in its upper parts and prepared for this purpose; likewise the opposite of this. Therefore, this tool, which specifically shortens at the bottom, shall henceforth be called the <term>distorter<note>original: "felscher". Dürer uses this term for a geometric device that "falsifies" or alters standard proportions for specific artistic effects.</note></term>, because anything measured therein with a hanging line falls away as false <note>meaning it deviates from the natural standard of the original model</note>.

Now you must first learn its use, thus: Take another line of the figure’s length; let it be **d.** at the top and **e.** at the bottom. Place this line with its lower end **e.** upon the line **b. c.** and push it back from the angle **b.** toward the point **c.** by approximately one-eighth of the figure's length; and lean its upper end **d.** against the diagonal line **a. c.** Then this line **d. e.** will be intersected and proportionately divided by all the cross-lines drawn from the points of the figure’s division into point **c.**—and yet it is distorted, for everything lengthens toward the top and becomes shorter toward the bottom. If you now set this newly created dotted line before you, which is **d.** at the top and **e.** at the bottom, and draw horizontal lines through all the points, and upon these and between them you create the previously described thicknesses and breadths of the figure, and draw the shape of the figure into it anew, then you will see its transformation. 

For this reason, I have drafted this <term>distorter</term> below twice; in the second instance, the bottom is turned to the top to correctly direct oneself from it. And for this reason: if you wish to invert the figure contrary to the aforementioned way, then turn your dotted line bottom-to-top and use it exactly according to the previously described method. Of such two figures which have been distorted through the aforementioned means, I have drafted a pair of man and woman below. However, I have neither enlarged nor diminished them at the top or bottom, as was spoken of above regarding the pointer; but whoever wishes to may use it so. And whoever considers this <term>distorter</term> well and uses it correctly in many kinds of things will accomplish and make wonderful things and create much of use. For the previous words of distinction are all to be considered herein, in order to find hidden things necessary for such work. But do not give or take as much from things as is done here <note>Dürer warns the artist not to over-apply these distortions to the point of absurdity</note>. Anyone who wishes may, by means of the <term>scale<note>original: "maßstab"</note></term>, describe anew all these previously made and altered figures as they have become.

But if someone wishes to describe them, let him flee the deformity that comes from too much distortion; for I do not present these things as if they must be so and not otherwise, but I have set them so far out of their right nature as a lesson, so that from such gross distinction one may clearly recognize that all these previously described things should be used for necessity and not for ruin. Therefore, do not push the leaning line in the <term>distorter</term> too far back on the transverse line, whether it be upright or inverted, and guard yourself lest your inversions become <term>changelings<note>original: "wechselbelg". In German folklore, these were deformed offspring left by fairies; here Dürer uses it to mean grotesque or monstrous proportions.</note></term>. For as useful as these things are when used out of necessity, they are also as misleading if one uses them without need and out of <term>idle curiosity<note>original: "bierwitz". Literally "beer-wit," meaning a sort of reckless or unthinking experimentation.</note></term>, in excess when one does too much without cause. This <term>distorter</term> may be used in specific parts, just as well in one part as in the whole. From such various inversions of the human form, it is found which are stronger or weaker, more agile or slower, free or heavy. Therefore, it is necessary to know all this, for such things extend much further than is indicated here.

<summary>Dürer explains the construction of the "Distorter," a geometric tool for systematically altering the proportions of a human figure. He cautions artists to use this tool with moderation to avoid creating grotesque or monstrous figures.</summary>
<keywords>distortion, distorter, felscher, geometry, human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, artistic technique, changelings, scale</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion. Having previously described how to construct the "Distorter" (Fellscher) tool, Dürer now explains how an artist should manually adjust specific body parts—such as the forehead and shins—to compensate for the mathematical distortions created by the device.</meta>

Likewise, whoever wishes for the sake of novelty <note>original: "seltzamkeyt". This refers to creating unusual or specialized physical types rather than standard "ideal" proportions.</note> to use the pair of figures—man and woman—that have been altered through the <term>distorter<note>original: "felscher". A geometric construction used to systematically skew or "falsify" natural proportions for artistic effect.</note></term>, should proceed as follows: for the two figures that were lengthened at the top, one should make the head much lower above the forehead and make the insteps of the feet higher. But for the other pair of figures that were lengthened at the bottom, one should make the shins together with the instep of the foot much shorter, and then redistribute everything evenly using the <term>transformer<note>original: "verkerer". A related geometric tool used to shift or "turn" proportions, helping to balance the distorted dimensions.</note></term>. However, in the following figures, I have left everything exactly as they turned out.

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues from the anatomical measurements of the human body, transitioning from the "what" of human proportions to the "how" of their application. Dürer here introduces geometric methods for distorting a figure so that it appears correct when viewed on curved surfaces, such as vaulted ceilings or through mirrors. This is a practical application of the proportions established on the previous pages.</meta>

I will now draw and show you another **distorter** <note>original: "felſcher," literally a "falsifier." Dürer uses this term for a geometric tool or method that "falsifies" (distorts) a drawing so it appears correct when applied to a curved surface.</note> with which one may also transform a previously described human figure along its length. However, according to its thickness and width, it is properly transformed through the **proportional scale** <note>original: "weier," likely referring to a gauging scale or transfer instrument.</note>; and as often as one needs it, one may use it. It is measured in two ways: the first is measured on the outside of the circumference of a **circle** <note>original: "zirckels"</note>, the other on the inside of the circumference of a hollow circle. Let everyone explore therein to see what can be done with it as long as it pleases them. Whoever understands this with the right reasoning will surely realize what it should be used for. Through this, one can also perceive how it happens that a straight, parallel ruler appears curved before a round mirror; if it stands upright before it, in the middle of the mirror it gains a bulging belly and becomes wide, but narrow at the top and bottom and curved backwards. Likewise, all other things align themselves according to the circle. Therefore, whoever wishes to paint in **vaults** <note>original: "gwelber"; architectural vaulted ceilings.</note> must necessarily know such things; although it is not yet **perspective** <note>original: "perspectiua"</note> in the strict sense, it follows along when one sees such things in a mirror. But here I speak only of measurement in itself, for this distorter only demonstrates what is measured on a round or hollow object from a straight one. But you will realize the cause of why it falls thus and see it in the distortion of the images when you draw them using this distorter, for the results speak for themselves. And although it looks strange when being drawn—where its practical use is not immediately apparent and it seems useless—it is nonetheless necessary that one knows this and all other practices, so that an intelligent person may extract what is useful to them; though the ignorant may mock this and even greater things.

Now I will show how one should set up this other **distorter** so that a previously made image may be transformed from it; and if one uses this correctly, it brings improvement to some things; therefore, avoid excessive words. But that I go into such detail here happens because in a small thing the differences are recognized more noticeably, and also because I do not have the space to make it here on a larger scale due to the small area; therefore, one should use this thing moderately in larger works. 

Construct this distorter thus: First, take the length of the image on its line, which is divided with all its **points** <note>original: "puncten"</note>; stand it upright before you, and mark its ends **x.** at the top and **y.** at the bottom. Then divide **x.y.** with a point **c.** in the middle between them. From this point, draw a straight line at equal angles <note>i.e., perpendicular</note> across; let its end be **z.** Then draw two lines, **x.z.** and **y.z.**; thus a long **triangle** <note>original: "triangel"</note> is formed, and **z.** is the apex. Then from all the points of the vertical line **x.y.**, draw lines to point **z.** as far as you need them. 

Then, behind line **x.y.** toward point **c.**, draw a vertical **parallel line** <note>original: "bar lini"</note> as long as you need, so that it shall go far below and above the front line of the triangle, extending up and down. Then take a long **compass** <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and set one foot in point **z.** and with the other foot set it on the center line of the triangle near the back of the vertical **x.y.**, so that it touches the line **x.y.** at one point. From there, draw a circular arc upwards and downwards until it reaches the set-back vertical parallel line. And where this circular stroke cuts the set-back vertical parallel line, place an **s.** at the top and a **t.** at the bottom. 

And where this circular stroke further cuts the lines that are drawn from the points of line **x.y.** to point **z.**, draw from all those same points straight parallel lines across into the set-back vertical line **s.t.** Thus the line **s.t.** is divided from the line **x.y.**, but it changes entirely: first, because it becomes shorter; second, because it is drawn from the points of the circle, and thus all things shorten themselves upwards and downwards, the further they go. Then mark all points in this newly formed line above the middle: mark the topmost cross-line with an **a.**, the next **b.**, then **c.**, and so on: **d.e.f.g.** etc. But below the middle, mark them with numbers; begin at the very bottom with a **1.**, then **2. 3. 4. 5.** etc. These

<summary>Dürer explains a geometric method for distorting human proportions to fit curved surfaces or mimic mirror reflections, essential for muralists painting on vaults. He provides a step-by-step technical guide for constructing a 'distorter' (felſcher) using triangles, circles, and parallel lines.</summary>
<keywords>distortion, vaults, geometry, perspective, mirror reflection, transfer tool, technical drawing, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "distorter" (*felscher*). While the previous page introduced the concept of how a figure might look when reflected in a curved mirror or painted on a vault, this page provides the specific geometric steps to construct these distorted figures using a "proportional scale" (*weiler*). This is a bridge between pure anatomy and the practical mathematical perspective required for Renaissance art.</meta>

<sig>S iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: so</meta>

Take **line a. i.** with the contents of all the proportions of the limbs; use it as previously described, and set two of these lines at the same height next to each other—the first for the side view and the second for the front-facing figure. Draw through all your newly formed divisions with horizontal lines. Now that the lengths of the parts are divided, you must first find how to divide the thickness and width; do this through the **proportional scale** <term>weiler: a gauging instrument or scale used to transfer and adjust proportions</term> with its movable line. You must use it on both sides, which will give you equal results at the top and bottom.

First, draw a straight horizontal line, the ends of which are **a.** and **a.**, and in the middle set an upright line at right angles; let this be **g.** at the top and **b.** at the bottom. Now make two movable lines **a. c.**, one on each side. You must use them thus: In the **distorter** <term>felscher: literally a 'falsifier,' Dürer's term for a geometric tool that distorts a drawing for specific visual effects</term> you just made, find the greatest width on the middle horizontal line of the triangle (which is drawn from point **i.** through the arc **f. t.**) between the straight line and the arc. Place one end at the bottom on the horizontal line of the scale **a. a.**, standing upright on the line **g. b.** At this height, place the two movable lines **a. c.** so that they cross over one another.

Afterward, take the upper half of the horizontal lines between the arc and its fixed or straight lines of each length, and note with which letters they are marked. Place them upright on one side of the scale so that one end of each touches the horizontal line of the scale **a. a.** Slide them **parallel** <term>baralel: lines that stay an equal distance apart</term> back and forth along the horizontal line **a. a.** until their tops all touch the movable line **a. c.** Thus, the short lines in the scale near point **a.** correspond to how they are in their arc in the distorter near point **f.** However, the distance between them does not remain as it was in the arc because of the curve of the arc—for a straight line compared to a curved one creates a change. Just as I place the upper half (which is marked with letters in the distorter) into one part of the scale, I do the same with the other half of the arc—the bottom part in the distorter marked with numbers—and I place those in the other half of the scale. 

Now that this is prepared, take any thickness or width of the figure described before on any horizontal line (or between them) and place it in the scale on the vertical line **g. b.** so that it touches the horizontal line **a. a.** at the bottom. Then lay your movable line **a. c.** upon it; it will intersect all the vertical lines that are or stand in the scale. Therefore, on whichever vertical line a width or thickness is taken, its correct measure is found through this laying and intersecting, just as I have very precisely drawn hereafter. Thus, I have also drawn and transformed two figure measurements and types (which I described at the end of the second book) through this distorter. Only the arms, hands, and feet I did not do with this distorter, but rather by **skill** <note>original: "geschickligkeyt"; refers to the artist's manual dexterity and judgment rather than a mathematical tool.</note>, for when this distorter reaches the highest or lowest points, it **misleads** <note>original: "verfurter"; meaning the distortion becomes too extreme to look natural.</note> far too much.

Furthermore, if one turns this distorter around and uses it in reverse, and then transforms a figure from it, that same figure will be lengthened more and more in its parts toward the top and bottom, but shortened more and more toward the middle, as in the previously transformed figure. To make this inverted distorter, do this: Take again the dotted line **r. y.** of the figure's length, stand it upright before you, and extend it with **f. t.** as before. Then set your point **z.** and draw the two lines **r. z.** and **z. y.** Afterward, draw straight lines from all the points in line **r. y.** to point **z.** Next, set a point **t.** across from **z.** at the same height. Take a long **compass** <term>zirckel: a tool for drawing circles and measuring distances</term> and place one foot on point **t.** and the other foot on point **s.**, and draw a circle around point **t.** so that the arc is between **r. y.** and point **z.** Wherever this circle-cut intersects the straight lines drawn from the points between **r. y.** to point **z.**, draw horizontal parallel lines across them. Behind the arc, toward **z.**, draw an upright line through all the horizontal lines. That is then the newly found line, divided anew with the horizontal lines through the entire length of the figure. Afterward, you must find the thickness and width of the figure through the scale as before, but inverted. Whoever wishes to do so may draw a figure this way themselves; hereafter I have only drawn this distorter for that purpose.

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric construction of the "weiler" (proportional scale) and how it works in tandem with the "felscher" (distorter) to transform human proportions. He details how to transfer measurements from a curve to a scale and warns that some parts, like hands and feet, require an artist's eye rather than just a mathematical tool.</summary>
<keywords>proportional scale, distorter, geometric transformation, foreshortening, vault painting, mathematical perspective, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion</keywords>
