--- Page 199 ---
<meta>Following the detailed measurements of the female form provided on the previous page, Dürer now moves into a philosophical and practical discussion on how to utilize these proportions. This page, marked with the signature "I," serves as a crucial instruction on the variation of figures, the importance of artistic judgment, and the ethical responsibility of the artist to reflect the truth of nature while maintaining a balance between rigid rules and creative freedom.</meta>

<sig>I</sig>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'W' featuring foliate designs and a small figure or face within the ornamentation. This is a fine example of early 16th-century German woodcut typography, characteristic of Albrecht Dürer's publications.</note>**W**hoever now wishes to proceed further may take each previously described measurement of the figures that have been transformed—whether through the transformer, the shaper, the doubler, the stretcher, or the modifier <note>Dürer uses a variety of terms here—*verkerer, weler, zwiling, zenger, felšcher*—to describe different methods of manipulating or distorting standard proportions for artistic effect</note>—and transform them again in specific parts or in their entirety. You may also mix all previously made figures with one another according to their measurements, in whatever way may most skillfully be done, until you find something pleasing to yourself and others. But such mixing produces strange results; therefore, pay close attention, especially when you wish to draw the form of each figure with its lines, both upon all the straight divisional lines and between them. You must take great care not to create a deformity <note>original: *ungeſtalt*</note>. For between the horizontal lines <note>original: *zwerch linien*</note>, the length and character <note>original: *art*</note> of the limbs are indicated; and upon these lines, the thickness and breadth of that specific place are shown. According to the written instructions, one should not fail here. 

But between the horizontal lines, one easily errs; for sometimes a feature is drawn too far inward or outward, so that those parts become too thick, thin, wide, or narrow. For the sake of brevity, these things are not described here so precisely that they would be as obvious as if someone were showing you by hand how it should go. Therefore, I expect there will be many who are unskilled in drawing <note>original: *auffreyſſen*</note>; if they follow my instructions and spoil the work, they will lay the blame on me and say I have described it poorly. Therefore, whoever intends to draw figures according to this little book, but is not well-versed in the matter, will at first find the task difficult. Let such a person then place a human being before them who is roughly suitable to that specific measurement. After that, let them draw the outer lines as well as they can and understand. For it is considered good when one follows life closely in the drawing so that it resembles it and becomes like nature; and especially when that which is made is beautiful, it will be held as artistic and worthily praised. 

But furthermore, it remains within a person’s honest will whether or how they wish to use all the previously described terms of distinction. For one may, if they wish, learn to work with the <term>art [Kunst]</term> in which truth resides, or without art, whereby every thing is led astray in that same "freedom" <note>Dürer is warning against a lawless creativity that ignores the underlying mathematical truths of nature</note>, making their labor a mockery to the knowledgeable. For well-done work honors God and is useful, good, and pleasing to man. But to perform incompetent work in the arts is blameworthy and harmful, and it is hated in small works as in great. 

And therefore it is necessary that everyone uses moderation <note>original: *beſcheydenheyt*</note> in their work that is to come to light. From this it follows that he who wishes to create something right should take nothing away from nature, nor impose anything intolerable upon it. Yet some wish to do so little with the outlining that it can hardly be noticed; this is of no use if it cannot be tested, and too much is also of no use; a right middle path is best. But the reason I have diverged so far from one another in this little book is so that it might be better perceived in small things. However, whoever wishes to work on a larger scale should by no means follow this "hardness" <note>original: *hertigkeyt*; referring to the exaggerated or stiff proportions used in the diagrams for clarity</note> of mine; rather, make your work softer, so that it does not become beast-like <note>original: *thiriſch*</note>, but rather appears artistic to the eye. For these distinctions are not good to see if they are used incorrectly or without mastery. 

It is also no wonder that an artistic master considers many variations of form that he could all create if he had enough time; for such occurrences are countless among artists, and their minds are full of images that it would be possible for them to make. Therefore, if a man were granted many hundreds of years to live, who skillfully used such art and was naturally gifted for it, he would—through the power God has given to man—be able every day to pour out and create many new forms of men and other creatures that had neither been seen before nor imagined by anyone else.

<summary>In this concluding instructional passage, Dürer discusses the practical application of his proportional systems, urging artists to balance mathematical rules with the observation of nature. He warns against both rigid exaggeration and formless 'freedom,' emphasizing that true artistic mastery honors God through the infinite creation of new, harmonious forms.</summary>

<keywords>artistic judgment, transformation of figures, horizontal lines, moderation, nature vs art, divine creativity, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, Renaissance theory</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the manipulation of human proportions, Dürer now elaborates on the natural variations that occur in art and the creative freedom an artist possesses when applying these mathematical rules to their work.</meta>

Therefore, God gives to skillful people <note>original: *kunstreichen menschen*</note> much power in these and other matters. And although much is said about differences, it is well known that all things a human can do naturally differ from one another. Thus, no artist <note>original: *kunstner*</note> lives who is so certain that he could cast two figures from one mold without someone immediately finding differences by which they might be recognized. For none of our actions is exactly and entirely equal to another. We cannot prevent this; we see that if we take two prints from one engraved copper plate, or cast two figures in one mold, differences are immediately found by which they can be distinguished from one another for many reasons. If this is found to be true in even the most certain and mechanical things, it is all the more so in other things made by a free hand.

This, however, is not the "difference" <note>Dürer here distinguishes between accidental variation and intentional artistic variation.</note> of which I speak here; for I am speaking of the difference that a person specifically undertakes and that lies within their own will, of which I have spoken continuously. When it falls to a person to make this or that, they choose something from among these differing things. But this is not the aforementioned difference that we cannot separate from our work; rather, it is a difference that makes things either beautiful or ugly. These are brought about through the "words of difference" described earlier in this booklet. When such things are drawn into a work, they are judged by each person’s sight in the mind according to their own thinking; these judgments rarely agree with one another. And therefore, I wish to leave all my previously described things—including those I have altered—entirely free to everyone, so that if one wishes, they need not leave anything exactly as it is shown. I will hereafter give instruction on the ways in which one may do this. However, an honest man should take care not to create anything impossible that nature could not endure, unless one intends to create dream-work <note>original: *traumwerck*; Dürer’s term for works of pure imagination or fantasy, such as chimeras or mythological beasts, where natural laws are suspended.</note>; in such a case, one may mix all sorts of creatures together.

But to proceed with the work, let us first take a **Figure** <term>Figure: a human form as defined by Dürer's proportional system</term> as it is divided in its length by horizontal lines <term>horizontal lines: original *zwerch linten*, the cross-sectional grid lines used to mark heights on the body</term>. These same lines have their own numbers in all the previously described figures. Whoever wishes may make more or fewer of these horizontal lines. However, making more horizontal lines serves to provide a more precise measurement for seeking further detail than I have shown and set down before; and this is necessary for a diligent master. If one were sufficiently certain, he would not even need to make straight lines, but could simply set points, provided he can recognize them. But he who makes fewer horizontal lines than I have set down before will have less effort, yet he will also achieve less; this is the difference regarding the lines. The other way to handle the horizontal lines through the body works just as it did before with the "turned" faces <note>referring to Dürer's earlier instructions on foreshortening and rotating heads</note>. Thus, throughout the entire body, you may shift all the horizontal lines from their places, moving them closer to or further from one another. If the lines are pushed close together, the parts between them become short; but where they are pushed far apart, the parts between them become long. All of this concerns the terms "long" and "short."

Now we shall consider thickness and breadth. This change occurs on all the horizontal lines and also between them. Whatever you add to that same line, that figure will become broader toward the sides and thicker from front to back at that location. But if you take away from it, the figure will become narrower and thinner at that end. This also happens between all the horizontal lines, so that one can make everything on the figure larger or smaller. Yet, you should not make anything too long, short, thick, thin, broad, or narrow. Without doubt, if you are skilled, you may create wonderful variations through these things described above.

<summary>Dürer discusses the inevitability of variation in art and explains how artists can intentionally manipulate horizontal grid lines to alter the length, thickness, and breadth of the human figure while cautioning against unnatural "impossible" forms.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, artistic variation, horizontal lines, dream-work, aesthetic judgment, measurement, anatomy, artistic freedom</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Dürer moves from the idea of accidental variations in art to the intentional use of proportions to create a variety of human types, ranging from the delicate to the robust.</meta>

<sig>L ij</sig>

...make them in such a form and introduce great inequality <note>original: *vngleycheyt*; here meaning diversity or variation</note> between them. From this, it is found that some acquire broad shoulders, thin flanks, and narrow hips, while others have the opposite. Some acquire a short body and long legs, and again the reverse; some have straight bodies, arms, and legs, while others have them crooked. Thus, through measurement, it follows that nature becomes recognizable through the form of the human being. From these aforementioned things, one finds the reason for how to make things lovely or ugly. 

Therefore, whoever wishes to set about such a work should know in his own mind what he intends to make. For this reason, I have indicated what is described above: so that a person might be primarily thoughtful before beginning his work. It is not enough for one who wishes to show his skill in art to simply put forward the best he can that is fit for that specific work. Rather, it must be mentioned that an understanding, practiced artist <note>original: *kunstner*</note> can demonstrate his great power and art more in a coarse, peasant-like figure <note>original: *beürischer gestalt*</note>—or even in small things—than many others can in a great work. These rare words will only be understood by powerful artists: that I speak the truth.

From this, it happens that someone may draw something with a pen in a single day on half a sheet of paper, or carve something into a small piece of wood with his little engraving tool <note>original: *eysellein*; likely a burin or small chisel</note>, which proves to be more artistic and better than another person’s large work upon which they labored for an entire year with the highest diligence. This gift is wonderful, for God often gives one person the ability to learn and understand how to make something good, the like of which no equal is found in his own time; perhaps none like him existed for a long time before, and none shall follow soon after. 

We see an example of this in the times of the Romans, when they were in their splendor. In the ruins <note>original: *drümer*</note> of what was made by them, we still see a level of art that is rarely found in our own works today. But if we ask how we should make a beautiful image, some will speak according to human judgment, and then others will not agree; I myself do not have a certain knowledge of it. Who then will make us sure? For I believe there is no human living who could conceive of the most beautiful limit <note>original: *endt*; the ultimate perfection</note> in even the smallest living creature, let alone in a human, who is a special creature of God to whom other creatures are subject. 

I grant that one person may conceive and make a prettier image and demonstrate its good, natural cause to simple reason better than another, but not to the ultimate degree that it could not be even prettier. Such perfection does not enter the human mind. God alone knows such things; to whomever He revealed it, that person would also know it. The truth alone resides within that person who finds the most beautiful form and measure <note>original: *maß*</note> of humans, and no other.

People deliberate over such things and hold countless different judgments, seeking many ways to achieve them, though one attains ugliness much more easily than beauty. In the midst of the error we currently have among us, I do not know how to steadfastly describe what specific measure might claim to be the "right" beauty. However, I would gladly help as much as I can so that the coarse deformity of our work might be cut away and avoided—unless it were the case that someone intentionally wanted to make something deformed with special diligence. 

Now we return, as mentioned before, to human judgment. People consider a certain form beautiful at one time, and at another time, they choose a different one instead. If these same people then require a work from a master artist, the master must be skilled enough to satisfy their desire; if he does so, he is to be praised. To do this, he must have a powerful command of his craft <note>original: *gewaltigen brauch*</note> if he is to fulfill their will. It would be useful to him if he understood in his mind what the right measure truly was, so that he could also demonstrate it through his work.

<summary>Dürer discusses the subjective nature of beauty and the artist's ability to show skill even in "coarse" subjects, while noting that ultimate perfection in proportion is known only to God.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, artistic skill, Roman art, beauty, divine knowledge, judgment, deformity, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s discourse on the theory of beauty and proportion. He transitions from discussing the artist’s individual skill to the philosophical problem of defining "perfect" proportions, emphasizing the role of geometry as an objective truth to counter human uncertainty.</meta>

But it seems impossible to me for someone to say he knows how to demonstrate the best measurements for the human form. For the lie is in our knowledge, and darkness is stuck so firmly within us that even our groping <note>original: *nach dappen*; to feel one's way in the dark</note> fails us. However, whoever proves his case through Geometry <note>original: *Geometria*</note> and demonstrates the fundamental truth, him the whole world should believe. For there, one is captured by logic, and it is right to hold such a person as one gifted by God as a master in such matters. The reasons for their proofs should be heard with desire, and it is even more joyful to see their finished works.

Since we cannot reach the very best, should we then abandon our learning altogether? We do not accept such beastly <note>original: *fihischen*; modern German *viehisch*, meaning animalistic or crude</note> thoughts, for humans have both good and evil before them; therefore, it befits a rational person to pursue the better path. And so that we might return to how a better image may be made, we must first order the whole figure well and magnificently with all its limbs, and thereafter each limb must be crafted with specific, well-considered skill—in the smallest things as in the largest. We do this to see if we might draw out the beauty that is given to us, so that we may come closer to the right goal. 

As mentioned before, a human being is a piece gathered from many parts, and as each part has its own specific nature, one must very diligently take heed of all things by which they might be corrupted, so that such flaws are avoided. One must diligently cling to the right natural properties and not deviate from them, to the best of our ability. 

To make something praiseworthy to the sight requires great diligence and high attention. For if one first takes up the head—as was described in the previous little books—one sees what strange roundness it has. Likewise with other things, what strange lines everything requires! These cannot be drawn by any rule <note>i.e., not by a simple straightedge or compass</note> but must be drawn from point to point. Thus, the forehead, cheeks, nose, eyes, mouth, and chin, with their inward and outward curves and specific shapes, must be drawn so diligently that even the very smallest little thing is not left out, but is instead crafted with careful consideration. 

Just as each part should be well-arranged on its own, so should it harmonize <note>original: *reymen*; literally "rhyme," used here to mean fitting together or matching</note> in the assembly of the whole. Furthermore, the neck should fit the head well, being neither too short, nor too long, nor too thick or thin. Likewise, take further heed to diligently portray the chest, belly, back, and buttocks, the legs, feet, arms, and hands with all their contents, so that the smallest details are well-arranged and made to the highest standard. These things should also be finished in the work as purely and diligently as possible, and the very smallest wrinkles <note>original: *runtzelein*</note> and small veins <note>original: *ertlein*; likely referring to the small ridges of veins or tendons</note> should not be omitted as far as possible. 

For it will not do to simply run over the surface or clutter a thing—unless it is a case where one must produce an image very quickly, in which case one must be satisfied. But even then, one should give an indication of right understanding, so that even in haste, a correct intention is recognized. This style should be uniform throughout the whole body and in all figures, whether they be of a hard or soft nature, fleshy or lean. It should not be that one part is fat and another thin, as if you made fat legs and lean arms, or the reverse; or fat in the front and lean in the back. Rather, all things should "rhyme" and fit together, and not be falsely assembled, for things that match are considered handsome. Therefore, in every image, a consistent age should be indicated in all its limbs; it should not be that the head is of a youth, the chest of an old man, and the hands and feet of a middle-aged man. And the image should not...

<summary>Dürer argues that while absolute human perfection is unattainable due to human fallibility, artists must use geometry and consistent natural observation to create harmonious, well-proportioned figures. He emphasizes the importance of anatomical consistency, such as ensuring all parts of a figure reflect the same age and physical build.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, aesthetics, human proportion, artistic consistency, anatomy, Renaissance art theory, naturalism, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s practical advice on how an artist should approach the human form, moving from the philosophical theory of beauty to the necessity of consistency, the importance of drafting, and the observation of diverse human types.</meta>

-><sig>L iij</sig><-

If a figure is made young in the front and old in the back, or is otherwise crafted in a manner contrary to reason—for whatever is against nature is bad—it is improper. Therefore, it is fitting that every image be consistent in its type throughout: either young, old, or middle-aged <note>original: *mittelmessig*</note>; thin or stout; soft or hard. Thus, you will find that grown youth is smooth, even, and full of body; but old age is uneven, gnarled <note>original: *knorret*; meaning bony or rugged</note>, wrinkled, and the flesh is wasted away. 

It is useful to point these things out before one begins the actual work, so that one may first draft <term>reyssen<gloss>The act of drawing, drafting, or designing the initial lines of a composition.</gloss></term> the figure with lines as one desires it to be. In this way, one may see the form beforehand to determine if anything within it should be improved. If you do this with diligence and careful consideration, you will not easily regret what you have made afterward. Therefore, it is necessary for every artist to learn to draft well, for it serves many arts beyond measure and much depends upon it. 

Even if someone has a good set of measurements described before him, if he attempts to copy them but cannot draft—proceeding with an unskilled hand through the length, thickness, and breadth of the image—he will very soon ruin what he intended to make. However, if one who understands drafting takes up a well-described image, he can assist himself in the drafting so that the result becomes even better. 

In order that we might arrive at a good measurement and thereby bring a degree of beauty into our work, it seems most profitable to me that you take your measurements from many living people. Seek out people who are considered beautiful and copy that nature with all diligence. For a knowledgeable person can gather something good from many different people by examining all the parts of the limbs; it is rare to find a single person who has all limbs perfectly formed, for everyone has some defect. 

Yet, while one should gather from many different people, one must still use only one type of person for a single image to maintain the consistency mentioned before. In crafting a young image, use only young people; for an old one, use the old; for a middle-aged one, use middle-aged people. Do the same with thin, stout, soft, and hard people, whether strong or weak; use each type specifically for specific images. 

Whoever exerts himself in these things to truly investigate every part of the human form individually will find more requirements for his work than he can ever fulfill. The human understanding can seldom grasp how to correctly depict the beauty found in creatures. And even though we cannot speak of the greatest beauty of a physical creature, we nevertheless find in visible creatures a beauty so far exceeding our understanding that none of us can perfectly bring it into our work.

Furthermore, many different types of people are needed to make many different kinds of images. To this end, you find two distinct races of people: the whites and the Moors <note>original: *Morn*; a historical term for people of African descent.</note>. Between them and us, a difference in nature is to be noted. The faces of the Moors are seldom considered beautiful <note>Dürer is reflecting the Eurocentric aesthetic standards of the 16th century here.</note> because of their flat noses and thick lips; likewise, their shins, knees, and feet are too gnarled and not as pleasing to look at as those of the whites; the same applies to their hands. 

However, I have seen some among them who were otherwise so well-proportioned and elegant in their entire bodies that I have never seen, nor can I imagine, a better-formed person—possessing a truly excellent nature in their arms and all things, such that they could not be improved. Thus, one finds among the races of men all kinds of types that are useful for various images, depending on their constitution <term>complexion<gloss>In the 16th century, this referred to the "humoral" temperament or physical makeup of a person.</gloss></term>. For instance, strong, hard people have a structural frame <term>gebzech<gloss>The underlying physical build or anatomical structure of the body.</gloss></term> in their bodies like lions; but the weak are of a softer frame and not as muscular as the strong. Therefore, it is not fitting to give a very strong image a very soft frame, nor to give a slender image a very hard one.

<summary>Dürer emphasizes the importance of consistency in depicting age and physique, the necessity of mastering drafting, and the method of combining the best features of various individuals—including different races—to approximate an ideal beauty that nature suggests but humans can never fully replicate.</summary>
<keywords>drafting, consistency of type, ideal beauty, anatomical observation, Moors, physical constitution, artistic training</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s discourse on the variety of human forms and the artist's relationship with nature, building on the previous discussion of consistency in age and physique.</meta>

...deficiency. Although one must yield somewhat to the thin and the stout in images, one may reasonably use a degree of softness and hardness in all sorts of different figures as one wishes. But life in nature reveals the truth of these things; therefore, look at it diligently, guide yourself by it, and do not depart from nature in your own opinion, thinking you might find something better by yourself, for you would be misled. 

For truly, art <note>original: *kunſt*; in Dürer's time, this refers to both technical skill and the theoretical knowledge of one's craft.</note> lies within nature; whoever can pluck it out has it. If you obtain it, it will take away much that is common <note>original: *feils*; literally "for sale," implying something cheap or mediocre.</note> in your work. Through <term>Geometria<gloss>Geometry; for Dürer, the mathematical foundation of all artistic proportion and spatial reasoning.</gloss></term> you may prove much of your work; what we cannot prove, however, we must leave to good intentions and the judgment of men. Yet, experience does much in these things. The more closely your work conforms to life in its form, the better your work appears—and this is the truth. Therefore, never intend to do better than what God has given His created nature the power to produce, for your ability is powerless against God's creation.

From this, it is concluded that no man, out of his own mind alone, can ever make a beautiful image, unless he has filled his mind with much copying from life. This is then no longer called one’s own, but has become an acquired and learned art, which grows and bears the fruit of its kind. From this, the collected secret treasure of the heart becomes manifest through the work, and the new creature which one creates in his heart in the form of a thing. This is the reason why a well-practiced artist does not need to copy living models for every single image, for he pours out enough of what he has gathered over a long time from the outside into himself. Such a man has the ability to produce good work; but very few reach this understanding. There are many, however, who with great effort produce much that is wrong. 

Therefore, whoever has gained a good practice through right understanding is well able to make something good without any external model, as far as our human ability allows; yet it will always be better if he uses life as he works. To the unpracticed, however, this is impossible, for these things do not happen by chance. It also happens, though rarely, that someone through great experience and long, diligent practice becomes so certain that, out of the understanding he has gained with great effort, he may produce something better without a model than another who has many living people before him to copy, simply because the latter lacks understanding. 

Therefore, we must take great care and see to it that deformity and impropriety do not creep into our work. We should avoid making useless things in images that are otherwise meant to be beautiful, for this is a great fault. Take a comparison from the blind, the lame, withered cripples, those who limp, and the like; such things are all ugly because of their deficiency. Likewise, excess is also to be avoided, such as if one wanted to give a person three eyes, three hands, or three feet. But the more one leaves out all the ugliness of the things mentioned above and instead makes straight, strong, clear, and necessary things that all people usually love, the better the work becomes—for such things are now considered beautiful <note>original: *hubſch*; Dürer uses this to describe an objective, pleasing harmony of form.</note>. 

But beauty is so contained within man, and our judgment of it is so doubtful, that we may find two people who are both very beautiful and lovely, and yet neither is like the other in any single piece or part, neither in measurement nor in character. We do not even understand which of them is more beautiful; so blind is our perception <note>original: *erkantnus*</note>. Therefore, when we pass judgment upon it, it is uncertain. Yet, in certain parts, one may still surpass the other, even if it remains unrecognizable to us.

<summary>Dürer argues that true artistic skill is "plucked" from nature through diligent observation and mathematical proof (Geometry), eventually forming a "treasure of the heart" that allows a master to create without a model. He defines beauty as the removal of both deficiency and excess, while acknowledging the subjective and often blind nature of human judgment.</summary>

<keywords>Nature, Geometry, Artistic skill, Treasure of the heart, Beauty, Human judgment, Proportion, Anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s discourse on the variety of human forms and the artist's relationship with nature, building on the previous discussion of consistency in age and physique. It explores how the artist's internal knowledge and external observation must combine to create works that reflect the diversity of human nature.</meta>

From this, it follows that no powerful artist should devote himself to one style alone, but rather that he should be practiced and knowledgeable in many ways and in every kind of style. From this, it comes that he will be able to create whatever kind of figure is requested of him. Based on the aforementioned principles <note>original: *meynungen*; here referring to the artist's theoretical foundations and artistic judgment.</note>, one may know how to create an angry, a kind, or any other kind of figure. Each figure can be well-made in its own right. Thus, if someone comes to you wanting a somber <note>original: *vntreuff*; likely "untreuf," meaning cheerless or gloomy.</note> Saturnine figure, or a Martial one, or one representing Venus <note>original: *Venerem*; the Latin name for the goddess of love, used here to denote a graceful or beautiful type of person.</note> that should look lovely and graceful, then from the previously mentioned teachings—if you are practiced in them—you will easily know what measurements and style you should use. Thus, through external measurements, every type of human can be indicated: those who are of a fiery, airy, watery, or earthy nature <note>These refer to the four temperaments (choleric, sanguine, phlegmatic, and melancholic) which 16th-century science believed were determined by the balance of these four elements in the body.</note>. For the power of art, as said before, masters all works. True artists recognize in an instant which is a powerful work, and from this, a great love is born in those who understand it. True masters <note>original: *gesellen*; literally "companions" or "journeymen," referring to skilled craftsmen who have completed their apprenticeship.</note> know this well and know what the proper practice is. For knowledge is true, but opinion often deceives. Therefore, let no one believe in himself too much, lest he become erring in his work and fail. For this reason, it is very useful for one who deals with such things to see many kinds of good images, especially those made by famous, good masters, and to hear them spoken of. However, you should always take note of their flaws and think of how they might be improved. Do not let yourself be talked into only one style that a particular master follows; for everyone likes to make things similar to themselves, whatever pleases them. But if you observe many, take the best for your own use; for error is found in almost all opinions. No matter how well we make a work, it could always be made better—just as with people: however handsome one may be found, an even more beautiful one might yet be found. But let everyone adopt what is most certain, whether he learns it from another or discovers it himself from life. Yet let everyone beware of learning from those who speak well of the matter but have always produced reprehensible, incompetent works with their own hands—of whom I have seen many. For if you follow them, they will mislead you, as witnessed by their work and their lack of skill. For there is a great difference between talking about a thing and making it. Yet a truth should not be rejected because an unlearned person says it; for it is possible that a peasant might tell you the error in your work, even though he cannot instruct or teach you how you should improve it.

Anyone who has never learned anything of this art before and wishes to make a beginning from this little book must read this with diligence and learn to understand what he reads. He should take on only a little at a time and practice it well until he can do it, and only then begin something else. For understanding must begin to grow along with practice <note>original: *gebrauch*; the practical application or habit of the craft.</note>, so that the hand can do what the will desires in the mind. From this, over time, grows the certainty of art and of practice. For these two must be together; for one without the other is worth nothing. It should also be noted that while an ordinary person <note>original: *gemeyner maß*; literally "a common measure," referring here to the general public or those without specialized training.</note> can recognize the better from the worse, no one understands how to judge a work more perfectly than an intelligent artist who has often proven his skill through his own work.

<summary>Dürer argues that a master artist must be versatile, capable of depicting various temperaments and planetary types through correct proportions. He emphasizes the necessity of balancing theoretical understanding with constant physical practice and warns against teachers who lack practical skill.</summary>
<keywords>versatility, temperaments, four elements, Saturnine, Martial, Venusian, practice vs. theory, artistic judgment, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page concludes the third book of Dürer’s treatise. It transitions from the technical application of his measurement systems to a philosophical defense of his methods, addressing potential criticisms regarding the speed of work and the scalability of his proportions from small sketches to large-scale monuments.</meta>

Now one might say: who would want to always endure the toil and labor of spending so much time just to measure a single figure, especially when it often happens that one must produce perhaps twenty or thirty different figures in a short time? In such a case, it is not my opinion that a person should measure everything they do at all times. But once you have learned how to measure well, and have acquired understanding along with practice—so that you can create a thing out of free certainty <note>original: *freyer gewißheyt*; meaning an internalized, confident knowledge that does not require constant checking of references.</note> and know how to do right by every object—then it is not always necessary to measure every single thing. For your acquired art <note>original: *vberkumne kunst*; Dürer uses "art" here to mean both theoretical knowledge and technical skill.</note> provides you with a good **eye-measure**, and then the practiced hand becomes obedient. Thus, the power of art drives error from your work and prevents you from making mistakes; for you know the principles and, through your knowledge, you become undaunted and entirely ready in your work, so that you do not make a single stroke or blow in vain. This agility means you do not need to deliberate for long when your head is full of art. Through this, your work appears artistic, lovely, powerful, free, and good, and it will be praised by everyone, for **correctness** <note>original: *gerechtikeyt*; in this context, it refers to the "rightness" or geometric truth of the proportions.</note> is mixed into it.

But if you have no right foundation, it is not possible for you to make anything correct and good, even if you had the greatest practice in the world in the freedom of the hand. Indeed, such freedom is more of a pitfall <note>original: *gesencknuß*; literally a "sinking" or a hollow, implying a deceptive path or a trap.</note> if it leads you astray. Therefore, no freedom should exist without art, just as art is hidden without practice; the two must be joined together as said above. For this reason, it is necessary that one learns to measure with true artistry. Whoever can do that well creates wonderful things. For the human form cannot be traced with simple straightedges or compasses; rather, it must be drawn from point to point, as mentioned before. Outside of right measurements, no one will create anything good.

Now it may happen that some, when they transfer these previously described measurements of figures into a large-scale work, might fail through their own lack of skill. They might then lay the blame on me and say that my drafting is correct in small things, but in large works, it is deceptive. This cannot be so. For either the small is right and the large is good, or the small is bad and the large is worth nothing at all. Therefore, the argument cannot be divided in this matter; for a circle drawn with a compass <note>original: *zirckelriß*</note> remains round whether it be small or large, and the same applies to a square. Therefore, every proportion remains true to itself whether it be large or small; in the same way that in song, one **octave** <note>original: *octaff*; Dürer draws a parallel to music theory, where a mathematical ratio (2:1) creates the same harmony regardless of whether the pitch is high or low.</note> holds true to another, even if one is high and the other low, it is still the same tone.

It should also be noted that a pre-made figure can be disguised or altered in many ways and fashions so that it is no longer recognized, and yet in doing so, nothing is added to or taken away from its measurements.

->End of the Third Book.<-

<note>A decorative calligraphic flourish or printer's mark consisting of two interlaced circles with an elegant cursive stroke. Such ornamental woodcuts were common in 16th-century German printing to signify the end of a section.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Albrecht Dürer, Measurement, Art, Eye-measure, Proportion, Compass-circle, Human Form, Octave, Square.

<summary>Dürer argues that rigorous study of proportion eventually grants the artist an intuitive "eye-measure," allowing for fast, confident work without constant measuring. He also defends his system by explaining that geometric proportions remain harmoniously consistent regardless of the scale of the finished work.</summary>
<keywords>eye-measure, artistic practice, theory and practice, scalability, geometric truth, musical analogy, Book Three conclusion</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the fourth and final book of Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion." Having established the ideal measurements for various body types in the previous volumes, Dürer now moves from static measurement to "kinematics"—how these figures move, bend, and transition from a rigid pose into a natural gesture.</meta>

## The fourth book indicates how and where one
## should bend the previously described figures.

<note>A decorative woodcut historiated initial 'S' featuring figures and scrolling vine-work. This fine example of early modern German book illustration shows high-quality Renaissance woodcut artistry used for decorative initials at the start of major sections.</note>

**S**ince much has been described in the previous three books regarding how figures may be made, altered, and varied through many different distinctions, it is therefore necessary that one knows where they should be bent and turned in their limbs, as one desires, as far as the body can tolerate it. For the figures, if left as straight as they were described before, would be of no use and would be unpleasing to look upon. And let a proportion be as good as it may, if one attaches an ill gesture to it, the figure will be disgraced. Likewise, a lesser proportion can be deemed worthy through a good gesture. 

But as for how one should speak of the limbs and how they marvelously fit into one another, those who deal with <term>Anatomy</term> <note>original: *Anatomia*; Dürer refers here to the scientific study of the body's structure, which was a burgeoning field in the Renaissance.</note> know this. I leave it to them to speak of it, for I do not wish to speak further of it here, except for as much as I cannot avoid for necessity’s sake. Therefore, note that the previously described figures, when seen from the side, are to be bent through the entire spine from the nape of the neck down to the hip, along all the horizontal lines <note>original: *zwerch linien*; these are the cross-sectional measurement lines Dürer uses in his diagrams to define the body's proportions.</note> and in between. Yet they are to be bent more forward than backward. And this bending is applied in the hindmost quarter of the body toward the back, on all horizontal lines and in between—except that the upper leg <note>The thigh.</note> with its joint is bent in the middle of the horizontal line where the hip ends. Bend the knee also in the middle of its horizontal line. Bend the foot by the ankles of the shinbone, and the toes in their joints. But the figure seen from the front is to be bent on both sides from the neck through the entire spine. To better note this, I have placed small triangles and little rings at the principal points where they are to be bent in the following figures.

Now it is further necessary that one speaks distinctly and understandably of such bending. Therefore, it should be noted: everything that belongs to such bending and is necessary must be observed with special diligence, so that one can bend and pose each thing seriously or charmingly. For first, one must use a grim posture for stern subjects, and for love, a friendly one. And therefore, what pertains to and depends upon bending, understand this well in your practice. To this end, note these six following distinctions, and pay close attention to these words in all bending.

->These are the distinctions:
{ Bent
{ Curved
{ Turned
{ Twisted
{ Stretched / Contracted
{ And shifted.<-

These six aforementioned terms are all performed in a person, more or less according to how they move. And these six kinds of distinctions—how I mean each one and how they are to be understood—I will explain through lines as follows.

<summary>This page introduces Book IV, transitioning from static anatomical proportions to the principles of movement and posture. Dürer emphasizes that even perfect proportions look poor without natural "gestures" and introduces six specific types of bodily movement: bending, curving, turning, twisting, stretching/contracting, and shifting.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, movement, bending, spine, joints, gesture, aesthetics, kinematics, horizontal lines, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues from Dürer’s introduction to the fourth book, where he transitions from describing static human proportions to explaining how to manipulate those figures into poses. To do this, he first defines three fundamental ways a line—and by extension, a limb—can be moved: by bending at a joint, by curving, or by pivoting at a central point.</meta>

First, I wish to address that which is **articulated** <term>gebogen; literally "bent," but here referring to a line composed of straight segments joined at specific points</term>. Set down a straight line *a. b.* and give it two joints or two points, *c.* and *d.* At these two points *c.* and *d.*, bend the line back and forth as you wish. However, the lengths between *a.c.*, *c.d.*, and *d.b.* must always remain straight. You can see the figure of this sketched below.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates an articulated or bent line (gebogen) with points labeled a, b, c, and d. This demonstrates how a rigid limb might be positioned by "breaking" it only at the joints while keeping the segments themselves straight.</note>

---

Secondly, let that which is **curved** <term>Krümbt</term> be named. I again set down a line *a. b.*, which one may draw with a compass or pull into a curve by hand—curved backward at the top and forward at the bottom, or in opposite directions. In short, let this line be drawn curved according to whatever necessity requires. This is indicated here below in these three lines.

<note>A geometric diagram shows three variations of curved lines (Krümbt), including S-curves and simple arcs. These represent the organic, fluid bending of the torso or neck, as opposed to the mechanical bending of a jointed limb.</note>

---

Thirdly, let that which is **pivoting** <term>Wenden; literally "turning"</term> be named. If two lines—let one be *a.b.* and the other *c.d.*—lie exactly on top of one another, they appear as a single line. Thereafter, place a point *e.* in the middle. At this point *e.*, pivot one line upward or downward toward whichever side you wish. And although their four ends *a., c.* and *b., d.* move away from one another, the two lines nevertheless remain joined to one another at point *e.*, just as is sketched in the following figure.

<note>A geometric diagram illustrates intersecting or "turning" lines (Wenden), showing two lines meeting and rotating at a central point e. This principle explains how parts of the body, like the head or torso, might rotate around a fixed axis.</note>

<summary>Dürer defines the three geometric principles—articulation, curvature, and pivoting—that allow an artist to move a proportional figure into a natural pose. These definitions serve as the mathematical foundation for human motion in his treatise.</summary>
<keywords>articulation, curvature, pivoting, geometry of motion, pose, joints, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric definitions of movement. Having previously covered articulation, curving, and pivoting, he now moves into complex transformations: winding (helices), extension/shortening, and shifting. These concepts are the foundation for his method of "transferring" or "distorting" human proportions into different poses and perspectives.</meta>

<sig>B ij</sig>

Fourthly, let it be called **winding** <term>winden</term>, when two lines, *a. b.* and *c. d.*, are wound around each other in the manner of a screw <term>schrauffens weyß; referring to a helical or spiral motion</term>, as the following figure informs you.

<note>A geometric diagram shows two lines intertwined like a helix, labeled with the points a, b, c, and d. This illustration defines the helical or "screw-like" linear movement used to represent twisting in the human body.</note>

Fifthly, let it be called **extending and shortening** <term>gestreckt vnd krůpfft; "gestreckt" means stretched, while "krůpfft" (modern *gekröpft*) implies being bent or crimped to occupy less length</term>, when a line is pulled apart and lengthened, or crimped into itself and shortened. I can give you no better way to understand this than to place two lines side by side: a long one, *a. b.*, and a short one, *c. d.*

<note>A geometric diagram shows two vertical parallel lines of unequal lengths to demonstrate the concepts of extension and shortening, labeled a, b and c, d.</note>

Sixthly, let it be called **shifting** <term>geschoben</term>. As you already know, when two lines lie exactly upon one another, they appear as a single line. But if they are shifted away from one another so that they no longer touch, then there are two. From this, parallel lines <term>bar linien; literally "pair lines," an early term for parallels</term> or others that converge may be drawn, shifted up or down, or moved and pushed however one wishes. This is indicated here below by the two lines *a. b.* and *c. d.*

<note>Geometrical diagrams show two sets of shifted lines to illustrate the concept of "shifting" (geschoben). This visual representation shows how geometric planes or lines can be displaced, a fundamental concept for Dürer's later instructions on changing the perspective or posture of a figure.</note>

<summary>Dürer defines the final three geometric transformations—winding, extending/shortening, and shifting—completing his toolkit for manipulating human figures in space.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, winding, extension, shortening, shifting, parallel lines, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues from the geometric definitions of movement—bending, curving, turning, winding, stretching, and shifting—and applies these abstract principles directly to the anatomy of the human body. Dürer establishes that the body behaves like a three-dimensional volume governed by the same rules as lines and planes.</meta>

You should know that just as these aforementioned things occur in lines, so too do they occur in a flat surface. And as they happen in flat surfaces, they also happen in every soft body <term>corpus; Dürer uses the Latin term for a three-dimensional volume</term> that is wide, thick, and movable. For such a body is to be bent, curved, turned, wound, stretched, shortened, and shifted in the same way as the lines, because no body exists without lines. Therefore, all the things determined above may be applied to a body just as they are to lines. Now, observe for what purposes these six aforementioned distinctions are to be used in the human form.

**Firstly**, the word **bending** <term>gebogen</term> is used in regard to the human limbs, though not in the sense that the bones themselves are bent—for they would break. For this reason, I have placed in the figure at the front that the lines between the points must remain straight. <note>Dürer is emphasizing that while a limb "bends" at the joint, the skeletal structure between joints remains rigid.</note>

**Secondly**, **curving** <term>krummen</term> is to be used in the human body for the veins <term>geeder; a term often including tendons and sinews in 16th-century anatomy</term> and the flesh. For when a person leans to one side, backwards, or forwards, the veins and the flesh curve either inward or outward.

**Thirdly**, **turning** <term>Wenden</term> is used in the human limbs; for example, when one turns the head, or likewise the body, the arms, thighs, and most parts of the limbs may be turned in this way.

**Fourthly**, **winding** <term>Winden</term> is used in the human body for the flesh and the veins; for if you have turned a part, as mentioned before, then all the soft things wind themselves from one end to the other, whether it be in the neck, the torso, the arms, or the thighs. <note>This refers to the spiral-like twisting of muscles and skin during rotation.</note>

**Fifthly**, note that **stretching and shortening** <term>Strecken vnnd Krüpffen; "krüpffen" means to crimp or contract</term> are often used in the human body. Stretching makes a part thin, but shortening makes it thick; take heed of this. When you stretch an arm out, the veins pull tight and stretch the flesh lengthwise, so the arm becomes thinner. But when you pull the arm inward, the flesh bunches up over itself and the arm becomes thicker. It is the same in the other parts of the body as well. <note>This is a foundational observation of how muscle mass displaces during contraction.</note>

**Sixthly**, **shifting** <term>Schieben</term> is used in the human body, specifically in the limbs, such that one part shifts in front of, behind, or beside another. Or, when one limb is bent toward another or the body is curved, the flesh shifts outward, inward, or to one side.

These six different things, as stated above, may all happen at once in a human body through movement. Thus, the limbs bend, turn, and shift, and simultaneously the flesh curves, winds, stretches, and shortens. One part pushes the other, as is commonly found in nature.

From these described things, you may give your figures any gesture <term>geberd</term> you wish, provided it is useful to you and consistent with nature. It is especially important to note that anything excessive looks poor; therefore, one should do neither too much nor too little of these things. Otherwise, the gestures will become too fierce or too lazy, unless it is one’s intention to diligently create bold or sleepy figures. This applies to men, women, children, horses, and other creatures.

It is important to know that in all my previously described figures, if they are bent here and there, they do not remain the same thickness and width in all parts at all ends as previously described. For movement takes from one part and gives to another; therefore, things are transformed. The best way to learn this clearly is by making many drawings of living people, for there one sees how everything happens. Therefore, let everyone take care that he does not lead his work astray.

<summary>Dürer applies his six geometric principles of movement to human anatomy, explaining how muscles thicken when contracted and how proportions shift during motion. He emphasizes that while bones remain straight, the "soft body" of flesh and veins must be depicted with naturalistic displacement learned from life drawing.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, movement, muscle contraction, naturalism, bending, curving, turning, winding, stretching, shifting, life drawing</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s transition from abstract geometric theory to practical application. Having defined the types of movement (bending, turning, etc.) on the previous page, he now introduces a specific technique for "blocking out" the human form into simplified geometric volumes to assist artists and sculptors in visualizing complex poses.</meta>

<sig>B iij</sig>

Furthermore, if one wishes to bend a figure, there are many ways to do so; one in particular is very useful for sculptors <note>original: "bildhawern"</note>. When <term>drafting</term> <gloss>auffreyssen; the process of geometric layout</gloss> a figure, one should enclose and contain the primary parts of the limbs between the <term>horizontal lines</term> <gloss>zwerch linien</gloss> of the whole proportion within <term>squared</term> <gloss>gefierte</gloss> or <term>angular volumes</term> <gloss>eckette corpus</gloss>. This technique serves for <term>roughing out</term> <gloss>bossiern</gloss>, as they call it. Just as the head is contained in such a block, so too should the neck and the torso be handled, piece by piece, down to the waist, from there to the end of the hip, then the thigh down to the knee, and likewise the shins and the feet. The same should be done with the arms. In such an angular volume, every part, point, and line of the body may be very easily indicated through the <term>vertical</term> <gloss>auffrechten</gloss> and <term>horizontal parallel lines</term> <gloss>zwerch bar linien</gloss>, showing where each feature is to be found. However, such <term>squaring</term> <gloss>fierungen</gloss> need not, and cannot, always be perfectly square or equilateral; rather, they should be elongated and adjusted according to the requirements of the body, with their corners aligned so that they provide a helpful guide to the understanding.

But to demonstrate how one may bend and turn the previously described figures as mentioned before, I will take the first described man's figure from the first book as my instruction. I will begin first with the head, leaving it upright but turning it to one side. To do this: Take the flattened ground plan of the face and turn the front part toward you and slightly to the side, so that the <term>squaring</term> <gloss>vierung</gloss> of the base stands almost <term>diagonally</term> <gloss>vber ort; literally "across the corner," meaning rotated</gloss>. Since the flattened <term>square</term> <gloss>vierung</gloss> is marked at its four corners with the numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4, you must project upward from these four points with <term>vertical lines</term> <gloss>auffrechten linien</gloss> as far as you require. Thus, the turned <term>square</term> <gloss>vierung</gloss> is found, showing how wide it becomes here when rotated.

Next, intersect these four vertical lines with a long <term>horizontal line</term> <gloss>zwerch lini</gloss> marked **x . y.** and place the upright, forward-facing <term>face</term> <gloss>angesicht</gloss> in its <term>square</term> <gloss>vierung</gloss> beside the flattened ground plan. Then, transfer all the <term>horizontal lines</term> <gloss>zwerch linien</gloss> of the forward-facing face through the four lines that were drawn upward from the four corners of the flattened ground plan. Thus, in this new <term>square</term> <gloss>vierung</gloss>, you will find through the newly drawn <term>horizontal lines</term> the height and placement of the forehead, eyebrows, eyes, nose, ears, mouth, and chin. Afterward, project upward from the foreshortened face of the flattened ground plan—from all corners and ends of the nose, eyes, mouth, chin, ears, forehead, neck, head, and whatever else is necessary—using <term>vertical parallel lines</term> <gloss>auffrechten barlinnien</gloss> into the upper plan, up to the <term>horizontal lines</term>. Within and between these, each feature shall be found. Where the positions and ends of each feature are struck, one should then draw the form of the face. In this way, the turned view is found quite correctly. This requires diligence to do it properly. But to demonstrate this more understandably, I have drafted such a turned face below.

<note>A decorative calligraphic flourish or printer's ornament is placed here as a page filler, typical of 16th-century German typography.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to "block out" the human body into geometric volumes (cuboids) to assist sculptors and painters in posing figures. He provides a specific geometric method for rotating a head by projecting points from a ground plan onto a vertical grid.</summary>
<keywords>stereometry, blocking out, orthographic projection, figure drawing, sculpture technique, foreshortening, facial proportions, geometric drafting</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on transforming the human head in space. Following his explanation of how to "block out" the body into geometric volumes, he now provides a step-by-step mathematical method for foreshortening the face when it is tilted upward and viewed from the front.</meta>

<note>A technical diagram illustrates the geometric proportions of the human head in profile, frontal, and tilted upward views. Dürer utilizes a precise grid system with lettered labels (a through k, and others) to demonstrate how the features of the face shift and compress when the head is tilted back, showing the intersection of geometry and anatomy.</note>

In the same way that I have turned the aforementioned face to one side, I will now direct the grid from the side with the chin tilted upwards and the head tilted backwards, and then see how it appears from the front; this is how it is to be done. Take the **square** <gloss>vierung; the geometric frame containing the drawing</gloss> of the **side-view** <gloss>nebensichtigen</gloss> face with all its contents, and place its back lower corner upon a **horizontal line** <gloss>zwerch lini</gloss> **r. y.**, and at the front corner by the chin, direct it upwards. Thereafter, draw a **horizontal parallel line** <gloss>zwerch bar linien</gloss> **t. z. s.** <note>original: "tz. ß."</note> at the upper corner of this square, relative to the lower line **r. y.** Between these two horizontal parallel lines, the two corners of the side-view square must be contained **diagonally** <gloss>vber ort; literally "over the place" or across the corners</gloss>.

Afterward, on the side of the side-view face, between the two horizontal lines, create the upward-turned face in its **frontal** <gloss>fürsichtig</gloss> width, as was described before; to do this, do as follows: Draw between the two horizontal lines two **vertical lines** <gloss>auffrechten linien</gloss> **a. b.** for the width of the frontal face, and draw within them the other vertical lines **c. d. e. f. g. h. i. k.** as they were described before, between which all things are divided according to their width.

Then, by means of transfer from the upright side-view face, draw all features across into the square of the frontal face using horizontal parallel lines, starting from the height of the...

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric method for drawing a human head tilted backward and viewed from the front, using a system of transferring points from a profile grid to a frontal grid.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, perspective, foreshortening, geometry in art, head construction, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric instructions for projecting the features of the human head from various angles. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing the placement of vertical lines to define the width of a frontal face within a tilted grid.</meta>

<sig>B iiij</sig>

...of the head, the forehead, eyebrows, nose, ears, mouth, chin, neck, and whatever else is necessary. From these crossed intersecting lines <note>These are the construction lines used in orthographic projection to map points from a profile view to a frontal view.</note> you will be instructed where you should draw the shape-lines of all things in the face differently than before. Therefore, take good note while drawing the shape of the face where everything falls. And in the same way that one uses these things in the upright face, one also uses them in the downward tilt.

But to understand this more clearly, I have subsequently drawn out both types of faces—the upright and the foreshortened <note>original: "nider gedückt," literally "pressed down," referring to the compression of features seen in perspective</note>. These two side-view and front-view faces, both the upright and the foreshortened, may also be turned to one side; in both cases, a change occurs that would look strange in the drawing. For this, however, the foreshortened ground-plans <note>original: "nider gedruckten grundt," the mathematical base or horizontal projection upon which the figure stands</note> of both parts must serve, as they now fall from the upright and the bent face.

In such shifting of the ground-plans, great skill <term>kunst; in Dürer’s time, this meant both artistic skill and systematic, scientific knowledge</term> must be used, for these things reveal themselves quite accurately therein. And thus one may proceed with all kinds of previously described faces of men and women, and not only with the face, but with the entire body.

And in the same way that one places the head into a foreshortened ground-plan, so also may the entire body be placed into a foreshortened ground-plan where necessary. But before one does that, one should first bend the image and place it in a proper pose <gloss>bossen; a posture, model, or plastic form</gloss> as one desires it. Then only should one drop down from all points and ends with vertical parallel lines into the foreshortened ground-plan.

<note>A complex technical diagram illustrates the orthographic projection of a human head from profile to frontal view, adjusted for a tilted perspective or "foreshortened ground-plan." It features numerous construction lines and alphabetic labels (a-z) along with symbols, characteristic of Albrecht Dürer's studies on human proportion. This diagram represents a masterpiece of Renaissance scientific illustration and the application of geometry to art and anatomy.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to use intersecting construction lines to accurately draw the human head and body from tilted or foreshortened perspectives. He emphasizes that the same geometric principles used for the head can be applied to the entire body to maintain correct proportions in any pose.</summary>
<keywords>orthographic projection, foreshortening, ground-plan, human proportion, geometry in art, construction lines, anatomy, pose</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on transforming the human figure through motion. <sig>y</sig>

<note>A technical diagram from Albrecht Dürer's work on human proportion, showing a human head inscribed within a coordinate grid and a construction arc, demonstrating perspective and fore-shortening techniques. This is an original woodcut diagram from Albrecht Dürer's seminal 1528 work "Four Books on Human Proportion," a foundational text in Western art history and geometric perspective.</note>

->e<-

And so that one may understand more clearly how the figures may be bent, and how they appear when they are made so, I will first bend the previously described man of seven heads <note>Dürer uses a "seven-head" canon for this specific figure, meaning the total height of the body is seven times the length of the head.</note> at the places where he is to be bent, as mentioned before. And take the downward-tilted head of the man described earlier and set it in profile <note>original: "nebensichtig," literally "side-seeing"</note> upon the frontal <note>original: "fürsichtigen," literally "forward-seeing"</note> man; thus his face will be turned toward his left side. But the frontal face I set upon the profile body, for the single man must be drawn <term>aufgerissen; this term refers to the technical process of drafting or "ripping open" a geometric plan</term> in two ways. Thereafter, I bend him above the flanks <note>the soft area of the waist between the ribs and hips</note> toward his left side; thus the right shoulder becomes higher than the left. I also bend the back straight upon several transverse lines <term>zwerch linien; horizontal construction lines used as axes for rotation</term> so that the body is not inclined with a completely straight, stiff line. And I stretch his left arm slightly in front of the body. But below the belt, I bend his hip a little toward his left side and let him stand with his left foot under his head. But his right leg I stretch a little toward his right side, and below the knee I bend the shinbone a little behind him; thus he must stand with this foot upon his toes.

Once the frontal man is first drawn, then one draws the man from the side, using the transfer <note>original: "obertrag," referring to the method of orthographic projection where points are moved horizontally from one view (front) to create another (side)</note> through the transverse lines which clearly show where every part finds its place, within which one must draw the shape-lines anew and differently than before.

And in the same way, I also bend the seven-head-tall woman who belongs to the man described above, but in the opposite manner, for I direct her face upward; yet it is also turned to the left side as was done with the man. And just as the man is bent toward his left side, I bend the woman toward her right side, yet with a feminine gesture <note>original: "weybliche̅ geberd," suggesting a softer or more graceful posture appropriate to 16th-century artistic decorum</note>. And I make use of the transfer from one into the other, as mentioned before. And I have drafted these above-described figures in the following pages for you to follow, and I show thereby that anyone may use these things differently and according to their own pleasure. And take special note that, along with the bending, you may turn or rotate the parts upon every transverse line—as much as is possible—toward whichever side you wish. Understand it thus: if the man is bent with his left shoulder lower toward the hip, you may also turn or rotate that same shoulder backward and the right one forward, so that the body moves along with them. This is to be done in all parts. You may use all this even further than is described here.

<summary>Dürer explains how to pose the human figure by bending and rotating body parts along geometric transverse lines, using orthographic projection to maintain proportions in both front and side views.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, orthographic projection, figure drawing, contrapposto, technical drafting, Albrecht Dürer, transverse lines, anatomy in art</keywords>
