--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the male figure, shifting from static measurements to a dynamic geometric method. Dürer introduces his "block" system, using cubes to represent body segments to explain how the human form can be rotated and manipulated in space.</meta>

**Following this**, I wish to look a bit further into how I might bend, shift, turn, twist, stretch, and compress the figures, and through practical application provide a correct understanding, just as a small sketch has already been provided for bending and turning.

And to reach such an understanding more easily, I will make use of two **squared bodies** <term>original: "gefieratẽ corpus"; referring here to cubes or three-dimensional rectangular blocks</term> which shall stand upon one another. Note that in German, a squared plane <note>a two-dimensional square</note> should be understood as distinct from a squared body <note>a three-dimensional cube</note>. However, it should be considered that if such bodies are taken as being soft, one may pull them apart, stretch them, or compress them, just as one handles all the other things mentioned above; I shall thus put this into practice.

In place of the limbs, we will set two squared bodies upon one another, as described above, and mark each corner of the two squared planes where they meet so that they may be clearly distinguished. Therefore, mark the four corners of the lower plane of the upper body with **a, b, c, d**, and the four corners of the upper plane of the lower body with **1, 2, 3, 4**. Let their mutual middle point, or center, be **o**, so that they lie exactly upon each other. This point **o** always remains stationary, no matter how one turns the other elements this way or that.

Now, I wish to draw a **ground plan** <note>original: "nidergedruckten grund"; a top-down geometric projection</note> of the two bodies standing upon one another beneath them, and mark the corners with letters and numbers as the vertical lines falling down from the **elevation** <note>original: "vbertrag"; the side or front view from which points are projected down into the plan</note> indicate in the ground plan. Thus, **a** and **1** fall into one corner, **b** and **2** into another, **c** and **3** into the third, and **d** and **4** into the fourth, just as I have sketched here alongside.

Now we wish to turn the upper body slightly to one side, so that no corner remains directly above the other. Do this in the ground plan so that corner **a** moves away from corner **1**, **b** from **2**, **c** from **3**, and **d** from **4**. If you then project upward from these shifted corners with vertical lines through the elevation, the upper squared body will appear as it would when turned, just as I have sketched next to the previous ground plan.

This turning or rotating may be applied to all **transverse lines** <note>original: "zwerch linien"; horizontal cross-sections of the body</note> that I have drawn through the human limbs at every joint, as far as nature will naturally allow.

And from this principle, the previously described turned face was constructed.

<note>Four geometric diagrams illustrate the projection and rotation of squared bodies (cubes) shown in both ground plan and elevation views. These serve as a mathematical basis for understanding how parts of the human body, represented here by abstract blocks, can be turned or twisted in space while maintaining proportional accuracy.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains a geometric method for rotating and posing the human figure by representing body segments as flexible cubes. He demonstrates how to project these rotations from a top-down ground plan into a vertical elevation view.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, projection, squared bodies, cubes, ground plan, elevation, rotation, anatomical joints, Albrecht Dürer, perspective, transverse lines, 16th-century art theory</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric demonstration of how to manipulate solid blocks in space. Having previously established how to stack two cubes (representing segments of the human body) and rotate them, he now explains how to tilt these blocks sideways and forward while projecting their new positions onto a flat ground plan.</meta>

D

So now, once the previously described or squared **body** <term>corpus: a three-dimensional block or cube used here to represent a part of the human figure</term> is turned onto one side, and the corners have moved away from one another, we shall furthermore bend it to one side after it has been turned, so that it does not remain upright. And afterward, we shall lay it again into a flattened **ground plan** <term>grund: a two-dimensional geometric projection or plan view</term>. But so that the upper corners of the **body** <note>original: "corpus"</note> may be recognized when one wishes to lay them into a flattened ground plan, we will mark them with **e, f, g, h**, just as the four lower corners are marked with **a, b, c, d**. And if you do this as described above, it will look as I have drawn it here below, shown in the **transfer** <term>vbertrag: the process of projecting points from one view to another to maintain proportions</term> with the vertical lines in the ground plan.

<column-break/>

Now that this **body** <note>original: "corpus"</note> atop the other has been turned in two ways and bent to one side, we will now, for the third time, bend it forward once more at the top. However, let the point **o** always remain stationary in both parts of the **bodies** <note>original: "corpora"</note>, unless it should be necessary to do otherwise in certain instances. Thus, the turning and the bending to the side and forward happen together. Afterward, lay all of this into a flattened ground plan through the **transfer** <note>original: "vbertrag"</note> as previously mentioned, and it will appear as I have drawn it below.

---

<note>A geometric diagram showing the perspective projection of a rectangular block that has been rotated and tilted to the side. It includes labels for the corners (a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h), a central pivot point (o), and numerical markers (1, 2, 3, 4, 9) showing how construction lines are projected from a plan view to create the illusion of three-dimensional depth.</note>

<note>A geometric diagram illustrating a more complex perspective projection of a rectangular block tilted in multiple directions—both sideways and forward. It displays intricate construction lines, vanishing points, and vertex labels (a-h, o, 1-9) to demonstrate the mathematical principles of advanced foreshortening.</note>

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains the geometric process of rotating and tilting three-dimensional blocks (representing body segments) and how to accurately project these complex movements onto a two-dimensional ground plan.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, perspective, projection, foreshortening, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, corpus, ground plan, technical drawing, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric demonstration of how to manipulate solid blocks in space. He transitions from rigid, straight-edged geometric forms to the more organic, curved shapes required to accurately represent the human body's "soft" tissues during movement.</meta>

As was shown before, when two squared **bodies** <term>corpora: geometric blocks used here to represent structural segments of the human figure, such as the torso and pelvis</term> are stacked upon one another, the upper body may be turned to one side or bent, and likewise it may be bent forward or backward. The lower body may also be handled in the same way. As one wishes, one may choose to let one remain stationary while moving the other, or move both of them together as the specific use requires. 

However, when such things are **projected** <term>aufgerissen: the technical process of drafting or drawing out a three-dimensional form onto a two-dimensional surface</term>, their hard edges and planes intersect one another quite strangely in both the vertical and flattened **ground plans** <term>grund: a two-dimensional geometric projection or plan view</term>, as you can see before your eyes. For in some places, the bodies merge into one another, while on the opposite side, they move outward or away from each other. 

And when this is applied to soft things <note>Dürer is shifting his focus from pure geometry to the pliable nature of human flesh and muscle.</note>, one part stretches while the other part **bunches up** <note>original: "krupfft" – referring to the way skin and muscle fold or contract when a limb or torso is bent.</note> over itself. Because of this, in a finished work, what is taken from one part must be given to the other, so that the gaps are filled.

<note>A geometric diagram shows two intersecting and tilted cuboids with straight edges, labeled with letters and numbers for spatial reference. This illustrates how the "blocks" of the body overlap when the torso twists or leans.</note>

Now it should also be noted that, just as the previously described bodies are turned and bent with straight lines, one may also **curve** <note>original: "krumen"</note> all lines, sides, and planes within a "soft" body however one wishes. For a clearer understanding of this, I will redraw the two previously projected bodies below—just as they were turned and bent before—but I will curve several of their side lines and planes, so that the drawn lines may make the concept understandable to you.

<note>A second geometric diagram shows two intersecting cuboids, but here the edges are curved and distorted. This illustrates the transition from rigid geometric theory to a more naturalistic representation of a human body in motion.</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to rotate and tilt the geometric blocks that represent the human torso, noting that in organic forms, movement causes one side of the body to stretch while the other contracts. He introduces the use of curved lines to better simulate the appearance of flesh and muscle in these shifted positions.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, anatomy, projection, spatial rotation, foreshortening, muscle contraction, organic form</keywords>

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric demonstration of how to manipulate solid blocks in space. He transitions from simple bending to the more complex "twisting" of shapes, laying the groundwork for how a student of art can use these blocks to represent the complex movements of the human body in three-dimensional space.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>
-><sig>D ii</sig><-

Now that the various differences in bending, curving, and other such things have been understood regarding the two squared **bodies** <term>corpora: geometric blocks or "volumes" used as a structural scaffold for the human figure</term>, there yet remains the **twisting** <note>original: "Winden" — referring to torsion or rotation along an axis.</note>. This must also be understood. This twisting is applied to the side lines from corner to corner, and thus the squared planes of the body twist along with it. It is quite a similar matter to what was said before regarding curving, except that this twisting occurs in the manner of a **screw** <note>original: "schrauffens weyss" — Dürer is describing a helical or spiral rotation.</note>. You may use such twisting according to your will, for through it, nothing remains as it was before. However, if you lay this altered upper square, with its twist, into a **ground plan** <term>grundt: a flat, two-dimensional geometric projection, like an architectural floor plan</term>, the flattened plan will appear different than it did before, as I have drawn below.

For whenever any thing that has been **projected** <term>aufgezugen/aufgerissen: the technical process of drafting a 3D form into a 2D vertical elevation or diagram</term> is moved or displaced, it creates a new ground plan. And let this also be noted: if one wishes to **turn** <note>original: "wenden" — specifically referring to rotation around a vertical axis.</note> an object, one must do so in the flattened ground plan first and then project it upward. But whatever one **bends** back and forth must be done in the upright projection first, and only then laid back into the flattened ground plan.

<note>A stereometric diagram shows a rectangular block—representing an abstracted human torso or head—subjected to twisting and tilting. It is illustrated with projection lines and a corresponding plan view (ground plan) positioned below the main figure to show how the spatial coordinates shift.</note>

And although I have used these two squared bodies up to this point, I also intend for other bodies to be handled in this same manner. For it happens that one requires long, short, narrow, wide, thick, or squared bodies, or other bodies with more sides, corners, and planes. For these same angular bodies should be made suitable to the **figure** that belongs within them; therefore, they are made with unequal planes, sides, and angles—sometimes large at the top and small at the bottom, and vice versa. Thus, it may well happen that no part of the body is equal to another. He who works in such a way will find that I do not mention this in vain. But one should handle **distorted bodies** <note>original: "verkertenn corpora" — shapes that have been transformed, inverted, or otherwise non-symmetrical.</note> in the same way as the squared ones mentioned before. Furthermore, one should proceed in this way with the **entire human body**, whether it is circumscribed by an angular body or not. Indeed, this method may be applied to many other things. For every living creature, once it has been projected, may have its limbs turned or bent at the **transverse lines** <term>zwerch linien: horizontal lines marking joints or points of movement like the waist or neck</term>—to one side, backward, or forward. You may encompass these parts within squares or angular bodies and give a figure any pose you wish, whether lying or sitting. Therefore, as mentioned before, if one wishes, one may place a previously described figure entirely into squared or angular bodies, and after the figure is so contained, bend and turn it according to one’s mind. From this, one finds the stretches, bunches, curves, and twists, as shown before, through which the right path is revealed. This way or method...

<summary>Dürer explains the "twisting" (torsion) of geometric blocks and how rotating an object requires adjusting the ground plan, while bending requires adjusting the vertical projection. He argues that these geometric scaffolds can be adapted to any part of the human body to achieve realistic poses.</summary>
<keywords>twisting, torsion, ground plan, projection, angular bodies, human figure, stereometry, rotation, bending, anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for using geometric volumes to represent the human body. It concludes the discussion on the utility of these methods for sculptors and begins a practical demonstration using a specific figure type—the "nine-head-long" man—divided into cross-sections.</meta>

[These instructions] are not useless to sculptors who are beginning to learn, and who wish to make something out of wood or stone, so that they may correctly and precisely match a thing. For through this, one finds on every flat path how to carve through the angled **bodies** <term>corpora: geometric volumes or "blocks" used as a structural scaffold for the human figure</term> what needs to be removed; so that one does not carve away too much or leave too much. But an understanding, well-practiced man does not always need such things, unless a work were exceptionally large. However, he who is artistically understanding should mark with dots the previously described figure, and then bend it; and then, only then, draw the figure lines again from point to point in the newly formed change that the displacement brings. Writing about these things is difficult, and even more tedious to read and learn because of the abundance of words, points, and signs. But there are many more advantageous ways to use these things; for if that were not so, these things would be so very difficult and hard to make. Therefore, whoever seeks in these things finds daily almost a way through which every day the things become easier to approach. Therefore it is good that one has a master who gives him daily guidance; but to learn a thing without a master is six times the toil and labor.

Now I will partly put into practice what was shown above, and I take for myself the nine-head-long man of the second little book, whose head is lengthened at the top, so that he no longer remains nine heads long; and I enclose his figure lines with individual squared or angled **bodies** <term>corpora</term> to be seen from the side and from the front. However, such bodies become high or low because of the **transverse lines** <note>original: "zwerch linten" — horizontal lines used for measurement and cross-sections.</note> that divide them; and the bodies occur unequally because of the human figure contained within them. And I will divide this whole man's length with nine cuts into ten squared bodies; none of them takes a form like any other has. And these shall be the cuts: The transverse line of the chin, the shoulder flesh, the pit of the throat, the nipples, the flanks, the hip joint, the end of the hip, the hollow of the knee, the end of the ankles at the bottom of the leg. But so that such a thing may be understood, I have first drafted this aforementioned man here below with his figure lines; and thereafter drawn him with the straight lines of the squared bodies, to be seen from the side and from the front. So that he stands upright, straight without any bend, stretches the right arm away from him, and lets the left hang down under him as you see. But after that, on the other sheet, I will draw only the squared bodies as they have been placed upon one another, and leave out the man's figure lines. Thereafter I take these squared bodies as they stand upon each other, in which the image is embodied, and lay them through the transfer into a **flattened ground plan** <term>nider gedrucktē grund: a two-dimensional geometric projection, similar to a floor plan</term>, as was done before with the head. This I have drafted below the upright drawing as it turns out.

Following that, I have further drafted what form the body takes in each cut, how it should be circled around when one looks at its **squared plane** <note>original: "gefierte ebne"</note>. One searches for and finds this from the image from the side and front views; then one sees what is missing from the **squared plane** <note>original: "gefierten plano"</note> in each cut. One should also note that where two squared bodies stand upon each other in a cut, the drawing of the body on the lower plane of the upper body is the same thing as the drawing of the body on the upper plane of the lower body. Also, I have drawn these squared cuts, in which the body is specially circled in each, with straight lines drawn crosswise for identification; so that if the squared planes are turned or displaced, the drawing of the body can be drawn in correctly again. For it often happens that the squared planes become narrow or come in a **diamond shape** <note>original: "rautens weyß" — rhomboid or lozenge-shaped, a result of shifting perspective or torsion.</note>; then the rounding of the body must be drawn correctly into them again. And in the same way as the squared plane becomes diamond-shaped, so must the cross-lines within it be drawn proportionately to the same. And where you want to divide again the things that were previously wide and have become narrow, for that you must use the **transformer** <note>original: "verkerer" — a geometric drafting tool or grid system Dürer introduced in his first book to mathematically alter proportions.</note> of the first little book. After that, I have again drafted in a flattened ground plan these circled body lines that occur in the cuts outside the squared planes as they fall upon one another, and so it may be shown how much one part overlaps the other.

<summary>Dürer explains how sculptors can use geometric "blocks" to accurately carve the human figure and introduces a practical exercise dividing a man's body into ten sections via horizontal cuts. He details how these sections and their cross-sectional "planes" can be projected into a ground plan and manipulated through perspective changes.</summary>

Now I will look further to see how the squared **volumes** <term>corpora: the Latin plural for geometric "bodies" or blocks used as a structural scaffold</term> of the body are shifted and no longer remain straight upon one another, and how the outlined shape of the body changes in the drawing on every **plane** <term>plano: a flat surface or geometric plane</term> within the cross-section. To show this clearly, I will take for myself the squared **volume** <term>corpus</term> between the cross-section of the **nipples** <term>tütlein: literally "little teats," used here as a standard anatomical landmark on the chest</term> and the **flanks** <term>weychen: the soft area of the waist between the ribs and the hips</term>. 

<note>A detailed central diagram of a torso cross-section shows the complex interaction of concentric circles and geometric grid lines used to determine body volume and curvature, representing the height of Renaissance scientific illustration.</note>

First, I set it upright according to the side on a **horizontal line** <term>zwerch lini: a transverse or horizontal line used as a reference point in perspective</term>, and I mark the four corners of the nipple cross-section at the top: front a. b., and back c. d. But for the cross-section of the **flank**, I mark front 1. 2., and back 3. 4. I then place a **ground plan** <term>nidergedruckten grund: literally a "pressed-down ground," referring to a top-down plan view or horizontal projection</term> beneath it, which I also mark with its letters and numbers as above, so that one corresponds to the other. 

After that, take the side view that stands upright on the **horizontal line**, and bend it forward and downward at the point of the **spine** <term>ruckgrads: the backbone</term>, and drop its changed ground plan beneath it, always placing the letters and numbers in their respective corners. Thus, this **volume** <term>corpus</term> is bent forward once and creates a new ground plan. 

Once this ground plan is made, take this plan and turn the front at the chest toward you and well to the right side under the aforementioned **horizontal line**. Then, proceed from all corners of the letters and numbers of the rotated ground plan upward toward the horizontal line and beyond. After that, draw from the forward-bent elevated **volume**—from all corners of the letters and numbers—along the transferred **horizontal line** through the vertical lines that were drawn upward from all the letters and numbers of the rotated ground plan. Mark these corners where the vertical and horizontal lines of the letters and numbers meet each other; in this way, every corner of the **volume** <term>corpus</term> is found.

Then draw the side lines together from corner to corner; thus you will see how the **volume** <term>corpus</term> has turned to one side. Thus, the aforementioned volume is bent forward and to one side. Now take the forward-bent and side-turned **volume**, and bend it at the point of the spine in the **horizontal line** to the right side. After that, proceed from all corners of the letters...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Albrecht Dürer's "transfer" method. Dürer is explaining how to geometrically manipulate the blocks (corpora) that represent sections of the human body. He focuses on rotating, bending, and distorting these blocks using a mathematical tool he calls the "inverter" (Verkerer), which allows for complex poses and perspectival foreshortening.</meta>

...and the numbers of the body <term>corpus: a geometric block used as a scaffold for the body</term>—which is now drawn upright, arched forward, turned, and bent to one side—with vertical lines drawn downwards. Then, by transferring from all the corners of the previously turned and flattened **base** <term>grund: the ground plan or foundation of the geometric block</term>, draw horizontal lines through the vertical ones at the points where they intersect with their corresponding letters and numbers. At those same points, draw the sides of the **volume** <term>corpus</term> together in the base; in this way, you will find everything correctly positioned. 

Next, take the thicknesses and widths from the side and the front of the newly formed **sections** <term>schnidt: a cross-section or profile cut</term> of the volume, both at the bottom and the top, and draw the grid lines back through the **inverter** <term>verkerer: literally "inverter" or "transformer"—an instrument or geometric procedure used to rotate or distort shapes in perspective</term>. Then draw the lines of the actual shape through the grid, just as they should wrap around the section, within the upright and flattened base, as I have illustrated in the diagram that follows. And just as I am proceeding with this single **volume** <term>corpus</term>, so must one do with all such volumes throughout the entire figure. For it is necessary, if one later wishes to translate the figure—as it has been shifted—into a foreshortened and **distorted painting** <term>abgestolln gemel: an anamorphic or "stolen-away" image, where the perspective is intentionally warped</term>, to have this preparation from which the final work can be made.

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of his geometric "transfer" method. While the previous page introduced the concept of the "inverter" (Verkerer) and the general manipulation of body segments, this page provides a step-by-step practical application of these rules, focusing specifically on the head as the first example of how to tilt and rotate the body's geometric blocks.</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'S' features floral scrollwork and a small seated figure nestled inside, typical of the fine printing of the 16th century.</note> SO, as has been shown here in the front view within a primary **framing** <term>fierung: a square or structural frame used to define the body's proportions</term> and **volume** <term>corpus: a geometric block used as a scaffold for the body</term> of the body—specifically between the cut of the nipples and the waist—we can manipulate and rotate these geometric blocks of the body (which all stand straight upon one another along an upright line) by shifting them. From this, I will now proceed with the practical use of this method. Therefore, we will first bend and turn the geometric blocks of the figure one after another, however we wish, and incorporate all the previously described necessary elements. We may set the pose of the figure as we like; another person might choose to do it differently.

And as previously indicated, it should be noted that every geometric block, when it is shifted, must always be viewed differently in the **elevation** <term>auffreyssenn: a technical drawing showing the vertical rise or front/side view of an object</term> and in the raised or **flattened ground plan** <term>nidergedruckten grund: the base or footprint of the geometric block seen from above</term>. For first, a block is bent either forward or backward; after that, one may turn it to one side; and thirdly, one may tilt it to one side. Therefore, through the aforementioned ground plans, such things are properly sought out and correctly found. That I am repeating some of what was said before is necessary, so that I may proceed from what has been mentioned through all other similar things. I could have left it at the previously shown framing of the body, but I thought it would not be sufficiently understandable.

And therefore, I first take up the beginning of the figure: that is, the geometric block of the **head** <term>haubtz: 16th-century German for "head"</term>. And however I handle this, I will do the same through all the frames of the figure. Now I begin and mark the top four corners of the head’s volume with a. b. c. d. and the bottom four corners with 1. 2. 3. 4., so that a. 1., b. 2., c. 3., and d. 4. correspond to one another. First, I look at this from the side and shift the block of the head well forward from the upright line upon which the figure stands, and I first bend it forward. From this, I must seek its flattened ground plan so that I know how much the upper part overlaps the lower; and when this is done, I label it with an A.

After that, I take this first flattened ground plan and place its front part facing me, so that I am looking at it from the front, and I also turn the front a little toward its right side. Then, from all the corners marked with letters and numbers, I extend upright lines upward. I then place the side-view geometric block of the head (as it was bent downward) next to these raised lines. Then, from all the corners of the letters and numbers of the side-view volume A, I draw **transverse lines** <term>zwerch linien: horizontal or cross-lines used in a grid</term> across the upright ones. Where the letters and numbers then intersect with one another, the corners are found—the letters above and the numbers below. If you then draw the points together, you will see how the side-view, downward-bent volume (which has now been turned to one side) appears when viewed **from the front** <term>fürwertig: frontal view</term>, and how its flattened ground plan lies beneath it. This second part of the drawing shall be marked with a B.

Now you must also know how this part B appears in the elevation in both the high and low ground plans from the side; for it is necessary that one always knows how a thing appears when viewed from the side and from the front in the elevation to gain its form. Now the volume is bent forward and turned to one side, as you see in ground plan B, both raised and flattened; but this is now drawn from the front. Now we must also know what form this takes from the side in the elevation. Do it thus: take the previous ground plan (turned to one side and flattened) which you looked at before, and now turn it again so you view it equally and correctly from the side. Then, from all the corners of the letters and numbers of that flattened ground plan, draw lines upward from the transfer. Then place the previous frontal volume (as it was turned and bent) next to those raised lines, and draw from all the corners of that raised frontal volume’s letters and numbers with transverse lines from the transfer.

These show you how the <term>corpus</term> is to be seen from the side in the drafting; mark this with an E. Do the same also in the subsequent <term>corpora: the plural of corpus, the geometric blocks making up the figure</term>, and always bend the lower volumes less forward and turn them less to the right side, and bend them less to the right side than the upper one placed upon it, so that all these things are used one after another in a proper arrangement, through which it is seen how the body twists to the right side and inclines to the side and forward. But when you come to the fifth squared volume between the cut of the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note> and the flanks <note>original: "weychhen"</note>, let that stand upright, though turn it a little to the right side and make its necessary ground plans; for this one needs no more than three kinds of ground plans: the side-view first for the sake of the flattened ground plan, which one turns forward for the second time and raises up. Afterward, one makes the third ground plan, how it is turned

<summary>Dürer explains how to use a square frame (vierung) to accurately rotate tilted plan views and begins applying these geometric principles to the torso between the waist and navel.</summary>

### The squared block <term>corpus: a geometric volume or 3D prism</term> belongs to the height of the shoulder-muscles <term>schulterfleisch: the fleshy parts of the shoulder and upper back</term>.

<note>Dürer uses the term "shoulder-flesh" to describe the upper trapezius and deltoid region. He treats this complex muscular area as a simplified geometric block to help artists understand how it occupies space before they add organic detail.</note>

->**A** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **B** &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **C**<-

This mathematical approach allowed Renaissance artists to draw the human figure in difficult poses—such as leaning or reaching—while ensuring the proportions of the torso remained consistent with the rest of the body.</note>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal conclusion to Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). It transitions from the technical geometric demonstrations of the previous pages to a summary of how these principles should be applied in artistic practice, followed by a posthumous editorial note regarding Dürer’s death.</meta>

Whoever now undertakes and follows this my instruction <term>vnderweyssung: Dürer’s systematic method of teaching</term>, let them first form a correct proportion <note>original: "rechte proportion"</note> through the given measures, then place them in order, bend them, and lay them into the ground-plan <term>grund: the geometric foundation or horizontal plan</term>. Then, they should project them <term>auffziehen: to draw a vertical elevation or projection from a plan</term> and bring them into perspective <term>perspectiu: the mathematical representation of three-dimensional objects on a flat surface</term>, and thus further artistically draw them into the painting <term>gemel</term> or work. Such a person will soon realize what usefulness may result for him from it and will undoubtedly find much more than is indicated or reported here.

Notwithstanding that this instruction of mine may be considered difficult in some places, it is nevertheless true that what is difficult to understand will not be learned without effort and diligence. And with that, gracious Lord, I wish to make an end to my writing for this time; and if God grants it in His time, I shall write further on what belongs to painting <term>malen</term>, so that such art does not rest upon practice <note>original: "brauch", referring to rote habit or manual skill without theoretical understanding</note> alone, but may also in time be learned and understood from a correct and orderly foundation <term>grund</term>, to the praise of God and for the use and pleasure of all art-lovers.

---

Although the pious and artistic Albrecht Dürer <note>Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528), the leading figure of the German Renaissance.</note> wrote these four books, he only reviewed and corrected the first one again. Before he could reach the other three, the swiftness of death <note>Dürer died in April 1528, shortly before the full work was published; his friend Willibald Pirckheimer oversaw the final stages.</note> overtook him, so that he was unable to review them. It is quite possible that, had he been granted more time, he might have changed, increased, or diminished some things. Nevertheless, his good friends considered it more useful that the other three books also be published uncorrected <term>vncorrigiert</term> rather than having them remain suppressed. This was done regardless of whether there might be something in them to improve (though they do not believe this to be the case). 

If God had granted him a longer life, he would have brought to light many more wonderful, rare, and artistic things, especially useful for the art of painting landscapes <term>landschafft</term>, colors, and the like. He also particularly intended to describe and publish a longer and clearer treatise on perspective than he had done before. But God, who has ordained all things for the best, prevented such things not without cause; His name be praised and honored eternally.

<summary>This concluding text explains how to apply Dürer's proportional measurements to painting and perspective, followed by a posthumous note explaining that Dürer died before he could finish revising the final three books.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, perspective, posthumous publication, painting theory, landscape, artistic foundation</keywords>

--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Dürer’s own concluding remarks to the official legal protection of his work. It is an Imperial Privilege—an early form of copyright—granted by Emperor Charles V to Dürer’s widow, Agnes, to prevent unauthorized reprinting and intellectual theft of the "Four Books on Human Proportion."</meta>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'W' depicts an architectural and landscape scene within the letterform, characteristic of the high-quality typography used in official imperial documents of the early 16th century.</note> **W**e, Charles the Fifth, by the grace of God elected Roman Emperor, at all times Augmenter of the Empire, etc. In Germany, Spain, both Sicilies, Jerusalem, Hungary, Dalmatia, Croatia, etc. King; Archduke of Austria; Duke of Burgundy, etc. Count of Habsburg, Flanders, and Tyrol, etc. We acknowledge openly with this letter and make known to everyone: Whereas the honorable, our dear and devout Agnes, the surviving widow of the late Albrecht Dürer, has brought before our Imperial government in the Holy Empire how the aforementioned Albrecht, her husband, brought together several books on **human proportion** <term>menschlicher proporcion: the mathematical study of the relative sizes of the parts of the human body</term> from true art and perspective through his own diligence and invention, not without great effort, and had intended to bring these same books into print so that they might have been useful and beneficial to those who love such art;

And however, the same Dürer died recently and before the completion of such art-books; And now she, the widow, has had some of these books cut into forms <note>referring to the woodcut blocks used for printing</note> and intends to print them, some might undertake to bring his discovered art and something of such human proportion into their own hands through improper ways, and to bring them into print under their own title, as if they, and not Dürer, had discovered them.

To this end, she also had to fear (as often happens when something discovered with such diligence, effort, and labor is brought into print for the common good, that others immediately reprint <term>nachdrucken: unauthorized reprinting or piracy</term> it and thus seek and conduct their own profit in an unfair manner, to the disadvantage of the inventor) that if she were to have her late husband's discovered art printed, others would—to her significant disadvantage—immediately reprint the same if this were not prevented.

And she has therefore humbly called upon and petitioned us to appear with gracious provision and help. Following this, and so that the aforementioned art and invention of Dürer may not be withheld, and also so that the aforementioned widow may not be defrauded or brought to harm thereby, we give to the same widow this following privilege. We also hereby give it to her from Roman Imperial power, knowingly by virtue of this letter: so that no one, whoever they may be, shall bring into print, or allow to be issued, or sell, the aforementioned discovered art and books of the late Albrecht Dürer, or any excerpt from them, in his own name and as if he had made them, nor otherwise without the consent of the said widow.

Also, that no one shall reprint <note>original: "nachdrucke"</note> the aforementioned books, art, and proportion of his named widow—if she allows them to be brought into print or has brought them—for ten years immediately following the publication of the same print, nor sell them so reprinted.

And we command thereupon all and every one of our and the Empire's subjects, and especially all painters, book-printers, image-printers or art-printers, distributors, and sellers, and each one individually by Roman Imperial power, and under penalty of **ten marks of fine gold**—half to be paid inevitably into our Imperial chamber <term>Kayserlich kamer: the imperial treasury</term>, and the other half to the aforementioned widow—and also under the loss of such prints and reprints; we hereby earnestly will that no one shall claim for himself the aforementioned discovered art-books of the late Albrecht Dürer, nor any excerpt or addition to them, as if he had made them, nor otherwise bring them into print or let them be issued. Furthermore, that no one shall reprint or carry to the market for sale the aforementioned books and art, which the said widow has or will have issued in print, within the aforementioned time of ten years, in any way, as you—all and each of you individually—desire to avoid the aforementioned penalty and the confiscation of your prints and reprints; which prints and reprints the said widow, or those she...

<summary>This page contains the Imperial Privilege granted by Emperor Charles V to Agnes Dürer, protecting her late husband's work from intellectual property theft and unauthorized reprinting for ten years.</summary>
<keywords>Charles V, Agnes Dürer, Imperial Privilege, copyright, human proportion, printing rights, intellectual property, 16th-century law</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the conclusion of the Imperial Privilege granted by Emperor Charles V. # AN ELEGY BY WILLIBALD PIRCKHEIMER
<note>original: "ELEGIA BILIBALDI PIRCKEYM-"</note>
## ON THE DEATH OF ALBRECHT DÜRER YESTERDAY.
<note>original: "HERI IN OBITVM ALBERTI DVRERI." Dürer died on April 6, 1528. Pirckheimer, his lifelong friend, wrote this heartfelt poem to be included in Dürer’s posthumous masterpiece on human proportion.</note>

> You who for so many years were most closely joined to me,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Albrecht, and the greatest part of my soul,
> With whom I could share sweet conversations
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Safely, and pour my words into a faithful breast.
> Why, unhappy one, do you suddenly leave your mourning friend
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And hasten with a swift foot, never to return?
> I was not allowed to touch your dear head, nor your right hand,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor to say the final words of sad farewell.
> But you had scarcely surrendered your weary limbs to the bed,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When death, hastening, suddenly snatched you away.
> Alas, deceitful hopes and a mind ignorant of evils!
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How quickly everything suddenly falls in a swift slip.
> Favorable fortune had given everything to Dürer:
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Genius, beauty, and faith along with integrity.
> But again, death took everything away with a swift daring;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Yet that wicked power was not able to take away his praises.
> For Dürer’s virtue remains, and his renowned fame;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They shall shine as long as the bright stars in the sky.
> Go, honor, go, the great glory of our people,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And with Christ as your leader, seek the heavenly realms.
> There you will always rejoice in no empty honor,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Happily bearing worthy rewards for your merits.
> While we, fragile, wander in the shadow of death,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And wretchedly glide in an unstable boat,
> Yet when the preserving grace of Christ is present,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With Him as our leader, we shall enter upon the happy journey.
> Meanwhile, in our grief, we shall pour out tears for our friend,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Than which nothing can be sweeter for the afflicted.
> And prayers will be added, to appease the highest Thunderer
> <note>original: "tonantem." A classical poetic reference to God, borrowing the epithet of Jupiter (the Thunderer) common in Renaissance humanist Latin.</note>
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If pious vows have any power at all.
> And so that nothing is lacking for your tomb, we will scatter scents:
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Narcissus, violets, lilies, garlands, and roses.
> Meanwhile, happy one, rest in blessed sleep;
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For a good man sleeps in Christ—he does not die.

<summary>This page features a Latin elegy written by the humanist Willibald Pirckheimer mourning the sudden death of his dear friend Albrecht Dürer. The poem praises Dürer's character and artistic genius, ensuring his fame will endure as long as the stars.</summary>
<keywords>Willibald Pirckheimer, Albrecht Dürer, elegy, epitaph, funeral poem, Latin humanism, Renaissance friendship, grief</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues the memorial sequence for Albrecht Dürer, following the elegy on the preceding page. # Epitaph for the Same
<note>original: "Epitaphium eiusdem". "The same" refers to Albrecht Dürer, who was the subject of the elegy on the previous page.</note>

After Albrecht had adorned the world with painting,
And all things were filled with such graceful art.
“Now only heaven remains,” he said, “let its heights be painted,”
Soon, leaving the earth, he sought the bright stars.

### Another

Genius, honesty, sincerity, prudence, virtue,
Art, piety, and faith—here they lie buried.

### Another by the same

If life could be returned through tears, Albrecht,
This lowly earth would not cover your body.
But because the fates are moved neither by tears nor weeping,
Only by mourning do we pay these final rites.
<note>original: "inferias". This refers to the Roman custom of making offerings to the souls of the deceased.</note>

# Dedicated to the Most Blessed Memory
<note>original: "M. Beatiss S." Likely an abbreviation for *Memoriae Beatissimae Sacrum* (Sacred to the most blessed memory), a common humanist adaptation of ancient Roman funerary inscriptions.</note>

To the memory of Albrecht Dürer, a man of the highest character and the most perfected painter of his age. He was not only the first among the Germans to advance and illuminate the art of painting, bringing it under a stricter law <note>Dürer was a pioneer in applying mathematical theory, perspective, and geometry to German art, moving it away from medieval tradition toward the Renaissance.</note>, but he also began to entrust his knowledge to writing for the benefit of posterity. Because of this achievement—and especially for his refined character, his singular prudence, and his modesty—he was most dear to his fellow citizens of Nuremberg, and indeed to all those abroad. He was most highly esteemed by the Divine <note>The term "Divo" (Divine) was a title of honor used by humanists for deceased Emperors.</note> Maximilian and his grandson Charles, both Emperors, as well as by Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia. They supported him with a generous annual stipend and followed his career with the greatest favor. He passed away, leaving his friends in great longing, on the eighth day before the Ides of April <note>According to the Roman calendar, this date corresponds to April 6, 1528.</note>, in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

->Willibald Pirckheimer<-
->placed this for his most upright friend.<-
<note>original: "amico integerrimo. M. P." The "M. P." stands for *monumentum posuit* (placed this monument).</note>

<summary>A collection of Latin epitaphs and a formal funeral dedication written by Willibald Pirckheimer to honor the life, character, and royal patronage of Albrecht Dürer.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Willibald Pirckheimer, funerary inscription, epitaph, Nuremberg, Emperor Maximilian I, Emperor Charles V, King Ferdinand I, 1528, Renaissance art theory</keywords>
